The open dihypergraph dichotomy for generalized Baire spaces and its applications Thanks: We are grateful to Stevo Todorčević for comments and discussions. We further thank Raphaël Carroy and Ben Miller for answering a query regarding their work. Thanks: The first-listed author was partially supported by EPSRC grant number EP/V009001/1 and FWF grant number I4039. This project has received funding from the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant agreement No 794020 of the first-listed author. The second-listed author was partially supported by NKFIH grant number K129211. For the purpose of open access, the authors have applied a ‘Creative Commons Attribution’ (CC BY) public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.
Philipp Schlicht, Dorottya Sziráki

Abstract
. The open graph dichotomy for a subset X X of the Baire space ω ω {}^{\omega}\omega states that any open graph on X X either admits a coloring in countably many colors or contains a perfect complete subgraph. This strong version of the open graph axiom for X X was introduced by Feng and Todorčević to study definable sets of reals. We first show that its recent generalization to infinite dimensional directed hypergraphs by Carroy, Miller and Soukup holds for all subsets of the Baire space in Solovay’s model, extending a theorem of Feng in dimension 2 2 . The main theorem lifts this result to generalized Baire spaces κ κ {}^{\kappa}\kappa in two ways. (1) For any regular infinite cardinal κ \kappa , the following holds after a Lévy collapse of an inaccessible cardinal λ > κ \lambda>\kappa to κ + \kappa^{+} . Suppose that H H is a κ \kappa -dimensional box-open directed hypergraph on a subset of κ κ {}^{\kappa}\kappa such that H H is definable from a κ \kappa -sequence of ordinals. Then either H H admits a coloring in κ \kappa many colors or there exists a continuous homomorphism from a canonical large directed hypergraph to H H . (2) If λ \lambda is a Mahlo cardinal, then the previous result extends to all box-open directed hypergraphs on any subset of κ κ {}^{\kappa}\kappa that is definable from a κ \kappa -sequence of ordinals. This approach is applied to solve several problems about definable subsets of generalized Baire spaces. For instance, we obtain variants of the Hurewicz dichotomy that characterizes subsets of K σ K_{\sigma} sets, strong variants of the Kechris-Louveau-Woodin dichotomy that characterizes when two disjoint sets can be separated by an F σ F_{\sigma} set, the determinacy of Väänänen’s perfect set game for all subsets of κ κ {}^{\kappa}\kappa , an asymmetric version of the Baire property and an analogue of the Jayne-Rogers theorem that characterizes G δ G_{\delta} -measurable functions.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
(Primary) 03E15, (Secondary) 03E35, 03E55

1.
Introduction

It is natural to wonder whether Ramsey’s theorem for
n
-tuples of natural numbers
[
Ramsey1930
]
can be extended to the set of real numbers.
Sierpinski’s counterexample is a partition of pairs of reals into two pieces such that no uncountable homogeneous set exists
[
sierpinski1933probleme
]
.
Since his example is not constructive, one can ask if a version of Ramsey’s theorem holds for simple partitions.
Galvin
gave a partial answer to this
by proving that for any partition
of pairs of reals
into two open pieces there exists an uncountable (in fact, perfect) homogeneous set

[
galvin1968partition
]
.
Blass generalized this to partitions of
n
-tuples into finitely many Borel pieces
by weakening the conclusion

[
blass1981partition
]
:
instead of homogeneity, one requires that
all pairs in the set belong to at most
(n-1)!
pieces.

The next step was to generalize these results to spaces other than the reals. Abraham, Rubin and Shelah formulated a strengthening of Galvin’s theorem for countably based metric spaces of size
\aleph_{1}

[
AbrahamRubinShelah
]
.
Abstracting from many applications, Todorčević introduced the
open graph axiom

\mathsf{OGA}

[
TodorcevicPartitionProblems
]
.
1
1

1

It is also often called the
open coloring axiom

\mathsf{OCA}
.
Note that a graph
G
on a set
X
can be identified with a partition or coloring of pairs in
X
, for example by coloring edges in
G
red and edges in the complement of
G
blue.
Galvin’s theorem is then equivalent to the statement that for any clopen graph
G
on the reals, there exists either a perfect independent set or a perfect complete subgraph.

For a topological space
X
,

\mathsf{OGA}(X)
states that any open graph
G
on
X
either admits a coloring
2
2

2

Recall that a coloring of a graph
G
assigns different colors to adjacent vertices.

in countably many colors or else contains an uncountable complete subgraph.

\mathsf{OGA}
states that
\mathsf{OGA}(X)
holds
for all countably based metric spaces
X
.

\mathsf{OGA}
follows from the proper forcing axiom
\mathsf{PFA}

[
TodorcevicPartitionProblems
]

and has many applications
[
bekkali1991topics
,
TodorcevicFarah
,
VelickovicOCA
]
.
Feng and Todorčević studied a stronger version of
\mathsf{OGA}(X)
, the
open graph dichotomy

\mathsf{OGD}(X)
, that is useful for applications to definable sets of reals
[
FengOCA
,
TodorcevicFarah
]
.
Here the uncountable
complete subgraph
has to be perfect.

\mathsf{OGD}(X)
is provable for all analytic sets
X
of reals and it is in fact consistent that
\mathsf{OGD}(X)
holds for all definable sets
X

[
FengOCA
]
.
3
3

3

See
[
FengOCA
]
*Theorem 1.1 and Section 4, as well as
[
FengOCA
]
*pages 676-677 for an alternative approach for analytic sets due to Todorčević.

The previous statements only mention open graphs because they cannot hold for all closed graphs
[
TodorcevicFarah
]
.
4
4

4

See Example
5.3.3
in this paper.

The verbatim analogues of the open graph axiom and dichotomy fail in dimension
3
and higher:
an open dihypergraph
5
5

5

A dihypergraph, or directed hypergraph, of dimension
d
is a set of nonconstant functions with domain
d
.

on the Baire or Cantor space might neither admit a countable coloring nor have an uncountable complete subhypergraph
[
TodorcevicFarah
,
HeHigherDimOCA
]
.
6
6

6

See Examples
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
in this paper.

However, one can replace the latter
by a continuous homomorphic image of a canonical large dihypergraph.
Carroy, Miller and Soukup
proved a countably infinite dimensional version of
\mathsf{OGD}(X)
for
analytic sets
X

and extended this to
all sets of reals assuming the axiom of determinacy
{\sf AD}

[
CarroyMillerSoukup
]
.
7
7

7

It follows that this dichotomy is consistent with
{\sf ZFC}
for all box-open
\aleph_{0}
-dimensional dihypergraphs on the Baire space which are definable from a countable sequence of ordinals. To see this, take a model of
{\sf AD}+{\sf DC}+{V=L(\mathbb{R})}
and add a well-order of the reals by a
\sigma
-closed homogeneous forcing.

A similar idea
is implicit in work of
Avilés and Todorčević
[
avitod2010
]
.
8
8

8

We would like to thank Stevo Todorčević for pointing out that the idea for a higher dimensional version of
\mathsf{OGD}(X)
is implicit in the proof of
[
avitod2010
, Theorem 7]
.

The main motivation behind this dichotomy
is to provide new proofs and strong versions of several seemingly unrelated results in descriptive set theory
[
CarroyMillerSoukup
]
.
The first such result is the Hurewicz dichotomy that
characterizes the circumstances in which an analytic subset of a Polish space is contained in a
K_{\sigma}
subset

[
Hurewicz1928
,
SaintRaymond1975
,
Kechris1977
]
.
The Hurewicz dichotomy has also been studied by theoretical computer scientists
[
de2018generalization
]
.
A dichotomy due to Kechris, Louveau and Woodin describes when
an analytic subset of a Polish space can be separated from a disjoint subset by an
F_{\sigma}
set

[
KechrisLouveauWoodin1987
]
.
A celebrated theorem of Jayne and Rogers characterizes
G_{\delta}
-measurable functions on analytic sets
[
JayneRogers1982
]
.

We study the above problems in the more general context where
\omega
is replaced by a regular infinite cardinal
\kappa
with
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
.
The topological properties of the generalized Baire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
of functions on
\kappa
resemble that of the Baire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
. In fact, larger classes of spaces including
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
have been identified as analogues to
Polish spaces
at uncountable cardinals
[
coskey2013generalized
,
agostini2021generalized
]
.
The study of descriptive set theory for these spaces in the uncountable setting is motivated,
inter alia,
by connections
with model theory
[
vaan
,
VaananenGamesTrees
]

and Shelah’s classification theory in particular
[
MR3235820
,
moreno2022isomorphism
,
moreno2022unsuperstable
]
.
There is an ample amount of background literature
on generalized Baire spaces and their descriptive set theory,
beginning with the study of closed,
\kappa
-analytic and
\kappa
-coanalytic subsets
[
VaananenCantorBendixson
,
vaan
,
lucke2012definability
,
MR3430247
]
.
It is known that some properties of definable subsets of generalized Baire spaces are closely linked to statements in combinatorial set theory, for instance to the existence of Kurepa trees
[
lucke2020descriptive
,
lambiestej
]
.
The extension of several classical dichotomies to the uncountable setting paves the way for a structure theory of definable sets.
For instance, the Hurewicz dichotomy for
\kappa
-analytic sets is consistent

[
LuckeMottoRosSchlichtHurewicz
]
.
Assuming the existence of an inaccessible cardinal,
it is consistent for all definable sets
[
Hurewiczdef
]
,
and so is the
\kappa
-perfect set property for definable sets
[
SchlichtPSPgames
]
.
Moreover, the analogue of the open graph dichotomy for
\kappa
-analytic sets is consistent relative to an inaccessible cardinal
[
SzThesis
]
.
There is keen interest in analogues of the open graph axiom
\mathsf{OGA}

and similar principles at cardinals beyond
\omega_{1}

[
mohammadpour2021guessing
]
.

The aim here is to extend the aforementioned results on the open graph dichotomy and
its infinite dimensional version to uncountable cardinals and utilize such results to derive applications to the descriptive set theory of generalized Baire spaces.
We next introduce the relevant variants of these dichotomies.
The precise definitions of some of the following concepts will be given in Section
2
.
We assume that
\kappa
is a regular infinite cardinal with

\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa

throughout this paper, unless otherwise stated.
The
\kappa
-Baire space
is the set
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
of functions from
\kappa
to
\kappa
equipped
with the
bounded topology
(or
{<}\kappa
-box topology
),
i.e., the topology generated by the base
\{N_{s}:s\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\}
, where

N_{s}=\{x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa:s\subseteq x\}

for each
s\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
9
9

9

The
\omega
-Baire space is the Baire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
, since the bounded topology equals the product topology for
\kappa=\omega
.

The
\kappa
-Cantor space is
{}^{\kappa}2
with the subspace topology.
A
graph
is a symmetric irreflexive binary relation.
A graph
G
on a topological space
X
is
open
if it is an open subset of the product space
X\times X
.
A subset of
X
is
G
-independent
if it contains no edges, and
a
\kappa
-coloring
of
G
is a partition of
X
into at most
\kappa
many disjoint
G
-independent sets.

Todorčević’s
open graph axiom

[
TodorcevicPartitionProblems
]

can be generalized to the uncountable setting.
For any class
\mathcal{C}
, let

\mathsf{OGA}_{\kappa}({\mathcal{C}})
state that the following holds for all subsets

X\in\mathcal{C}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
:

\mathsf{OGA}_{\kappa}(X)
:
If
G
is an open graph on
X
, then
either
G
admits a
\kappa
-coloring or

G
has a complete subgraph of size
\kappa^{+}
.

The following generalization of the open graph dichotomy
[
FengOCA
]
is a stronger version of this axiom.

Definition 1.1
.

For any class
\mathcal{C}
,

\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}({\mathcal{C}})
states that the following holds for all subsets
X\in\mathcal{C}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
:

\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
:
If
G
is an open graph on
X
, then
either
G
admits a
\kappa
-coloring or

G
has a
\kappa
-perfect
complete subgraph.
10
10

10

I.e.,
G
has a complete subgraph whose domain is a
\kappa
-perfect subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
See Definitions
2.2.4
and
2.3.1
.

Note that

\kappa

a continuous homomorphism from the complete graph

\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}2}
to
G
.

11

See Corollary
5.2.5

Thus,

Section

The
Hurewicz dichotomy
for a subset
X
of a Polish space
states that
X
is contained in a
K_{\sigma}
set
if and only if it does not contain a closed homeomorphic copy
of the Baire space.
Hurewicz showed this for Polish subspaces
X

[
Hurewicz1928
]
.
32
32

32

It follows immediately from
[
Hurewicz1928
, Section 6]
by passing to a compactification that an analytic subset of a Polish space is
K_{\sigma}
if and only if does not contain a relatively closed homeomorphic copy of the Baire space, but this statement does not seem to be mentioned directly in the paper. We call this the
exact Hurewicz dichotomy
(see Section
).

Saint Raymond
[
SaintRaymond1975
]
and Kechris
[
Kechris1977
]
proved it for analytic sets.
Kechris extended
it to more complex sets under appropriate determinacy assumptions

[
Kechris1977
]
.
In the uncountable setting, the Hurewicz dichotomy has a topological version

\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(X)

similar to the classical one
[
LuckeMottoRosSchlichtHurewicz
]
as well as a combinatorial version

\mathsf{HD}_{\kappa}(X)

using superperfect trees
[
Hurewiczdef
]
.
The next result is proved in Theorems
and
using variants of dihypergraphs from
[
CarroyMillerSoukup
]
.

Theorem 1.6
.

For all subsets
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,

\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
implies the following analogues of the Hurewicz dichotomy:

(1)

\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(X)
:
Either

X
can be covered by
\kappa
many
\kappa
-compact
33
33

33

A topological space is
\kappa
-compact
(or
\kappa
-Lindelöf
) if every open cover has a subcover of size strictly less than
\kappa
.

subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, or

X
contains a closed homeomorphic copy of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(2)

\mathsf{HD}_{\kappa}(X)
:
Either

X
can be covered by the sets of branches of at most
\kappa
many
{<}\kappa
-splitting subtrees of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
, or

X
contains a
\kappa
-superperfect subset.
34
34

34

See Definition
.

In fact, we obtain a generalization
\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(X,Y)
of
\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(X)
for all supersets
Y
of
X
from
\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}(X)
.
35
35

35

See Definition
and Theorem
. If
Y
is in
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
, then
\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
suffices.

Here, the
K_{\kappa}
set in the first option needs to be a subset of
Y
,
while it suffices that the homeomorphic copy of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a relatively closed subset of
Y
in the second option.We will use this principle to answer two questions of Zdomskyy in Section
.

Kechris, Louveau and Woodin
proved a dichotomy that characterizes when
an analytic subset of a Polish space
can be separated from a disjoint subset by an
F_{\sigma}
set
[
KechrisLouveauWoodin1987
, Theorem 4]
.
The special case of complements describes when an analytic set is
F_{\sigma}
.
This was already shown by Hurewicz
[
Hurewicz1928
]
*Section 6.
The theorem of Kechris, Louveau and Woodin strengthens the Hurewicz dichotomy for analytic sets in the sense that one can derive the latter by a short argument using compactifications.
36
36

36

See
[
KechrisBook
, Theorem 21.22 & Corollary 21.23]
.
Note that this argument has a version for weakly compact cardinals by
Proposition
and Corollary
, but it does not generalize to an uncountable regular cardinal that is not weakly compact.

Let
\mathbb{R}_{\kappa}
denote the set of elements of
{}^{\kappa}2
that take nonzero values
unboundedly often and
\mathbb{Q}_{\kappa}
the set of those that do not.
The proof of the next theorem is based on ideas from
[
CarroyMillerSoukup
]
.

It follows from the previous theorem

\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}({}^{\kappa}\kappa)
has further applications to
Väänänen’s perfect set game
that is described next.
Any set of reals can be decomposed as the disjoint union of a crowded set and a countable scattered set by Cantor-Bendixson analysis.
44
44

44

Recall that a set of reals

X
is

if it has no isolated points,

if it is closed and crowded, and
scattered
if each nonempty subspace contains an isolated point.

Väänänen showed that an analogue of this statement for all closed subsets of

is consistent

45

45

Väänänen used a measurable cardinal

In fact, the analogue for closed sets is equivalent to the
\kappa

for closed sets
[

]
.

but not provable

]
.
To this end, he generalized the notions of scattered and crowded sets
to subsets

via a game

of length

.
46
46

46

See Definition
.
Here,

of the game, where player

as their first move.

We extend Väänänen’s result to all subsets of

in the next theorem, which follows from

While there is a verbatim analogue of the Baire property at uncountable cardinals, it is not as useful as in the countable setting since even simple sets such as the club filter do not satisfy it
[
MR1880900
]
.
50
50

See the beginning of Subsection

\kappa

-Baire space.

The
asymmetric
\kappa
-Baire property

\mathsf{ABP}_{\kappa}(X)
introduced in
[

]
is a more general principle
that is equivalent to the determinacy of the Banach-Mazur game of length
\kappa
for
X
.
In the countable setting,
\mathsf{ABP}_{\omega}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega})
51
51

51

I.e.,
\mathsf{ABP}_{\omega}(X)
for all subsets
X\in\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
of
{}^{\omega}\omega
.

is thus equivalent to the Baire property for sets in
\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
.

The next result is proved in Theorem
.

Theorem 1.11
.

For all subsets
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,

\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
implies the asymmetric
\kappa
-Baire property
\mathsf{ABP}_{\kappa}(X)
.

A new consequence in the countable setting is that

\mathsf{ODD}^{\omega}_{\omega}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega},\mathsf{D}_{\omega})
implies the Baire property for subsets of the Baire space in
\mathsf{D}_{\omega}
.

A celebrated result of Jayne and Rogers characterizes
{\bf\Delta}^{0}_{2}
-measurable functions on analytic sets
as those that are
\sigma
-continuous with closed pieces
[
JayneRogers1982
, Theorem 5]
.
52
52

52

A function between countably based metric spaces is
{\bf\Delta}^{0}_{2}
-measurable if and only if it is
{\bf\Pi}^{0}_{2}
-measurable.
It is
\sigma
-continuous with closed pieces
if it can be obtained as a countable union of continuous functions on relatively closed subsets of its domain.

A simpler proof of this theorem was subsequently discovered by Motto Ros and Semmes
[
ros2010new
,
kacenamottorossemmes
]
.
Possible generalizations thereof are a subject of intense study
[
ros2013structure
,
gregoriades2021turing
]
.
Recently, Carroy, Miller and Soukup found a new proof that derives the theorem from the open dihypergraph dihotomy
[
CarroyMillerSoukup
]
.
Their proof allows us to generalize the Jayne-Rogers theorem to the uncountable setting.
The next result is proved in Corollary
.

Theorem 1.12
.

\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
implies the following analogue of the Jayne-Rogers theorem for all subsets
X\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
:

\mathsf{JR}_{\kappa}(X)
: Any function
f:X\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa

is
\mathbf{\Delta}^{0}_{2}(\kappa)
-measurable if and only if it is a union of
\kappa
many continuous functions on relatively closed subsets of
X
.

At the end of Section
,
we prove some implications and separations
between applications of the open dihypergraph dichotomy such as
the Kechris-Louveau-Woodin dichotomy, its generalization to arbitrary sets,
the
\kappa
-perfect set property, versions of the Hurewicz dichotomy
and the analogue of the Jayne-Rogers theorem.
These rely on the comparison relation between dihypergraphs introduced in Subsection
3.4
.
For example, the next results follow as special cases from
Remark
,
Proposition
,
and
Corollaries
and
.

Theorem 1.13
.

(1)

\mathsf{KLW}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})\Longrightarrow\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})\land\mathsf{PSP}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
.
53
53

53

\mathsf{KLW}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
states that
\mathsf{KLW}_{\kappa}(X,Y)
holds for all subsets

of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
,

and

are defined similarly; see Definitions
2.3.1
,
and
.
{}^{\text{,}}
54
54

54

Note that if
\kappa
is not weakly compact, then

\mathsf{PSP}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})\Longrightarrow\mathsf{THD}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathcal{P}({}^{\kappa}\kappa))

by
[

[

If

(2)

if

The above versions of the Hurewicz dichotomy,
the asymmetric
\kappa
-Baire property and the Jayne-Rogers theorem for definable subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

are consistent relative to an inaccessible cardinal by

.
The first two statements provide new proofs of the main results of
[

,
Hurewiczdef
]
and answer two questions of Zdomskyy
[
zdomskyy
]
.
55
55

55

See
[
zdomskyy
, pages 6-7]
.

The variant
\mathsf{KLW}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}):=\mathsf{KLW}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathcal{P}({}^{\kappa}\kappa))
of the Kechris-Louveau-Woodin dichotomy,
its extension
\mathsf{KLW}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

relative to box-open
d
-dihypergraphs for
2\leq d\leq\kappa
,
the determinacy of Väänänen’s perfect set game and
in fact
\mathsf{CB_{\kappa}^{2}}(X)

for arbitrary subsets
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

are consistent relative to a Mahlo cardinal.
The former answers a question of Lücke, Motto Ros and the first-listed author and solves a recent problem of Bergfalk, Chodounský, Guzmán and Hrušák.
The latter extends a result of Väänänen
[
VaananenCantorBendixson
]
from closed sets to arbitrary subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
Moreover, it lowers the upper bound for the consistency strength of a dichotomy studied in
[
SzVaananen
]

from a measurable to a Mahlo cardinal.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
,
\delta
denote ordinals and
\kappa
,
\lambda
,
\mu
,
\nu
cardinals.

\kappa
always denotes an infinite cardinal with
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
, unless otherwise stated.

{\mathrm{Lim}}
and
\mathrm{Succ}
denote the classes of limit and successor ordinals, respectively.
If
\alpha=\beta+1
, then
\alpha-1
denotes
\beta
.
For any set
d
,
a
d
-sequence
is a function
f:d\to V
.
We write
d
-sequences as
\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in d\rangle
.

\langle c\rangle^{d}
denotes the constant

d
-sequence with value
c
.
We write
\overline{x}^{\pi}:=\langle x_{\pi(i)}:i\in d\rangle
for any
D
-sequence
\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in D\rangle
and
\pi\colon d\rightarrow D
, where
D
is any set.
A subsequence of
\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in\alpha\rangle
is
\overline{x}^{\pi}=\langle x_{\pi(i)}:i\in\beta\rangle
for some
\beta\leq\alpha
and some strictly increasing map
\pi:\beta\to\alpha
.
For any set
d
,

\alpha<\kappa
and any sequence

\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in d\rangle\in{}^{d}{({}^{\kappa}\kappa)}
,
let
\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\alpha}:=\langle x_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha:i\in d\rangle
.

s{}^{\frown}t
denotes the concatenation of two sequences
s
and
t
,
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigoplus}{\bigoplus}{\bigoplus}{\bigoplus}}_{\alpha<\beta}s_{\alpha}
denotes the concatenation of the sequences

s_{\alpha}
for
\alpha<\beta
.
Given a function
f:X\to Y
, write
f(U)
for the pointwise image of a subset
U
of
X
under
f
, and
write
f^{-1}(W)
for the preimage of a subset
W
of
Y
.
For any set
d
, let
f^{d}:{}^{d}X\to{}^{d}Y
denote the function defined by letting

f^{d}(\langle x_{i}:i\in d\rangle):=\langle f(x_{i}):i\in d\rangle

for all
\langle x_{i}:i\in d\rangle\in{}^{d}X
.

\operatorname{id}_{X}
denotes the identity function on
X
.

A class
X
is
definable from a set
y
if
X=\{x:\varphi(x,y)\}
for some first order formula
\varphi(v_{0},v_{1})
with two free variables.
Let
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
denote the class of those sets which are definable from a
\kappa
-sequence of ordinals.
By a
definable
set, we always mean an element of
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
when
\kappa
is clear from the context.
We use the following notation for definable subsets of the
\kappa
-Baire space:
If
\varphi(v_{0},v_{1})
is a first order formula with two free variables and
y
is a set, write

X^{\kappa}_{\varphi,y}:=\{x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa:\varphi(x,y)\}
.
We further write
X_{\varphi,y}
for
X^{\kappa}_{\varphi,y}
if
\kappa
is clear from the context.

Basic open subsets of
{}^{\kappa}d
are denoted

N_{t}:=\{x\in{}^{\kappa}d:t\subseteq x\}

for any discrete topological space
d

and any
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
.
Ordinals are always equipped with the discrete topology.
Suppose that
X
is a topological space.
The closure of a subset
Y
of
X
is denoted by
\overline{Y}
.

Y^{\prime}
denotes the set of
limit points
of
Y
,
i.e., the set of those
x\in X

with
x\in\overline{Y{\setminus}\{x\}}
.
The
\kappa
-Borel
subsets of
X
are defined by closing the set of basic open sets under unions of length
\kappa
and complements.
The first level of the
\kappa
-Borel hierarchy is the class
{\bf\Sigma}^{0}_{1}(\kappa)
of open sets.
For any
\gamma
with
1<\gamma<\kappa^{+}
,
{\bf\Sigma}^{0}_{\gamma}(\kappa)
sets are of the form
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}A_{\alpha}
, where each
A_{\alpha}
is in
{\bf\Pi}^{0}_{\beta}(\kappa)
for some
\beta<\gamma
.
For any
\gamma
with
0<\gamma<\kappa^{+}
,
{\bf\Pi}^{0}_{\gamma}(\kappa)
sets are complements of
{\bf\Sigma}^{0}_{\gamma}(\kappa)
sets, and
a set is
{\bf\Delta}^{0}_{\gamma}(\kappa)
if it is both
{\bf\Sigma}^{0}_{\gamma}(\kappa)
and
{\bf\Pi}^{0}_{\gamma}(\kappa)
.
The
\kappa
-analytic
or
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(\kappa)
subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

are subsets of the form
f(Y)
, where
f:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is continuous and
Y
is a closed subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
A
\mathbf{\Pi}^{1}_{1}(\kappa)
set is a complement of a
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(\kappa)
set.
A function
f:X\to Y
between topological spaces is called
\mathcal{C}
-measurable

with respect to a collection
\mathcal{C}
of subsets of
X

if
f^{-1}(U)\in\mathcal{C}
for every open subset
U
of
Y
.

A topological space is
\kappa
-compact
(or
\kappa
-Lindelöf
) if every open cover has a subcover of size strictly less than
\kappa
.
It is
K_{\kappa}
if it is a union of
\kappa
many
\kappa
-compact subspaces.
A subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is
\kappa
-precompact
if its closure is
\kappa
-compact.
It is
bounded
by

b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa

if
x(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
for all
x\in X
and
\alpha<\kappa
.
Write
x\leq^{*}y
if there is some
\alpha<\kappa
such that
x(\beta)<y(\beta)
for all
\alpha<\beta<\kappa
.
A subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is
eventually bounded
by
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
if
x\leq^{*}b
for all
x\in X
.
An uncountable cardinal
\kappa
is weakly compact if and only if

{}^{\kappa}2
is
\kappa
-compact if and only if
every closed and bounded subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is
\kappa
-compact.
56
56

56

See
[
hung1973spaces
, Theorem 1]
and

[
LuckeMottoRosSchlichtHurewicz
]
*Lemma 2.6. Conversely, it is easy to see that
\kappa
-compact subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
are always closed and bounded for all infinite cardinals
\kappa
with
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
.

2.2.
Dihypergraphs

In this subsection,
X
denotes a set and
d
a set of size at least
2
.

{\Delta}^{d}_{X}
denotes the
diagonal
, i.e., the set of constant sequences

\langle c\rangle^{d}
with
c\in X
.
Let
\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(d)
denote the set of all permutations of
d
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

(a)

A
d
-dihypergraph
57
57

57

This is short for
d
-dimensional directed hypergraph
.
on
X

is a subset
H
of
{}^{d}X\,{\setminus}\,{\Delta}^{d}_{X}
.
Elements of
H
are called
hyperedges
of
H
.

(b)

A
d
-hypergraph
on
X
is a
d
-dihypergraph
H
on
X
that is closed under permutation of hyperedges, i.e.,
\overline{x}^{\pi}\in H
for all
\overline{x}\in H
and all
\pi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(d)
.

A
digraph
on
X
is then a
2
-dihypergraph on
X
, while a
graph
on
X
is a
2
-hypergraph on
X
, i.e., a symmetric irreflexive binary relation on
X
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

(a)

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}:={}^{d}X-{\Delta}^{d}_{X}

is the
complete
d
-hypergraph on
X
.

(b)

\mathbb{K}_{X}:=\mathbb{K}^{2}_{X}
is the
complete graph
on
X
.

(c)

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{X}
denotes the
d
-dihypergraph consisting of all
injective elements of
{}^{d}X
.

Note that
\mathbb{I}^{2}_{X}=\mathbb{K}_{X}
, but
\mathbb{I}^{d}_{X}\subsetneq\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}
if
d\geq 3
and
|X|\geq 2
.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
H
and
I
be
d
-dihypergraphs on
X
and
Y
, respectively. Let
Z
be a subset of
X
.

(a)

The
restriction
of
H
to
Z
is
H{\upharpoonright}Z:=H\cap{}^{d}Z
.

(b)

Z
is
H
-independent
if
H{\upharpoonright}Z=\emptyset
.

(c)

Z
is an
H
-clique
if
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{Z}\subseteq H
.

(d)

Let
\lambda
be a cardinal. A function
c:X\to\lambda

is a
\lambda
-coloring
of
H

if
c^{-1}(\{\alpha\})
is
H
-independent for all
\alpha<\lambda
.

(e)

A
homomorphism
from
H
to
I
is a function
f:X\to Y

such that
f^{d}(H)\subseteq I
.

Given a family of topological spaces
\langle X_{i}:i\in d\rangle
,
the
box-topology

on
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}X_{i}
is the topology generated by sets of the form

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}U_{i}
, where
U_{i}
is an arbitrary open subset of
X_{i}
for each
i\in d
.
If
X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, then
we always work with the box topology on
{}^{d}X
unless otherwise stated.

In the following, let
H
be a
d
-dihypergraph on a topological space
X
.
We call
H

box-open
on
X
if it is open as a subset of

{}^{d}X
with the box-topology.
For finite
d
, we will simply say that
H
is
open
on
X
.

Definition 2.2.4
.

(a)

The
domain

\operatorname{dom}_{H}
of
H
is the set of all
x\in X
such that
x
is an element of some hyperedge of
H
.
58
58

58

More precisely,
\operatorname{dom}_{H}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{i\in d}\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(H)
, where
\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{i}
denotes projection onto the
i^{\textrm{th}}
coordinate.

(b)

H
is
relatively box-open
if it is a box-open dihypergraph on its domain
\operatorname{dom}(H)
.

Note that
H
is box-open on
X
if and only if
H
is relatively box-open and
\operatorname{dom}_{H}
is a relatively open subset of
X
.

2.3.
Trees and order preserving maps

Let
d
and
D
denote discrete topological spaces of size at least
2
throughout this subsection.
Suppose
s,t\in{}^{\leq\kappa}d
and
A\subseteq{}^{\leq\kappa}d
.
Let
\operatorname{lh}(t):=\operatorname{dom}(t)
and
\operatorname{lh}(A):=\sup\{\operatorname{lh}(u):u\in A\}
.
Let
{{t}{\downarrow}}:=\{s\in{}^{<\kappa}d:s\subsetneq t\}

and
{{t}{\uparrow}}:=\{u\in{}^{<\kappa}d:t\subsetneq u\}
.

s
and
t
are called
compatible
,
denoted
s\parallel t
, if
s\subseteq t
or
t\subseteq s
.
59
59

59

For general posets
\mathbb{P}
, this is called
comparable
. Conditions
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
are called
compatible
if there exists a common extension
r\leq p,q
.

s
and
t
are called
incompatible
, denoted
s\perp t
, if they are not compatible.
Let
s\wedge t
denote the maximal
r\in{}^{\leq\kappa}d
with
r\subseteq s
and
r\subseteq t
.
Note that
s\wedge t
is the node where
s
and
t
split if
s\perp t
, and
s\wedge t=s
if
s\subseteq t
.
For incompatible
s
and
t
, let
\Delta(s,t):=\min\{\alpha<\kappa:s(\alpha)\neq t(\alpha)\}
.
Then
\Delta(s,t)=\operatorname{lh}(s\wedge t)
and
s\wedge t=s{\upharpoonright}\Delta(s,t)=t{\upharpoonright}\Delta(s,t)
.

A
subtree
of
{}^{<\kappa}d
is a subset
T
that is closed under forming initial segments (i.e., it is downwards closed).

[T]:=\{x\in{}^{\kappa}d:\>{\forall\alpha<\kappa}\ \ {x{{\upharpoonright}}\alpha\in T}\}
denotes its set of
branches
.
Note that it is a closed subset of
{}^{\kappa}d
.
The
height
of
T
is defined as
0pt(T):=\sup\{\operatorname{lh}(t)+1:t\in T\}
.
Thus,
0pt(T)=\operatorname{lh}(T)
if
0pt(T)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}\cup\{0\}
and
0pt(T)=\operatorname{lh}(T)+1
if
0pt(T)\in\mathrm{Succ}
.
The
stem
of
T
is
the unique maximal node
s\in T
with
s\parallel t
for all
t\in T
.

If
A
is a subset of
{}^{\leq\kappa}d
, let

T(A):=\{t\in{}^{<\kappa}d:\>{\exists a\in A}\ \ {t\subseteq a}\}

denote the tree of initial segments of its elements.
Note that if
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}d
,
then

[T(X)]
is the closure of
X
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Suppose
T
is a subtree of
{}^{<\kappa}d
.

(a)

T
is
pruned
if every node
t\in T
extends to a branch
x\in[T]
.

(b)

A node
t
in
T
is
splitting
if it has at least two direct successors in
T
.

(c)

T
is
cofinally splitting
if for each
t\in T
, there exists a splitting node
u
in
T
with
t\subseteq u
.

(d)

T
is
{<}\kappa
-closed
if for any strictly increasing sequence
\overline{t}=\langle t_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
in
T
with
\alpha<\kappa
, there exists an upper bound
t\in T
for
\overline{t}
.

(e)

T
is
\kappa
-perfect
if it is cofinally splitting and
{<}\kappa
-closed.

(f)

A closed subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}d
is
\kappa
-perfect
if
T(X)
is
\kappa
-perfect.

The

\kappa
-perfect set property

\mathsf{PSP}_{\kappa}(X)

holds for a subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}d
if either
|X|\leq\kappa
or
X
contains a
\kappa
-perfect subset.

\mathsf{PSP}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
states that

\mathsf{PSP}_{\kappa}(X)

holds for all subsets
X\in\mathcal{C}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are posets.
A partial function
\iota:\mathbb{P}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{Q}

is called
strict order preserving
if
p<q
implies
\iota(p)<\iota(q)
for all

p,q\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
,
and

\iota
is called
strict order reversing
if
p<q
implies
\iota(p)>\iota(q)
for all

p,q\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
.

Definition 2.3.2
.

Let
\iota
be a partial function from
{}^{<\kappa}d
to
{}^{<\kappa}D
.

(a)

\iota
is
\perp
-preserving
if
s\perp t
implies
\iota(s)\perp\iota(t)
for all
s,t\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
.

(b)

Suppose that
\iota
is strict order preserving.

\iota
is called
continuous

if
\iota(t)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{s\subsetneq t}\iota(s)

for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
with
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}

and
{{t}{\downarrow}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
.

Definition 2.3.3
.

Let
\iota
be a strict order preserving partial function from
{}^{<\kappa}d
to
{}^{<\kappa}D
.

(a)

[\iota]
denotes the partial function from
{}^{\kappa}d
to
{}^{\kappa}D

where

\operatorname{dom}([\iota])
consists of those

x\in{}^{\kappa}d
with
x{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
for unboundedly many
\alpha<\kappa
, and
for all
x\in\operatorname{dom}([\iota])

[\iota](x)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha<\kappa,\\
x{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)\end{subarray}}\iota(x{\upharpoonright}\alpha).

(b)

Let

T(\iota):=T(\operatorname{ran}(\iota))

denote the tree of initial segments of elements of
\operatorname{ran}(\iota)
.

We will usually assume that
\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
is a pruned subtree of
{}^{<\kappa}d
,

in which case
\operatorname{dom}([\iota])=[\operatorname{dom}(\iota)]
and

\iota(x)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}\iota(x{\upharpoonright}\alpha)
for all
x\in\operatorname{dom}([\iota])
.
Moreover,

T(\iota)=T(\operatorname{ran}([\iota]))
in this case,
and therefore

[T(\iota)]
is the closure of
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
in
{}^{\kappa}D
.

Lemma 2.3.4
.

Let
\iota
be a strict order preserving partial function from
{}^{<\kappa}d
to
{}^{<\kappa}D
.

(1)

[\iota]
is a continuous function from

\operatorname{dom}([\iota])

to
{}^{\kappa}D
.

(2)

If
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving,
then

[\iota]
is a homeomorphism from
\operatorname{dom}([\iota])
to
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
.

Proof.

Since
[\iota]
is strict order preserving, we have

[\iota]\big(N_{t}\cap\operatorname{dom}([\iota])\big)\subseteq N_{\iota(t)}

for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
.

(1)
follows easily from this observation.
To show
(2)
,
suppose that
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving.
Then
N_{\iota(s)}\cap N_{\iota(t)}=\emptyset

for all
s,t\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
such that
s\perp t
.
Therefore
[\iota]
is injective and

[\iota]\big(N_{t}\cap\operatorname{dom}([\iota])\big)=N_{\iota(t)}\cap\operatorname{ran}([\iota])

for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
.
This implies that
[\iota]
is a homeomorphism from
\operatorname{dom}([\iota])
to
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
.
∎

Note that the converse of
(2)
fails for the map

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
defined by fixing a nonempty element
s
of
{}^{<\kappa}d
and
defining

\iota(\emptyset):=\emptyset
,

\iota(t):=s
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
with
\operatorname{lh}(t)=1

and

\iota(t)=s{}^{\frown}t
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
with
\operatorname{lh}(t)>1
.
The converse of
(2)
does hold for maps
\iota
such that
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\cap N_{\iota(t)}=[\iota](N_{t})
for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\iota)
.
In fact, if
\iota
has the latter property and

[\iota]
is injective, then
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving.

A
\wedge
-semilattice
is a
poset
\mathbb{P}
such that for any two
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
, there exists a greatest lower bound
p\wedge q
.
We regard

{}^{<\kappa}d
and
{}^{<\kappa}D
as a
\wedge
-semilattices with respect to
\subseteq
.
60
60

60

The greatest lower bound
s\wedge t
of
s,\,t
of
{}^{<\kappa}d
is defined at the beginning of this subsection.

Definition 2.3.5
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are
\wedge
-semilattices and
\iota:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
.

(i)

\iota
is a
\wedge
-homomorphism
if
\iota(p\wedge q)=\iota(p)\wedge\iota(q)
for all
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
.

(ii)

\iota
is a
strict
\wedge
-homomorphism
if it is a strict order preserving
\wedge
-homomorphism.

Note that any
\wedge
-homomorphism is order preserving,
but not necessarily strict order preserving.
Moreover, a strict order preserving map

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}D
is a
\wedge
-homomorphism if and only if

\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\wedge\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)=\iota(t)

for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
and all
i,j\in d
with
i\neq j
.
61
61

61

Note that if
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}D
is strict order preserving, then
\iota
is a strict
\wedge
-homomorphism with
\operatorname{lh}(\iota(t))=\operatorname{lh}(t)
for all
t\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
if and only if
[\iota]
is an isometry in the standard
\kappa
-ultrametric.

This is a strong form of
\perp
-preservation.
62
62

62

\perp
refers to reverse inclusion on
{}^{\leq\kappa}d
throughout the paper.

2.4.
Forcing

A forcing
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}})
is a triple such that
\leq_{\mathbb{P}}
is a pre-order (i.e., a reflexive transitive relation) on
P
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}}
is a largest element of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\leq_{\mathbb{P}}
. We confuse
\mathbb{P}
with its domain
P
, and we usually write
\leq
,
\perp
,
\mathbbm{1}

and
\Vdash
instead of
\leq_{\mathbb{P}}
,
\perp_{\mathbb{P}}
,
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}}
and
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}
, respectively.
For all
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
, we let
p\parallel q
(or sometimes
p\parallel_{\mathbb{P}}q
) denote the statement that
p
and
q
are compatible, i.e., that
p\not\perp q
.
Let
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{P})
denote the Boolean completion of
\mathbb{P}
.
63
63

63

Note that
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{P})
is unique up to isomorphism.

If
\mathbb{P}
is separative, we assume that
\mathbb{P}
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{P})
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

(a)

An
atom
in a forcing
\mathbb{P}
is a condition
p\in\mathbb{P}
with no incompatible extensions. A forcing
\mathbb{P}
is
non-atomic
if it has no atoms.

(b)

A forcing
\mathbb{P}
is
homogeneous
if for all
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
, there is an automorphism
\pi:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{P}
such that
\pi(p)
and
q
are compatible.

Definition 2.4.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
are forcings.

(a)

A
dense embedding

\iota:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a homomorphism with respect to
\leq,\,\perp
and
\mathbbm{1}

such that
\iota(\mathbb{P})
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}
.

(b)

Two forcings
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are

equivalent

(
\mathbb{P}\simeq\mathbb{Q}
) if there exist
dense embeddings

\iota:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\nu:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{R}
into some forcing
\mathbb{R}
.

(c)

Let
\iota:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
be a dense embedding. Define a
\mathbb{P}
-name

\sigma^{\iota}
for each
\mathbb{Q}
-name
\sigma
by recursion on the rank as

\sigma^{\iota}:=\left\{(\tau^{\iota},p)\>:\>p\in\mathbb{P},\ \,\exists q\in\mathbb{Q}\ \big(\iota(p)\leq q\wedge(\sigma,q)\in\tau\big)\right\}.

Recall the following standard facts.
64
64

64

See e.g.
[
KunenBook2013
]
.

Two forcings
are equivalent if and only if they have isomorphic Boolean completions.
Suppose
\iota:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a dense embedding between forcings
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.
Given a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
G
over
V
,
the upwards closure
H
of
\iota(G)
in
\mathbb{Q}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter over
V
, and
G=\iota^{-1}(H)
.
Moreover,

(\sigma^{\iota})^{G}=\sigma^{H}
for each
\mathbb{Q}
-name
\sigma
.
Conversely, suppose
H
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter over
V
. Then
G=\iota^{-1}(H)
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
, and
H
is equal to the upwards closure of
G
in
\mathbb{Q}
. In both of the above cases, we have
V[G]=V[H]
.

The following definition of the forcing for adding Cohen subsets is non-standard but essential in several arguments below.

Definition 2.4.3
.

Suppose that
\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal.

(a)

\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
is defined as the forcing

\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1):={}^{<\kappa}\kappa=\{p:\alpha\to\kappa\,\mid\,\alpha<\kappa\},

ordered by reverse inclusion.

(b)

\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,\xi)
is defined as the
{<}\kappa
-support product
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<\xi}\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
for any ordinal
\xi
.

We use the following convention. For a given
y\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,
we sometimes confuse
y
with the set

{{y}{\downarrow}}=\{t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa:t\subsetneq y\}
.
For example, we say that
y
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic if and only if
{{y}{\downarrow}}
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic filter.
Moreover, if
y
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic and
\sigma
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name, let
\sigma^{y}:=\sigma^{{{y}{\downarrow}}}
.
Also let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<\xi}y_{i}
denote
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<\xi}{{y_{i}}{\downarrow}}
whenever
\langle y_{i}:i<\xi\rangle
is a sequence of elements of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
The next two lemmas contain
standard facts about adding Cohen subsets and collapse forcings.

Lemma 2.4.4
.

Suppose that
\kappa
is an uncountable cardinal

such that
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
.
If
\mathbb{P}
is a non-atomic
{<}\kappa
-closed forcing of size
\kappa
, then
\mathbb{P}
has a dense subset which is isomorphic to the dense subforcing

\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1):=\{p\in\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1):\>\operatorname{dom}(p)\in\mathrm{Succ}\}

of
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
In particular,
\mathbb{P}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.

We omit the proof of Lemma
2.4.4
, since is straightforward and well known.

Lemma 2.4.5

(
[
MR2387944
]
*Lemma 2.2)
.

Let
\kappa
be a regular cardinal.
Suppose that
\nu>\kappa
is a cardinal with
\nu^{<\kappa}=\nu
. Let
\mathbb{P}
be a separative
{<}\kappa
-closed forcing of size
\nu
which forces that
\nu
has size
\kappa
.
Then
\mathbb{P}
has a dense subset which is isomorphic to the dense subforcing

\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\kappa,\nu):=\{p\in\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,\nu):\>\operatorname{dom}(p)\in\mathrm{Succ}\}

of
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,\nu)
.
In particular,
\mathbb{P}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,\nu)
.

Lemma
2.4.5
can be proven
using an adaptation of the proof of

[
MR1940513
, Lemma 26.7]

which can be found in the proof of
[
MR2387944
, Lemma 2.2]
.

We will use the following notation
for subforcings of the Lévy collapse
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
.
Suppose that
\kappa<\lambda
are cardinals,
\alpha\leq\lambda
and
I\subseteq\lambda
is not an ordinal (to avoid a conflict with the notation for the standard collapse).
Then let
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}:=\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\alpha)

and

\mathbb{P}_{I}:=\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,I):=\{p\in\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda):\>\operatorname{dom}{p}\subseteq I\times\kappa\}.

The notation
\mathbb{P}_{I}=\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,I)
will be used for intervals
I
, for which we use the standard notation

\displaystyle(\alpha,\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\{\beta\in\mathrm{Ord}:\alpha<\beta<\gamma\}

\displaystyle[\alpha,\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\{\beta\in\mathrm{Ord}:\alpha\leq\beta<\gamma\}.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{\alpha}:=\mathbb{P}_{[\alpha,\lambda)}

for all
\alpha<\lambda
.
If
G
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic filter over
V
, write

G_{\alpha}:=G\cap\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
,
G_{I}:=G\cap\mathbb{P}_{I}

and
G^{\alpha}:=G\cap\mathbb{P}^{\alpha}
.

The next corollary follows easily from Lemma
2.4.5

using a straightforward analogue of the proof of

[
MR2387944
, Corollary 2.4]
.
65
65

65

See also
[
MR2387944
, Corollary 2.3]
.

[
MR2387944
, Corollary 2.4]

asserts that the conclusion of Corollary
2.4.6
holds when
\gamma=0
, under the weaker assumption that

\lambda>\kappa
is a cardinal such that
\mu^{<\kappa}<\lambda
for all cardinals
\mu<\lambda
.

Corollary 2.4.6
.

Let
\lambda>\kappa
be an inaccessible cardinal and
\gamma<\lambda
.
If
\mathbb{P}
is a
{<}\kappa
-closed forcing of size
{<}\lambda
,
then the forcings
\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
and

\mathbb{P}^{\gamma}=\mathbb{P}_{[\gamma,\lambda)}

are equivalent.

Definition 2.4.7
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{Q}
are forcings.

(a)

A
complete embedding

i:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a homomorphism with respect to
\leq,\,\perp
and
\mathbbm{1}
with the property that for every
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, there is a condition
p\in\mathbb{P}
(called a
reduction
of
q
to
\mathbb{P}
) such that for every
r\leq p
in
\mathbb{P}
,
i(r)
is compatible with
q
.

(b)

\mathbb{P}
is a
complete subforcing
of
\mathbb{Q}
if
\mathbb{P}
is a subforcing of
\mathbb{Q}
and the inclusion map
\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{P}}:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a complete embedding.

(c)

Suppose that
i:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a complete embedding and
G
is
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
V
.
The
quotient forcing
\mathbb{Q}/G
for
G
in
\mathbb{Q}
is defined as the subforcing

\mathbb{Q}/G:=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:\>\forall p\in G\ \,i(p)\parallel q\}

of
\mathbb{Q}
.
Moreover, we fix a
\mathbb{P}
-name
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{P}
for for the quotient forcing for
\dot{G}
in
\mathbb{P}
, where
\dot{G}
is the canonical
\mathbb{P}
-name for the
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter. We also refer to
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{P}
as (a name for) the quotient forcing for
\mathbb{P}
in
\mathbb{Q}
.

We recall the following standard facts.
66
66

66

See e.g.
[
KunenBook2013
]
and
Exercises (C7), (C8), (D4) and (D5) in

[
KunenBook1980
, Chapter VII]
.

Fact 2.4.8
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are forcings and
i:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
.

(1)

If
i
is a homomorphism with respect to
\leq,\,\perp
and
\mathbbm{1}
, then

i
is a complete embedding if and only if
for all maximal antichains
A
of
\mathbb{P}
,

i(A)
is a maximal antichain of
\mathbb{Q}
.
If
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are complete Boolean algebras, then
i
is a complete embedding (in the sense of Definition
2.4.7
) if and only if
i
is
an injective complete homomorphism of Boolean algebras
67
67

67

A homomorphism between Boolean algebras is called
complete
if it preserves arbitrary infima and suprema.

(2)

Any complete embedding
i:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
defines a complete embedding

j:\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
.
If
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are separative, we may assume that
i\subseteq j
.
In particular, if
\mathbb{P}
is a complete subforcing of
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}
is separative,
68
68

68

Note that complete subforcings of separative forcings are also separative.

we may assume that
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{P})\subseteq\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
.
Then
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{P})
is a complete Boolean subalgebra of
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
by
(1)
.

(3)

Suppose
i
is a complete embedding.
Let
G
be a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
.
If
D
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}
, then
D\cap\mathbb{Q}/G
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}/G
.
Therefore
all
\mathbb{Q}/G
-generic filters
H
over
V[G]
are also
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filters over
V
, and
G=i^{-1}(H)
for all such
H
.
Conversely,
if
H
is
\mathbb{Q}
-generic over
V
and
G=i^{-1}(H)
, then
G
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
and
H
is
\mathbb{Q}/G
-generic over
V[G]
.
Furthermore, under either of the above assumptions, we have

V[H]_{\mathbb{Q}}=V[G][H]_{\mathbb{Q}/G}
.

Definition 2.4.9
.

Given complete Boolean algebras
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
and a complete embedding
i:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
,
the
retraction
associated to
i
is the map

\pi_{i}:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}

defined by letting

\pi_{i}(q):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{P}}\{p\in\mathbb{P}:i(p)\geq q\}

for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.

The following lemma is standard, but we include a proof for the reader.

Lemma 2.4.10
.

Suppose
i:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a complete embedding between the complete Boolean algebras
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.

(1)

For all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\pi_{i}(q)
is the largest reduction of
q
to
\mathbb{P}
.

(2)

If
G
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
, then
q\in\mathbb{Q}/G
if and only if
\pi_{i}(q)\in G
.

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.
First, observe that
i(p)\perp q
if and only if
p\perp\pi_{i}(q)
holds for all
p\in\mathbb{P}
.
Indeed, given
p\in\mathbb{P}
, we have

i(p)\perp q

\Longleftrightarrow

i(p)\wedge q=\mymathbbold{0}

\Longleftrightarrow

\neg i(p)\geq q
.
By the definition of
\pi_{i}
and since
i
is a Boolean homomorphism, the last statement is equivalent to

\neg p\geq\pi_{i}(q)
,
and is therefore also equivalent to

p\wedge\pi_{i}(q)=\mymathbbold{0}
and to

p\perp\pi_{i}(q)
.

The previous observation easily implies that
\pi_{i}(q)
is a reduction of
q
to
\mathbb{P}
.
To see that
\pi_{i}(q)
is the largest reduction of
q
to
\mathbb{P}
, suppose that

p\in\mathbb{P}
and
p\not\leq\pi_{i}(q)
.
Let
r:=p\wedge\neg\pi_{i}(q)
.
Then
p\geq r\geq\mymathbbold{0}_{\mathbb{P}}
and
r\perp\pi_{i}(q)
, and therefore
i(r)\perp q
.
Thus,
r
witnesses that

p
is not a reduction of
q
to
\mathbb{P}
.
Moreover, if
G
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
then

q\in\mathbb{Q}/G

\Longleftrightarrow

p\perp\pi_{i}(q)
for all
p\in\mathbb{P}

\Longleftrightarrow

\pi_{i}(q)\in G
.
∎

As usual, by a name
\sigma
for an element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, we mean a name
\sigma
such that
\mathbbm{1}\Vdash\sigma\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
By a name
\sigma
for a
new
object, we mean a name
\sigma
such that
\mathbbm{1}\Vdash\sigma\notin\check{V}
.
In general, if
\varphi(v)
is any formula with one variable
v
, then by a name
\sigma
for an object with property
\varphi
, we mean a name
\sigma
such that
\mathbbm{1}\Vdash\varphi(\sigma)
.

We will use the following notation when working with quotient forcings induced by names.

Definition 2.4.11
.

Suppose
\mathbb{Q}
is a complete Boolean algebra and
\sigma
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for a subset of a set
x
.
Let
\mathbb{B}(\sigma):=\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)
denote the complete Boolean subalgebra of
\mathbb{Q}
that is completely generated by
the set of Boolean values
\{\llbracket{y\in\sigma}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{Q}}:y\in x\}
.

The following lemma is standard, but we include a proof for the reader.

Lemma 2.4.12
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{Q}
is a complete Boolean algebra and
\sigma
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for a subset of a set
x
.
If
H
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter over
V
, then

V\big[\sigma^{H}\big]=V\big[\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)\cap H\big]
.

Proof.

Let
A:=\{\llbracket{y\in\sigma}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{Q}}:y\in x\}
.
Then
V\big[\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)\cap H\big]=V\big[A\cap H\big]

by
[
MR1940513
, Lemma 15.40]
.
Moreover,

V\big[A\cap H\big]=V\big[\sigma^{H}\big]

since

y\in\sigma^{H}

\Longleftrightarrow

\llbracket{y\in\sigma}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{Q}}\in H

\Longleftrightarrow

\llbracket{y\in\sigma}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{Q}}\in A\cap H

for all
y\in x
.
∎

In the next definition and lemmas, suppose that
\mathbb{Q}\in V
is a forcing,
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\sigma\in V
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for an element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Definition 2.4.13
.

(a)

\sigma_{[q]}:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa:q\Vdash t\subseteq\sigma\}
.

(b)

\sigma_{(q)}:=\{(\alpha,\beta):q\Vdash(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma\}
.

(c)

The
tree
T^{\sigma,q}
of possible values for
\sigma
below
q
is defined as follows:

T^{\sigma,q}:=\{t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\>:\>\exists r\leq q\ \ t\subseteq\sigma_{[r]}\}.

For clarity, we at times write
\sigma_{[q,\mathbb{Q}]},\ \sigma_{(q,\mathbb{Q})}
and
T^{\sigma,q}_{\mathbb{Q}}
instead of
\sigma_{[q]},\ \sigma_{(q)}
and
T^{\sigma,q}
, respectively.

The next two lemmas list basic properties of
\sigma_{[q]}
and
T^{\sigma,q}
.
The first one follows immediately from the definitions.

Lemma 2.4.14
.

(1)

\sigma_{[q]}\subseteq\sigma_{(q)}
, and

\sigma_{[q]}=\sigma_{(q)}
if and only if
\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(q)})
is an ordinal.

(2)

\sigma_{[q]}{\upharpoonright}\alpha=\sigma_{(q)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha
for all ordinals
\alpha
with
\alpha\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(q)})
.

Lemma 2.4.15
.

(1)

If
q\not\Vdash_{\mathbb{Q}}\sigma\in\check{V}
,

then
\sigma_{[q]}\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
equals the stem of
T^{\sigma,q}
.

(2)

q\Vdash^{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}\sigma\in[T^{\sigma,q}].

(3)

If
S
is a subtree of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
with
q\Vdash^{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}\sigma\in[S]
, then
T^{\sigma,q}\subseteq S
.

(4)

If
M\supseteq V
is a transitive model of
{\sf ZFC}
with

({}^{<\kappa}\kappa)^{V}=({}^{<\kappa}\kappa)^{M}
, then

{(\sigma_{[q]})}^{V}={(\sigma_{[q]})}^{M}

and

{(T^{\sigma,q})}^{V}={(T^{\sigma,q})}^{M}.

Proof.

(1)
–
(3)
are immediate.

(4)
holds since the formula “
q\Vdash t\subseteq\sigma
” is absolute between
M
and
V
, as it can be defined by a recursion which uses only absolute concepts
[
KunenBook2013
, Theorem II.4.15]
.
∎

3.
Dihypergraphs and homomorphisms

We discuss basic facts about colorings, continuous homomorphisms and
the open dihypergraph dichotomy in Subsection
3.1
.
We characterize
H
-independence for box-open dihypergraphs
H
at the level of subtrees of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
in Section
3.2
.
We provide a characterization of
the existence of continuous homomorphisms from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H

via certain strict order preserving maps in Subsection
3.3
.
These facts will be used throughout the paper.
The relation
of
fullness
between dihypergraphs that is introduced in Subsection
3.4

compares the induced

\kappa
-ideals of
\kappa
-colorable sets and, as we show, allows us
to compare the existence of continuous homomorphisms
for relatively box-open dihypergraphs, too.
69
69

69

See Lemma
3.4.2
.

In particular,
equivalent relatively box-open dihypergraphs give rise to
the same instances of the open dihypergraph dichotomy.

3.1.
Colorings and homomorphisms

Throughout this subsection,
we assume that
d\geq 2
is an ordinal.
We first observe
that the two options in the definition of
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
are mutually exclusive.

Lemma 3.1.1
.

Let
H
be a
d
-dihypergraph on a topological space
X
.
Suppose that there is a homomorphism
f:{}^{\kappa}d\to X
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.

(1)

H
does not have a
\kappa
-coloring.

(2)

H{\upharpoonright}f(N_{t})
does not have a
\kappa
-coloring for any
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
.

Proof.

The proof of
(1)
is a straightforward analogue of the proof for the
\kappa=\omega
case
in
[
CarroyMillerSoukup
]
*Theorem 1.
We give the details for completeness.
Suppose
c^{\prime}:X\to\kappa
is a
\kappa
-coloring of
H
, and let
c:=c^{\prime}\circ f
. Since
f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
,
c
is a
\kappa
-coloring of
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
.
Construct by recursion a continuous increasing sequence

\langle t_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
such that

t_{\alpha}\in{}^{\alpha}d
and

c^{-1}(\{\alpha\})\cap N_{t_{\alpha+1}}=\emptyset

for each
\alpha<\kappa
.
Use the

\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
-independence of
c^{-1}(\{\alpha\})
at
stage
\alpha+1
of the construction.
Then

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}t_{\alpha}
is an element of
{}^{\kappa}d

which is not in
c^{-1}(\{\alpha\})
for any
\alpha<d
,
a contradiction.

(2)
follows from
(1)
, since
the map

f_{t}:{}^{\kappa}d\to f(N_{t});\;x\mapsto f(t{}^{\frown}x)

is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}f(N_{t})

for any given
t\in{}^{\kappa}d
.
∎

Note that

(1)
implies
|X|>\kappa
and
(2)
implies

\left|f(N_{t})\right|>\kappa

for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
.
We did not need to assume that
f
is continuous or that
H
is box-open.
Thus, if
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
holds and there exists an arbitrary homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
, then there also exists a continuous one.
This is analogous to the fact that for any subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
that satisfies the
\kappa
-perfect set property, the existence of an injective function from
{}^{\kappa}2
to
X
already implies the existence of a continuous injective function.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Suppose
d<\kappa
and
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(1)

If
U
is a box-open subset of
{}^{d}({}^{\kappa}\kappa)

then
U\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.

(2)

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, note that
U
is given by a sequence
x:\kappa\to{}^{d}({}^{<\kappa}\kappa)
since the base of the topology has size
|{}^{d}({}^{<\kappa}\kappa)|=\kappa
. Since
x
can be coded by an element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, we have
U\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.

(2)
follows from
(1)
.
∎

The next two lemmas give reformulations of
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
and
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
.
Recall that
H
is a
relatively box-open dihypergraph
if it is box-open on its domain
\operatorname{dom}_{H}
.

Lemma 3.1.3
.

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
is equivalent to the statement that
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
holds for all
relatively box-open
d
-dihypergraphs

H
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

with
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

This follows from the observation that a dihypergraph

H
is relatively box-open with
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}

if and only if

H=H^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}X
for some
subset
X\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and some
box-open
d
-dihypergraph
H^{\prime}
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
To see the direction from right to left, note that
\operatorname{dom}_{H^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}X}
is a relatively open subset of
X
,
and hence it is in
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
if
X
is in
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Suppose that
d\leq\kappa
. Then

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
is equivalent to the statement that
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
holds for all
relatively box-open
d
-dihypergraphs

H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Proof.

This follows from the equivalence of the following statements for any
d\leq\kappa
and any
d
-dihypergraph
H
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
:

(a)

H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
is relatively box-open.

(b)

H=H^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}X
for some
subset
X\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and some
box-open
d
-dihypergraph
H^{\prime}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

For
(a)

\Rightarrow

(b)
,
note that

X:=\operatorname{dom}_{H}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
and

H^{\prime}:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}N_{t_{\alpha}}:\,\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}(N_{t_{\alpha}}\cap X)\subseteq H\}\cap\mathbb{K}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}

is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

with
H=H^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}X
.
The converse is clear.
∎

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a continuous surjection between subsets
X
and
Y
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(1)

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(Y)
.

(2)

If
f
can be extended to a continuous function

g:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,
then
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(Y,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
.

Proof.

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
is equivalent to the statement that
\mathsf{ODD}^{I}_{\kappa}
holds for all box-open
d
-dihypergraphs
I
on
X
.
For a given box-open
d
-dihypergraph
H
on
Y
, let

H_{f}={{(f^{d})}^{-1}(H)}=\{\langle x_{\alpha}:\alpha<d\rangle\in{}^{d}X:\langle f(x_{\alpha}):\alpha<d\rangle\in H\}.

Since
{f^{d}}
is a continuous map between the spaces
{}^{d}X
and
{}^{d}Y
,
where both spaces are
equipped with the box-topology,

H_{f}
is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
X
.

Claim
.

\mathsf{ODD}^{H_{f}}_{\kappa}
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

Note that
f
is a continuous homomorphism from
H_{f}
to
H
.
Thus, if
h:{}^{\kappa}d\to X
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H_{f}
, then
h\circ f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
On the other hand, if
c:X\to\kappa
is a
\kappa
-coloring of
H_{f}
, then the map

c^{\prime}:Y\to\kappa
defined by letting

c^{\prime}(y):=\min\big\{\alpha:\>f^{-1}(\{y\})\cap{(c)}^{-1}(\{\alpha\})\neq\emptyset\big\}

for each
y\in Y

is a
\kappa
-coloring of
H
.
∎

(1)
follows immediately from the previous claim.
For
(2)
, suppose that
H^{\prime}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
Let
H:=H^{\prime}{{\upharpoonright}}Y
.
It suffices to show

\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
.
Let
H_{f}:={(f^{d})}^{-1}(H)
and
H^{\prime}_{g}={(g^{d})}^{-1}(H^{\prime})
.
Since
g
is continuous,

H^{\prime}_{g}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}

is a box-open

d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
Since we assume

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
, we have
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,H^{\prime}_{g})\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{ODD}^{H^{\prime}_{g}{{\upharpoonright}}X}_{\kappa}\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{ODD}^{H_{f}}_{\kappa}
.
Thus
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
by the previous claim.

∎

We next show that
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{c}_{\kappa}(X)

for all
c\subseteq d

with
|c|\geq 2
.
We will need the following lemma.
Let
\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{d,c}
denote
the projection of
d
-sequences onto
coordinates in
c
,
i.e.,
for any sequence

\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in d\rangle
,

\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{d,c}(\overline{x})=\overline{x}{\upharpoonright}c=\langle x_{i}:i\in c\rangle.

Recall that
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}
is the complete
d
-hypergraph on
X
and

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{X}
consists of
the injective elements of
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}
.

Lemma 3.1.6
.

Suppose that
c\subseteq d
and
|c|\geq 2
.
Let
H
be a

c
-dihypergraph
on a topological space
X
,
and let

I:=\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{d,c}^{-1}(H){{\upharpoonright}}X
.

(i)

I
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
X
.

(ii)

H
is box-open on
X
if and only if
I
is box-open on
X
.
In fact, if
c
is finite and
H
is open on
X
,
then
I
is product-open on
X
.
70
70

70

I.e.,
H
is an open subset of
{}^{d}X
in the product topology.

(iii)

If
c,X\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
, then
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
if and only if
I\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.

(iv)

Suppose that
Y\subseteq X
.

(a)

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{Y}\not\subseteq I
.

(b)

If
|Y|\geq|d|
, then

\mathbb{I}^{c}_{Y}\subseteq H
if and only if
\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\subseteq I
.

(v)

Suppose that
Y\subseteq X
.

(a)

Y
is
H
-independent if and only if
Y
is
I
-independent.

(b)

If
|Y|\geq|d|
, then

\mathbb{I}^{c}_{Y}\cap H=\emptyset
if and only if
\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\cap I=\emptyset
.

(vi)

A function
f:X\to\kappa
is a
\kappa
-coloring of
H
if and only if it is a
\kappa
-coloring of
I
.

(vii)

There exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
H
if and only if there exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
.

(viii)

\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}

\Longleftrightarrow

\mathsf{ODD}^{I}_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

(i)
–
(v)

are easy to show.

(vi)
follows from
(v)
.
To show
(vii)
,
suppose first that
g:{}^{\kappa}d\to X
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
.
Let
f:=g{\upharpoonright}{}^{\kappa}c
.
It is easy to see that
f
is continuous.
To see that
f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
H
,
suppose that
\overline{y}\in\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
.
Then there exists
\overline{x}\in{}\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
such that
\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{d,c}(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
.
Since
g
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
,
we have
g^{d}(\overline{x})\in I
.
Therefore

f^{c}(\overline{y})=g^{c}(\overline{y})=\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{d,c}\big(g^{d}(\overline{x})\big)\in H,

as required.
Conversely, suppose that
f:{}^{\kappa}c\to X
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
H
.
Fix a map
k:d\to c
with
k{\upharpoonright}c=\operatorname{id}_{c}
and define
g:{}^{\kappa}d\to X
by letting
g(x):=f(k\circ x)
.
It is easy to see that
g
is continuous.
To see that
g
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
,
suppose that
\overline{y}=\langle y_{i}:i\in d\rangle\in\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
.
Then
g^{d}(\overline{y})=\langle f(k\circ y_{i}):i\in d\rangle
and its projection equals
\langle f(k\circ y_{i}):i\in c\rangle
.
Suppose that
y_{i}
extends
t^{\smallfrown}\langle i\rangle
for all
i\in d
.
Then
k\circ y_{i}
extends
(k\circ t)^{\smallfrown}\langle i\rangle
for all
i\in c
and hence
\langle f(k\circ y_{i}):i\in c\rangle\in H
.
Thus
g^{d}(\overline{y})\in I
.

(viii)
follows from
(vi)
and
(vii)
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.7
.

Suppose that
c\subseteq d
,
|c|\geq 2

and
X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(1)

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{c}_{\kappa}(X)
.

(2)

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{c}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

if
c\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, suppose
H
is a box-open
c
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
It suffices to show
\mathsf{ODD}^{H{{\upharpoonright}}X}_{\kappa}
.

I=\operatorname{\mathbf{p}}_{d,c}^{-1}(H){{\upharpoonright}}{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
by
(i)
and
(ii)
in the previous lemma.
By
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
we have
\mathsf{ODD}^{I{{\upharpoonright}}X}_{\kappa}
and then obtain
\mathsf{ODD}^{H{{\upharpoonright}}X}_{\kappa}
from
(viii)
.
The proof of
(2)
is similar, but also uses
(iii)
.
∎

In particular,
\mathsf{ODD}^{\kappa}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{c}_{\kappa}(X)
for all
2\leq c<\kappa
and
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
by
Lemmas
3.1.2
and
3.1.7
.

3.2.
Independent trees

We assume throughout this subsection that
d
is a set
of size at least
2
.
We characterize
independence for box-open dihypergraphs
at the level of subtrees of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Suppose
X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
H
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
A subtree
T
of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

is an
(X,H)
-independent tree
if

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{t_{i}}\cap X)\not\subseteq H

for all sequences
\langle t_{i}:i\in d\rangle\in{}^{d}T
.
We omit
X
if
X={}^{\kappa}\kappa
, i.e.,
T
is called
H
-independent
if it is
({}^{\kappa}\kappa,H)
-independent.

We will most often work with
H
-independent trees.
Note that if
T
is an
(X,H)
-independent tree, then
T\subseteq T(X)
because otherwise, there exists some
t\in T
with
N_{t}\cap X=\emptyset
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Suppose
H
is a
d
-dihypergraph on a subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(1)

If
H
is box-open on
X
and
T
is an
(X,H)
-independent tree, then
[T]
is an
H
-independent set.

(2)

If
Y\subseteq X
is an
H
-independent set, then
T(Y)
is an
(X,H)
-independent tree.

Proof.

For
(1)
, suppose that
[T]
is not
H
-independent, and
let
\langle y_{i}:i\in d\rangle\in{}^{d}[T]\cap H
.
Since
H
is box-open on
X
,
there exist nodes
t_{i}\subsetneq y_{i}

such that

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{t_{i}}\cap X)\subseteq H
.
Then
t_{i}\in T
for all
i\in d
, so
T
is not
(X,H)
-independent.

For
(2)
, suppose that
Y\subseteq X
and
T(Y)
is not
(X,H)
-independent.
Take
\langle t_{i}:i\in d\rangle
in
{}^{d}T(Y)
such that

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{t_{i}}\cap X)\subseteq H
.
For all
i\in d
,
fix
y_{i}\in Y
with
t_{i}\subseteq y_{i}
.
Then
\langle y_{i}:i\in d\rangle
is a hyperedge of
H
.
∎

Corollary 3.2.3
.

Suppose
H
is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on a subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
If
Y\subseteq X
is
H
-independent, then
\overline{Y}

is
H
-independent.
71
71

71

Recall that
\overline{Y}
denotes the closure of
Y
in
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Proof.

The statement follows from the previous lemma, since
\overline{Y}=[T(Y)]
.
∎

3.3.
Order homomorphisms

We assume throughout this subsection that
d
is a discrete topological space of size at least
2
.
We first characterize the existence of a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
box-open dihypergraphs
using the following types of strict order preserving maps.
We use terminology from Subsection
2.3
.

Definition 3.3.1
.

Suppose
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
H
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
A strict order preserving map
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

is an
order homomorphism for
(X,H)

if

(a)

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq X
, and

(b)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\cap X)\subseteq H

for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
.

We omit
X
if it equals
\operatorname{dom}_{H}
, i.e.,

\iota
is an
order homomorphism for
H
if it is
an order homomorphism for
(\operatorname{dom}_{H},H)
.

We will most often work with order homomorphisms for
H
.
If
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\subseteq X
, then any order order homomorphism
\iota
for
(X,H)
is also an order homomorphism for
H
,
since
(a)
and
(b)
imply
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{H}
.
If
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\subseteq X
is relatively open,
72
72

72

For instance, if
H
is box-open on
X
.

then the converse holds:
if there exists an order homomorphism
\iota
for
H
, then there exists one for
(X,H)
.

To see this, one constructs by recursion a strict order preserving map
e:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}d
such that
\theta:=\iota\circ e
satisfies
N_{\theta(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle))}\cap X\subseteq N_{\iota(e(t){}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\cap\operatorname{dom}_{H}
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
;
then
\theta
is as required.

Remark 3.3.2
.

Suppose
X
and
H
are as in the previous definition
and
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to T(X)
.
We define the following
d
-dihypergraphs on
{}^{<\kappa}d
and
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
, respectively:

(1)

\mathbb{H}^{-}_{{}^{<\kappa}d}
consists of all sequences in
{}^{<\kappa}d
of the form

\langle t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle:\alpha<d\rangle
.

(2)

H^{-}_{X}
consists of all sequences
\langle u_{\alpha}:\alpha<d\rangle
in
T(X)

with
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<\kappa}(N_{u_{\alpha}}\cap X)\subseteq H
.
73
73

73

Note that a subtree
T
of
T(X)
is
(X,H)
-independent in the sense of Definition
3.2.1
if and only if it is
H^{-}_{X}
-independent in the sense that
H^{-}_{X}{\upharpoonright}T=\emptyset
.

Using these definitions,
(b)
in Definition
3.3.1
is equivalent to the statement that

\iota

is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}^{-}_{{}^{<\kappa}d}
to
H^{-}_{X}
.

Let
\mathbb{H}_{\iota,X}:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{t\in{}^{<\kappa}d}\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\cap X)
.

(b)
is further equivalent to the inclusion

\mathbb{H}_{\iota,X}\subseteq H
.
Note that
\mathbb{H}_{\iota,X}
is a box-open subset of
{}^{d}X
.
It is a dihypergraph if and only if

X\cap\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{i\in d}N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
since otherwise it contains constant sequences.
For
\mathbb{H}_{\iota,X}
to be a dihypergraph, it suffices that for every
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
, there exist
i,j\in d
with
\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\perp\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)
.
This condition is necessary if
\operatorname{ran}(\iota)\subseteq T(X)
,
(a)

T(X)
is
{\leq}|d|
-closed if
|d|<\kappa

and (b)

X
is closed and
T(X)
is
{<}\kappa
-closed if
|d|=\kappa
.

The next lemma shows that for relatively box-open dihypergraphs
H
,
the existence of an order homomorphism for
H

is equivalent to the existence of a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
We state a more general version for order homomorphisms for
(X,H)
which implies
the previous statement in the special case
X=\operatorname{dom}_{H}
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Suppose
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
H
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(1)

If
\iota
is an order homomorphism for
(X,H)
, then

[\iota]
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.

(2)

If
H{\upharpoonright}X
is box-open on
X
and
f
is

a continuous homomorphism
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
, then
there exists a continuous
74
74

74

See Definition
2.3.2

(b)
.

order homomorphism
\iota
for
(X,H)

and a continuous strict
\wedge
-homomorphism
75
75

75

See Definition
2.3.5
. Note that
e:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
is a strict
\wedge
-homomorphism if and only if it is strict order preserving and
e(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\wedge e(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)=e(t)
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
and
i,\,j\in d
with
i\neq j
. It is easy to see that such a map
e
is
\perp
-preserving, and hence
[e]
is a homeomorphism onto its image by Lemma
2.3.4

(2)
.

e:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}d

with
[\iota]=f\circ[e]
.
76
76

76

In general, it is not possible to find a strict order preserving map
\iota
that induces the original function
f
(i.e.,
[\iota]=f
). For instance, let
f
map
N_{\langle 0\rangle}
onto
N_{\langle 0\rangle}\cup N_{\langle 1\rangle}
. Note that it is always possible to find an order preserving map
\iota
that induces
f

[
andretta2022souslin
]
*Lemma 3.7. But order preserving maps
\iota
as in Definition
3.3.1

(b)
for graphs
H
are always
\perp
- and hence strict order preserving.

In particular,
(1)
and
(2)
apply to order homomorphisms for
H
and continuous homomorphisms from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
by letting
X=\operatorname{dom}_{H}
.

Proof.

We first show
(1)
.
Since
\iota
is strict order preserving,

[\iota]
is a continuous function from
{}^{\kappa}d
to
X

by Lemma
2.3.4
.
It thus suffices to show that
[\iota]
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
Suppose
\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in d\rangle\in\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
.
Let
u\in{}^{<\kappa}d
be such that

u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\subseteq x_{i}

for all
i\in d
.
Then

\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subseteq[\iota](x_{i})

for all
i\in d
, and therefore

{[\iota]^{d}}(\overline{x})=\langle[\iota](x_{i}):i\in d\rangle\in\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\cap X)\subseteq H.

To prove
(2)
, let
f
be a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.
We construct
continuous strict order preserving
maps

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

and

e:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}d

such that for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
and
i\in d
,

(i)

e(t){}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\subseteq e(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
,

(ii)

f(N_{e(t)})\subseteq N_{\iota(t)}
, and

(iii)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in d}(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\cap X)\subseteq H
.

We construct
\iota(t)
and
e(t)
by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(t)
.
Let
\iota(\emptyset):=e(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.
Let
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d\,{\setminus}\{\emptyset\}
, and suppose that
\iota(u)
and
e(u)
have been defined for all
u\subsetneq t
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
let
\iota(t):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq t}\iota(u)
and

e(t):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq t}e(u)
.
Since

N_{e(t)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{u\subsetneq t}N_{e(u)}

and
(ii)
holds for all
u\subsetneq t
,
we have

f(N_{e(t)})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{u\subsetneq t}f(N_{e(u)})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{u\subsetneq t}N_{\iota(u)}=N_{\iota(t)}.

Suppose
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in\mathrm{Succ}
, and let
u
be the immediate predecessor of
t
.
We define

\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

and

e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

for each
i\in d
simultaneously as follows.
Let

x_{i}\in{}^{\kappa}d
extend
e(u){}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
for each
i\in d
.
Since
f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
, the sequence

\langle f(x_{i}):i\in d\rangle
is a hyperedge of
H{\upharpoonright}X
.
Since
H{\upharpoonright}X
is box-open on
X
, there exists a sequence

\langle\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle):i\in d\rangle

such that

\iota(u)\subsetneq\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subsetneq f(x_{i})

and
(iii)
holds for
u
.
By the continuity of
f
, there exists for each
i\in d

an initial segment
e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
of
x_{i}
such that
e(u){}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\subseteq e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
and

(ii)
holds for
u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
.

Suppose the continuous strict order preserving
maps
\iota
and
e
have been constructed.

e
is a strict
\wedge
-homomorphism by
(i)
.

(ii)
implies that
for all
x\in{}^{\kappa}d
,

f\big([e](x)\big)\in\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{t\subsetneq x}f(N_{e(t)})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{t\subsetneq x}N_{\iota(t)}=\big\{[\iota](x)\big\}.

Thus
[\iota]=f\circ[e]
, and therefore

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq\operatorname{ran}(f)\subseteq X
.
This fact and
(iii)
imply that
\iota
is an order homomorphism for
(X,H)
.
∎

Remark 3.3.4
.

Fix any club
C
in
\kappa
.
The construction in the proof of Lemma
3.3.3

(2)
can be easily modified to obtain
\operatorname{lh}(\iota(t))\in C
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
in addition to the above properties.

3.4.
Full dihypergraphs

In this subsection,

d
and
c
are discrete topological spaces of cardinality at least
2
.
We show that if
H
and
I
are relatively box-open
dihypergraphs on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,
then

\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{ODD}^{I}_{\kappa}

whenever
H\equiv_{\mathsf{f}}I
in the sense of the next definition.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Suppose that
H
and
I
are
d
- and
c
- dihypergraphs, respectively, on
the same set
X
.

(a)

I
is
H
-full
(
H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
)
if
H{\upharpoonright}A
admits a
\kappa
-coloring for every
I
-independent subset
A
of
X
.
77
77

77

Equivalently,
H{\upharpoonright}A
admits a
\kappa
-coloring for all
A\subseteq X
such that
I{\upharpoonright}A
admits a
\kappa
-coloring.

(b)

H\equiv_{\mathsf{f}}I

if for all
A\subseteq X
,

H{\upharpoonright}A
admits a
\kappa
-coloring if and only if
