I{\upharpoonright}A
admits a
\kappa
-coloring.

Note that
H\equiv_{\mathsf{f}}I
if and only if
H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
and
I\leq_{\mathsf{f}}H
.
For example,

H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
if
H\subseteq I
or if
c\leq d
are ordinals and
any hyperedge of
H
has a subsequence in
I
,
since in these cases, every
I
-independent subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is
H
-independent.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Suppose
H
and
I
are relatively box-open
d
- and
c
- dihypergraphs, respectively, on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
If
H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
and
there exists a
continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
,
then there exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
I
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
The next claim is needed in the case that
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\not\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{I}

to ensure that

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{I}
for the map
\iota
constructed below.

Claim
.

There is a sequence
\langle U_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
of open subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that their intersection
U:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{\alpha<\kappa}U_{\alpha}
satisfies

U\cap\operatorname{dom}_{H}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{I}
and

{I{\upharpoonright}(U\cap f(N_{e(t)}))}\neq\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{\kappa}d
.

Proof.

Since
A:=\operatorname{dom}_{H}{\setminus}\operatorname{dom}_{I}
is
I
-independent and
H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
,
A
is the union of
H
-independent sets
A_{\alpha}
for
\alpha<\kappa
.
We show that the sets
U_{\alpha}:={}^{\kappa}\kappa\setminus\overline{A}_{\alpha}
are as required.
The first requirement is immediate.
Seeking a contradiction, suppose that
I{\upharpoonright}(U\cap f(N_{t}))=\emptyset
for some
t\in{}^{\kappa}d
.
Then
H{\upharpoonright}(U\cap f(N_{t}))
admits a
\kappa
-coloring
since
H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
.
Moreover,
H{\upharpoonright}(f(N_{t}){\setminus}U)
also admits a
\kappa
-coloring, since
\overline{A}_{\alpha}
is
H
-independent for each
\alpha<\kappa
by Corollary
3.2.3
.

Hence
H{\upharpoonright}f(N_{t})
admits a
\kappa
-coloring, contradicting Lemma
3.1.1

(2)
.
∎

We construct
continuous strict order preserving
maps

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}c\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

and

e:{}^{<\kappa}c\to{}^{<\kappa}d

such that for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}c
and
i\in c
,

(i)

f(N_{e(t)})\subseteq N_{\iota(t)}
,

(ii)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{j\in c}(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)}\cap\operatorname{dom}_{I})\subseteq I
, and

(iii)

N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\subseteq U_{\operatorname{lh}(t)}
.

\iota(t)
and
e(t)
are constructed by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(t)

by modifying the construction for Lemma
3.3.3

(2)
at successor lengths. In detail, let

\iota(\emptyset):=e(\emptyset)=\emptyset
.
Let
t\in{}^{<\kappa}c\,{\setminus}\{\emptyset\}
, and suppose that
\iota(u)
and
e(u)
have been defined for all
u\subsetneq t
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
let
\iota(t):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq t}\iota(u)
and

e(t):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq t}e(u)
.
Then
(i)
holds for

t
similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.3.3

(2)
.
Now, suppose
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in\mathrm{Succ}
and
let
u
be the immediate predecessor of
t
.
Define

\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

and

e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

for each
i\in c
simultaneously as follows.
Let
\overline{x}:=\langle x_{i}:i\in c\rangle
be a sequence in
N_{e(u)}

with
\langle f(x_{i}):i\in c\rangle\in I{\upharpoonright}U
.
Since
I
is relatively box-open and
f(x_{i})\in U\cap\operatorname{dom}_{I}\subseteq U_{\operatorname{lh}(u)}\cap\operatorname{dom}_{I}
for each
i\in c
,
take a sequence

\langle\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle):i\in c\rangle

with

\iota(u)\subsetneq\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subsetneq f(x_{i})

such that
(ii)
and
(iii)
hold for
u
.
By the continuity of
f
, choose

e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
with
e(u)\subsetneq e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subsetneq x_{i}
such that

(i)
holds for
u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
for each
i\in c
.

Suppose that
\iota
and
e
have been constructed.
Then
[\iota]=f\circ[e]

as in the proof of Lemma
3.3.3

(2)
, so

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq\operatorname{ran}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{H}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq U
by
(iii)
, and hence
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{I}
.
Thus
\iota
is an order homomorphism for
I
by
(ii)
,
so
[\iota]
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
I
by
Lemma
3.3.3

(1)
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.3
.

Suppose
H
and
I
are relatively box-open
d
- and
c
- dihypergraphs, respectively, on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
If
H\equiv_{\mathsf{f}}I
, then

\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{ODD}^{I}_{\kappa}
.
78
78

78

Equivalently, there exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
if and only if there exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
I
.

Remark 3.4.4
.

Suppose that
H
and
I
are
d
- and
c
- dihypergraphs, respectively, on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
I
is box-open on some superset
X
of
\operatorname{dom}_{H}
.

(1)

The assumption that
H
is relatively box-open is not needed for Lemma
3.4.2
.
To see this, we modify its proof as follows.
The claim is not needed, since
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\subseteq X
.
We construct
\iota
and
e
satisfying
(i)
and
(ii)
with
\operatorname{dom}_{I}
replaced by
X
.
The construction works as above, except at successor lengths when
\iota(u)
and
e(u)
have been constructed,
it suffices to find a hyperedge

\langle f(x_{i}):i\in c\rangle
of
I{\upharpoonright}f(N_{e(u)})
.
This can be done since

H\leq_{\mathsf{f}}I
and

H{\upharpoonright}f(N_{t})
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring
by Lemma
3.1.1

(2)
.
Since
I
is box-open on
X
, choose
\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subsetneq f(x_{i})
extending
\iota(u)
for each
i\in c
so that

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i\in c}(N_{\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\cap X)\subseteq I
,
and choose each
e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

as above.
Then
[\iota]=f\circ[e]

as in the proof of Lemma
3.3.3

(2)
,
so
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq\operatorname{dom}_{H}\subseteq X
.
Therefore
\iota
is an order homomorphism for
(X,I)
and hence
[\iota]
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
I
.

(2)

If
c=d
and
I\subseteq H
,
then the assumption that
H
is relatively box-open is not needed in the previous corollary.
This follows from
(1)
and the fact that any homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
is a homomorphism to
H
.

By
(2)
,

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}

if
H
contains a subdihypergraph
I
which is box-open on
X
and
H
-full, even if
H
is not relatively box-open itself.

Remark 3.4.5
.

Suppose that
H
and
I
are
d
- and
c
- dihypergraphs, respectively, on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
We call
I

injectively
H
-full
(
H\leq_{\mathsf{if}}I
)
if for every hyperedge
\overline{x}=\langle x_{j}:j\in d\rangle
of
H
,
there exists an injective map
\pi:c\to d

with
\langle x_{\pi(i)}:i\in c\rangle\in I
.
79
79

79

If
I
is injectively
H
-full, then
I
is
H
-full
since every
I
-independent subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is also
H
-independent.

We write
H\equiv_{\mathsf{if}}I
if
H\leq_{\mathsf{if}}I
and
I\leq_{\mathsf{if}}H
.

For instance, if
c\leq d
are ordinals and any hyperedge of
H
has a subsequence in
I
,
then
I\leq_{\mathsf{if}}H
.
If
I\leq_{\mathsf{if}}H
and
I
is box-open on a set
X
with
\operatorname{dom}_{H}\subseteq X
, then we can assume that the continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{c}}
to
I
in Lemma
3.4.2

is of the form
f\circ[e]

for some continuous strict
\wedge
-homomorphism
e:{}^{<\kappa}c\to{}^{<\kappa}d
and some continuous homomorphism
f
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
Moreover, we do not need to assume that
H
is relatively box-open.

To see this, we modify the construction in the previous remark at successor lengths as follows.
Assuming
\iota(u)
and
e(u)
have been constructed,
let

x_{j}\in{}^{\kappa}d
extend
e(u){}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle
for each
j\in d
.
Since
\langle f(x_{j}):j\in d\rangle\in H
and
I\leq_{\mathsf{if}}H
,

\langle f(x_{\pi_{u}(i)}):i\in c\rangle
is in
I
for some injective map
\pi_{u}:c\to d
.
Since
I
is box-open on
X
, take a sequence

\langle\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle):i\in c\rangle

such that

\iota(u)\subsetneq\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subsetneq f(x_{\pi_{u}(i)})

and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{j\in c}(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)}\cap X)\subseteq I
.
By the continuity of
f
, choose

e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

with

e(u){}^{\frown}\langle\pi_{u}(i)\rangle\subseteq e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\subsetneq x_{\pi_{u}(i)}

which also satisfies
(i)
from the previous construction.
As above,
\iota
is an order homomorphism for
(X,I)
with
[\iota]=f\circ[e]
.
Since
\pi_{u}
is injective for all
u\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
,

e(u{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\wedge e(u{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)=e(u)
for all
i\neq j\in c
, so
e
is a strict
\wedge
-homomorphism.

It follows that in this situation,
[e]
is a homeomorphism onto its image.
Thus, if
f
is injective or a homeomorphism onto its image, then the same holds for
f\circ[e]
. This will be relevant in Subsection
5.5
.

4.
The open dihypergraph dichotomy for definable sets

In this section we prove the main result, Theorem
1.4
.
The argument consists of three steps.
In the situation of the theorem,

\kappa
is a regular infinite cardinal,

2\leq d\leq\kappa
, and

X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and

H
is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

in some
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
-generic extension
V[G]
.
We may assume that
H{\upharpoonright}X
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring.
In the first step, we find an ordinal
\nu<\lambda
that reflects the set
\mathcal{T}
of trees that code
H
-independent closed sets
in the sense that

{\mathcal{T}}\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
,
where

G_{\nu}
denotes the restriction of
G
to
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\nu)
.
In the second step,
we work in
V[G_{\nu}]

to find a
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
-name
for an element of
X

that avoids all closed sets induced by trees in
\mathcal{T}\cap V[G_{\nu}]
.
Then we
transform this to an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name
\tau
to facilitate the third step,
where
\tau
is used
to construct a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.
The values of this homomorphism come from the evaluations of
\tau

by a
\kappa
-perfect set of
generic
filters with well-behaved quotient forcings.
The main technical step in the proof, the
Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
,
guarantees the existence
in
V[G]

of such a
\kappa
-perfect set.
More precisely, it constructs
a continuous function
g

from
{}^{\kappa}d

to the set of
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic filters with well-behaved quotient forcings
such that
g
induces a
homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
via
\tau
.
Controlling the quotient forcings is necessary to ensure the values of the homomorphism are in
X
.
The proof of the Nice Quotient Lemma is postponed to Subsections
4.2
and
4.3
.

In the countable case,
our proof
resembles
Solovay’s proof of the consistency of the perfect set property
\mathsf{PSP}_{\omega}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega})

[
Solovay1970
]
and
Feng’s proof of the consistency of the open graph dichotomy
\mathsf{OGD}_{\omega}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega})

[
FengOCA
]
.
80
80

80

See Definitions
1.1
and
2.3.1
.

In this case, the Nice Quotient Lemma is an easy consequence of Solovay’s lemma showing that all elements of the Baire space in
V[G]
have
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,{<}\lambda)
as a quotient.
However, it is well known that Solovay’s lemma fails in the uncountable case.
The Nice Quotient Lemma overcomes this difficulty by ensuring that
all elements of the
\kappa
-Baire space
that occur in the construction do have
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
as a quotient in the uncountable case.
This was done in
[
SchlichtPSPgames
]

for the
\kappa
-perfect set property using a similar idea but different technical means.

4.1.
Three important steps

4.1.a.
Reflection to intermediate models

We will work with the Lévy collapse of an inaccessible or a
Mahlo cardinal.
81
81

81

A cardinal
\lambda
is
Mahlo
if the set of inaccessible cardinals
\nu<\lambda
is stationary in
\lambda
.

The next lemma describes an essential property of such extensions.

Recall the notation
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},\mathbb{P}_{I},\mathbb{P}^{\alpha},G_{\alpha},G_{I},G^{\alpha}
from page
2.4
.
Note that if
\lambda
is inaccessible, then
\mathbb{P}^{\alpha}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
for all
\alpha<\lambda
, by Corollary
2.4.6
.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Suppose that
\kappa<\lambda
are regular infinite cardinals,
G
is
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic over
V
and
X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
in
V[G]
.
Suppose that either

(1)

\lambda
is inaccessible and
X\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}^{V[G]}
,
or

(2)

\lambda
is Mahlo.

Then there is a stationary (in
V
) subset
S\in V
of
\lambda
such that for all
\nu\in S
, we have
X\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
.
In fact, assuming
(1)
,
S
can be chosen as a final segment of
\lambda
.
Assuming
(2)
,
S
can be chosen so that every
\nu\in S
is inaccessible.

Proof.

First assume
(1)
.
In
V[G]
, let

X:=X_{\varphi,a}:=\{x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa:\varphi(x,a)\},

where
\varphi(x,a)
is a formula with a parameter
a\in{}^{\kappa}\mathrm{Ord}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
has the
\lambda
-c.c.,
a\in V[G_{\alpha}]
for some
\alpha<\lambda
.
We shall show that
X\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
for any
\nu\in S:=[\alpha,\lambda)
.
Let
x\in({}^{\kappa}\kappa)^{V[G_{\nu}]}
.
Since
a
is also in
V[G_{\nu}]
and

V[G]
is a
\mathbb{P}^{\nu}
-generic extension of
V[G_{\nu}]
and

\mathbb{P}^{\nu}
is homogeneous,

x\in X=X_{\varphi,a}\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{\nu}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}\varphi(x,a).

Thus,

X\cap V[G_{\nu}]
is equal to the set
\{x\in({}^{\kappa}\kappa)^{V[G_{\nu}]}:\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{\nu}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}\varphi(x,a)\}
,
which is in
V[G_{\nu}]
.

Now assume
(2)
.
Let
\dot{X}\in V
be a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name with
\dot{X}^{G}=X
.
Define
f:\lambda\to\lambda
as follows.
Given
\alpha<\lambda
, let
\mathrm{Name}_{\alpha}
denote the set of nice
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
-names for subsets of
\kappa\times\kappa
.
Note that
|\mathrm{Name}_{\alpha}|\leq 2^{\max\{\kappa,|\alpha|\}}
.
For each
\dot{x}\in\mathrm{Name}_{\alpha}
, choose a maximal antichain
A_{\dot{x}}
in
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
of conditions deciding whether
\dot{x}\in\dot{X}
.
By the
\lambda
-c.c. of
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
,
let
\mu_{\dot{x}}<\lambda
be least with
A_{\dot{x}}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\dot{x}}}
.
Finally, let
f(\alpha):=\sup\{\mu_{\dot{x}}:\dot{x}\in\mathrm{Name}_{\alpha}\}<\lambda
.

In
V
, there is a club of closure points of
f
, i.e. ordinals
\nu<\lambda
with
f(\alpha)<\nu
for all
\alpha<\nu
.
Since
\lambda
is Mahlo, the set
S
of inaccessible closure points of
f
is stationary in
\lambda
.

Let
\nu\in S
.
We shall show that
X\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
.

Claim
.

For any nice
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
-name
\dot{x}
for a subset of
\kappa\times\kappa
, exactly one of the following holds:

(i)

\exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\dot{x}\in\dot{X}
.

(ii)

\exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\dot{x}\notin\dot{X}
.

Proof.

Since
\nu
is inaccessible,
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
has the
\nu
-c.c.
Since
\dot{x}
is a nice
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
-name, it is a
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
-name for some
\gamma<\nu
.
By the definition of
f
, there is

A\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{f(\gamma)}
such that
A
is
a maximal antichain in
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
of conditions deciding whether
\dot{x}\in\dot{X}
.
Since
\nu
is a closure point of
f
, we have
f(\gamma)<\nu
.
Thus
A
is a maximal antichain in
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
.
Let
p\in G_{\nu}\cap A
.
∎

Claim
.

If
\dot{x}\in\mathrm{Name}_{\alpha}
and
\dot{x}^{G_{\nu}}=x
, then:

(i)

x\in X\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\dot{x}\in\dot{X}
.

(ii)

x\notin X\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\dot{x}\notin\dot{X}
.

Proof.

By the previous claim, it suffices to show the implications from right to left.
Suppose
p\in G_{\nu}
and
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\dot{x}\in\dot{X}
.
Since
\dot{x}
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
-name and
G_{\nu}\subseteq G
, we have
\dot{x}^{G_{\nu}}=\dot{x}^{G}\in\dot{X}^{G}
.
The second implication is similar.
∎

In
V[G_{\nu}]
, define

X_{\nu}:=\{{\dot{x}}^{G_{\nu}}:\>\exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\dot{x}\in\dot{X}\}
.
Then we have
X\cap V[G_{\nu}]=X_{\nu}\in V[G_{\nu}]
.
∎

Remark 4.1.2
.

The previous lemma also has a version for functions:
Let
\kappa<\lambda
be regular infinite cardinals, let
G
be
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic over
V
and let
F:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be a function in
V[G]
.
If either

(1)

\lambda
is inaccessible and
F\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}^{V[G]}
, or

(2)

\lambda
is Mahlo,

then there is a stationary (in
V
) subset
S\in V
of
\lambda
such that for all
\nu\in S
, we have
F{\upharpoonright}V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
.
Assuming
(1)
,
S
can be chosen as a final segment of
\lambda
, and for
(2)
,
S
can be chosen so that every
\nu\in S
is inaccessible.

To see this, apply the previous lemma
to the set

X=\{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle{}^{\frown}x:x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa,\,(\alpha,\beta)\in F(x)\}
.
If
X\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
, then
F{\upharpoonright}V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
,

since
F{\upharpoonright}V[G_{\nu}]
can be calculated from
X\cap V[G_{\nu}]
.

Remark 4.1.3
.

The Mahlo cardinal is necessary in Lemma
4.1.1

(2)
and Remark
4.1.2

(2)
.
To see this, assume the
{\mathrm{GCH}}
holds and
\lambda>\kappa
is any cardinal that is not Mahlo.
We claim that
there exists a function
F:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
in
V[G]
such that

F{\upharpoonright}V[G_{\alpha}]\notin V[G_{\alpha}]
for all
\alpha
with
\kappa\leq\alpha<\lambda
.

First suppose
\lambda
is singular in
V
.
Then
\lambda
remains singular in
V[G]
.
Hence
\lambda\neq(\kappa^{+})^{V[G]}
and
\lambda
is collapsed in
V[G]
, so there exists some
x\in({}^{\kappa}\kappa)^{V[G]}
with
x\notin V[G_{\alpha}]
for all
\alpha<\lambda
.

Now suppose
\lambda
is regular in
V
.
Let
C
be a club in
\lambda
whose elements are not inaccessible.
Additionally, if
\lambda
is a successor cardinal then assume
C
does not contain cardinals, while if
\lambda
is a limit cardinal then assume
C
contains only cardinals.
For any
\alpha<\lambda
, let
\alpha^{\prime}
denote the least ordinal
above
\alpha
in
C
.
Take any function
F:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
in
V[G]
with the following property: if
\alpha<\lambda
is least with
x\in V[G_{\alpha}]
, then
F(x)\notin V[G_{\alpha^{\prime}}]
.

We show that

F{\upharpoonright}V[G_{\alpha}]\notin V[G_{\alpha}]
for all
\alpha
with
\kappa\leq\alpha<\lambda
.
To see this, fix any such
\alpha
.
We distinguish two cases:

Case 1
.

\alpha
is not a limit point of
C
.

Then there is some
\gamma\in C
with
\gamma<\alpha\leq\gamma^{\prime}
.
Take any
x\in V[G_{\alpha}]\setminus V[G_{\gamma}]
.
Let
\beta>\gamma
be least with
x\in V[G_{\beta}]
.
Then
F(x)\notin V[G_{\beta^{\prime}}]
by the definition of
F
.
Since

\alpha\leq\gamma^{\prime}\leq\beta^{\prime}
, we have
F(x)\notin V[G_{\alpha}]
as required.

Case 2
.

\alpha
is a limit point of
C
.

We claim that there is some
x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
\alpha
is least with
x\in V[G_{\alpha}]
.
First suppose that
\lambda
is successor cardinal in
V
.
82
82

82

For successor cardinals
\lambda
, the proof does not use that
\alpha
is a limit point of
C
, so Case
1
is not needed.

Let
\mu:=|\alpha|^{V}
, so
\mu<\alpha<(\mu^{+})^{V}

since
C
does not contain cardinals.
Since
|\alpha|=\kappa
in
V[G_{\mu+1}]
,
G_{(\mu+1,\alpha)}
can be coded as a subset
x
of
\kappa
via a bijection
f:\kappa\to\alpha
with
f\in V[G_{\mu+1}]
.
Then
\alpha
is least with
x\in V[G_{\alpha}]
.
Now suppose
\lambda
is a limit cardinal in
V
.
Since
C
contains only cardinals and the
{\mathrm{GCH}}
holds,
\alpha
is singular in
V
.Thus
\alpha\neq(\kappa^{+})^{V[G_{\alpha}]}
and
\alpha
is collapsed in
V[G_{\alpha}]
, so the claim follows.

Then
F(x)\notin V[G_{\alpha^{\prime}}]
by the definition of
F
. Thus,
F(x)\notin V[G_{\alpha}]
as required.

Remark 4.1.4
.

The proof of Lemma
4.1.1

(2)

can be adapted to show that if
\lambda>\kappa
is inaccessible, then
the following strengthening of
\Diamond_{\kappa^{+}}

holds in

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic extensions
V[G]
:

\Diamond^{+}_{\kappa^{+}}
: there exists a sequence

\langle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle
of sets

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\alpha)
such that

(a)

|\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}|\leq\kappa
for all
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
, and

(b)

for all
X\subseteq\kappa^{+}
, there exists a club
C
in
\kappa^{+}
such that for all
\alpha\in C
,

X\cap\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
and
C\cap\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
.

We show that in
V[G]
,

\langle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{P}(\alpha)^{V[G_{\alpha}]}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is a
\Diamond^{+}_{\kappa^{+}}
-sequence.
It is clear that
(a)
holds in
V[G]
.
To show
(b)
,
we use an argument similar to the proof of Lemma
4.1.1

(2)
.
83
83

83

Note that this version of the argument works assuming only that
\lambda
is inaccessible.

Let
X
be a subset of
\kappa^{+}=\lambda
in
V[G]
, and let
\dot{X}\in V
be a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name with
\dot{X}^{G}=X
.
In
V
, first define a function
f:\lambda\to\lambda
as follows.
For each
\alpha<\lambda
,
let
A
be a maximal antichain in
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
consisting of conditions
deciding whether
\alpha\in\dot{X}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
has the
\lambda
-c.c., choose
f(\alpha)<\lambda
with
A\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{f(\alpha)}
.

Let
C
be a club of limit ordinals
\nu<\lambda
that are closure points of
f
.
We shall show that
C
satisfies
(b)
.

Since
C\in V
, it suffices to show that
X\cap\nu\in\mathcal{A}_{\nu}=\mathcal{P}(\nu)^{V[G_{\nu}]}
for any given
\nu\in C
.

Claim
.

For any
\alpha<\nu
, exactly one of the following holds:

(i)

\exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\alpha\in\dot{X}
.

(ii)

\exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\alpha\notin\dot{X}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
f
, there is

A\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{f(\gamma)}
such that
A
is
a maximal antichain in
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
of conditions deciding whether
\alpha\in\dot{X}
.
Since
\nu
is a closure point of
f
, we have
f(\gamma)<\nu
.
Thus
A
is a maximal antichain in
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
.
Let
p\in G_{\nu}\cap A
.
∎

As in the proof of Lemma
4.1.1

(2)
,
it now follows that
for all
\alpha<\nu
,

\alpha\in X\cap\nu\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists p\in G_{\nu}\ \,p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}^{V}\alpha\in\dot{X}.

Thus,

X\cap\nu
is an element of

\mathcal{A}_{\nu}=\mathcal{P}(\nu)^{V[G_{\nu}]}
.

4.1.b.
Names and witness functions

Assumptions 4.1.5
.

The following assumptions will be used throughout the rest of this section, unless explicitly otherwise stated.
In
V
:

•

\kappa
is a regular infinite cardinal,

2\leq d\leq\kappa

and
\lambda>\kappa
is an inaccessible cardinal.

G
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}=\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
-generic filter over
V
.

•

\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
holds in
V
.
This may be assumed without loss of generality,
because
\mathbb{P}_{2}
forces
\kappa{}^{<\kappa}=\kappa
and
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
.

In
V[G]
:

•

X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
in
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.
More specifically

X=X_{\varphi,a}=\{x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa:\varphi(x,a)\},

where

\varphi(x,a)

is a first order formula

with a parameter
a\in{}^{\kappa}\mathrm{Ord}
.

•

H
is a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

such that
H{\upharpoonright}X
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring.

•

\lambda
is a Mahlo cardinal in
V
, or
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
in
V[G]
.
84
84

84

This assumption can be weakened as in Remark
4.1.8
below.
,
85
85

85

If
d<\kappa
, then
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
follows from the assumption that
H
is box-open by Lemma
3.1.2
.

To prove Theorem
1.4
, it suffices to prove
that
\mathsf{ODD}^{H{\upharpoonright}X}_{\kappa}
holds under these assumptions.
Since we assume that
H{\upharpoonright}X
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring,
it further suffices to show that there is
a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.

We work in
V[G]
unless otherwise stated. Let
T
be a subtree of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
Recall from Definition
3.2.1

that
T
is an
H
-independent tree if
for all sequences
\langle t_{\alpha}\in T:\alpha<d\rangle
,
we have

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}N_{t_{\alpha}}\not\subseteq H
.
Since
H
is box-open on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,

[T]
is an
H
-independent set
whenever
T
is an
H
-independent tree
and the converse holds when
T
is pruned,
by Lemma
3.2.2
.

Definition 4.1.6
.

For all
\alpha\leq\lambda
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\{T\in V[G_{\alpha}]:T\text{ is a subtree of ${}^{<\kappa}\kappa$}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{ind}}

\displaystyle:=\{T\in V[G_{\alpha}]:T\text{ is an $H$-independent subtree of ${}^{<\kappa}\kappa$}\}.

Let
\alpha<\lambda
.
Then
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
has size
\kappa
in
V[G]

since
\lambda
is inaccessible,
and
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\in V[G_{\alpha}]
.
However,
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{ind}}
may not be in
V[G_{\alpha}]
.

Lemma 4.1.7
.

There is a stationary (in
V
) subset
S\in V
of
\lambda
such that

\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}\in V[G_{\nu}]
for all
\nu\in S
.

Proof.

In
V[G]
,
code
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{ind}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}({}^{<\kappa}\kappa)
as a subset
Y_{\lambda}
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, using a bijection
f:\kappa\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
in
V
.
If
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{ind}}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
and hence
Y_{\lambda}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.
Otherwise,
\lambda
is Mahlo.
In both cases, Lemma
4.1.1
yields stationarily many cardinals
\nu<\lambda
such that
Y_{\lambda}\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
, and hence
\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}=\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{ind}}\cap V[G_{\nu}]\in V[G_{\nu}]
.
∎

For the rest of this section, we fix a cardinal
\nu
such that
\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}\in V[G_{\nu}]
and
a\in V[G_{\nu}]
,
where
a\in({}^{\kappa}\mathrm{Ord})^{V[G]}
is the parameter with
X=X_{\varphi,a}^{V[G]}
.
This may be done by the previous lemma and the
\lambda
-c.c. for
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
.

Remark 4.1.8
.

Note that the assumption that either
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}^{V[G]}
or
\lambda
is Mahlo in
V
in Assumption
4.1.5

was needed only to obtain the previous lemma.
From now on,
we will use only the fact that

\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}\in V[G_{\nu}]
,
a\in V[G_{\nu}]
and the rest of Assumption
4.1.5
.

The next lemma shows that there is a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name
\sigma
in
V[G_{\nu}]
for an element
of
X
that
avoids all sets
[T]
with

Lemma 4.1.9
.

In
V[G_{\nu}]
, there exists a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name
\sigma
with the following properties:

(1)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}{\sigma}\in X_{\varphi,a}\setminus V[G_{\nu}].
86
86

86

Note that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}{\sigma}\notin V[G_{\nu}]
follows from
(2)
as in the proof of the lemma.

(2)

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}{\sigma\notin[T]}
for all
{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}}
.

Proof.

Work in
V[G]
.

[T]
is an
H
-independent set for all
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}

by Lemma
3.2.2

(1)
.
Since

H{\upharpoonright}X=H{\upharpoonright}X_{\varphi,a}
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring and

\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}
has size at most
\kappa
,
we have

(4.1)

X_{\varphi,a}-\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{[T]:T\in\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}\big\}\neq\emptyset.

Note that (

)
is a first-order statement with parameters in

,
because
\nu
was chosen so that

\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}
and

.

Since
\mathbb{P}^{\nu}
is homogeneous,

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{\nu}}
forces in

that (

) holds.
Since the forcings
\mathbb{P}^{\nu}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
are equivalent
by Corollary
2.4.6
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}
forces in

V[G_{\nu}]
that (
4.1
) holds.
By the maximal principle, there exists a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name
\sigma\in V[G_{\nu}]

for an element of

which satisfies
(2)
.
To see that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}{\sigma\notin V[G_{\nu}]}

let

.
Since
\{x\}
is

-independent,

T(\{x\})

is in
\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}
by Lemma

(2)
.
Thus
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}\sigma\neq x
by
(2)
.
∎

Recall from Definition
2.4.13
that
if
\mathbb{Q}
is a forcing,
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\tau
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for an element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,
then

T^{\tau,q}=T^{\tau,q}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\{t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\>:\>\exists r\leq q\ \ r\Vdash t\subseteq\tau\}

is the
tree of possible values for
\tau
below
q
.
Recall also that the definition of
T^{\tau,q}
is absolute between transitive models of
{\sf ZFC}
with the same

by Lemma
2.4.15

(4)
.
In particular,
if
\tau\in V[G_{\nu}]
, then

(T^{\tau,q})^{V[G_{\nu}]}=(T^{\tau,q})^{V[G]}
.

The next lemma shows that the trees

T^{\sigma,p}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}
are not
H
-independent for names
\sigma
as in the previous lemma.

Lemma 4.1.10
.

T^{\sigma,p}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\nu}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}

for all names
\sigma
as in Lemma
4.1.9
and
all
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
, and let

T:=T^{\sigma,p}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}
.
Since
\sigma\in V[G_{\nu}]
, we have

T\in\mathcal{T}_{\nu}
by Lemma
2.4.15

(4)
.
Moreover,
p\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\sigma\in[T]
by Lemma
2.4.15

(2)
.
Therefore

T\notin\mathcal{T}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ind}}

since
\sigma
was chosen so that Lemma

(2)
holds.
∎

We now prove an analogue of the two previous lemmas for
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
Recall that

\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1):=\{p:\alpha\to\kappa\,\mid\,\alpha<\kappa\}.

Lemma 4.1.11
.

For unboundedly many
\gamma<\lambda
, there is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name
\tau
in
V[G_{\gamma}]

for a new element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

with the following properties:

(1)

In
V[G_{\gamma}]
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}
forces that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash\check{\tau}\in X_{\varphi,a}.

(2)

T^{\,\tau,\,q}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}\in\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ind}}

for all
q\in\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.

Proof.

Fix a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name
\sigma
as in Lemma
4.1.9
.
We first sketch the idea.
First choose some
\gamma
so that

\sigma\in V[G_{\gamma}]
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
-name
and the forcings
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
and

\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
are equivalent in
V[G_{\gamma}]
.
Then transform

\sigma

to an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name
\tau\in V[G_{\gamma}]
,
and finally show that
Lemma
4.1.9

(1)

and Lemma
4.1.10
yield the required properties of
\tau
.

Here is the detailed proof.
First, note that for all sufficiently large
\gamma\in[\nu,\lambda)
, we have that

\sigma\in V[G_{\gamma}]
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
-name.
This holds
because
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
has the
\lambda
-c.c. and
\sigma\in V[G_{\nu}]
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-name for an element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
Let
\gamma
be sufficiently large with
\gamma=\xi+1
for some ordinal

\xi<\lambda
with
\xi^{<\kappa}=\xi
.
Then
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}

has size
\kappa
in
V[G_{\gamma}]
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
is also non-atomic and
{<}\kappa
-closed,
it is equivalent in
V[G_{\gamma}]
to
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
,
and in fact there exists a dense embedding
\iota:\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1)\to\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
in
V[G_{\gamma}]
by Lemma
2.4.4
.
Let

\tau:=\sigma^{\iota}
.
88
88

88

See Definition
2.4.2
.

This is an
\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1)
-name and thus an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name.

We first show that in
V[G_{\gamma}]
,

\tau
is a name for a new element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and

(1)
holds.
Let
J
be
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
,
and let
x:=\tau^{J}
. It suffices to prove the following claim.

Claim
.

x\notin V[G_{\gamma}]
and

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\gamma}][J]}\check{x}\in X_{\varphi,a}.

Proof.

Let
K
be the upwards closure of
\iota[J\cap\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1)]
in
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
.
Then
K
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
-generic filter over
V[G_{\gamma}]
such that

V[G_{\gamma}][J]=V[G_{\gamma}][K]

and

\sigma^{K}=x
.
Then
x\notin V[G_{\nu}]

by
Lemma
4.1.9

(1)
.
Since
K
and
G_{[\nu,\gamma)}
are mutually generic over
V[G_{\nu}]
,
we have
V[K]\cap V[G_{\gamma}]=V[G_{\nu}]
.
Therefore
x\notin V[G_{\gamma}]
.
We also have

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}]}{\sigma}\in X_{\varphi,a}

by Lemma
4.1.9

(1)
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}\times\mathbb{P}^{\gamma}
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{\gamma}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\nu}][K]}\check{x}\in X_{\varphi,a}.

Since

\mathbb{P}^{\gamma}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{[\nu,\gamma)}\times\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
89
89

89

See Corollary
2.4.6
.

and
V[G_{\gamma}][K]
is a
\mathbb{P}_{[\nu,\gamma)}
-generic extension of
V[G_{\nu}][K]
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\gamma}][K]}\check{x}\in X_{\varphi,a}.

The claim holds since

V[G_{\gamma}][J]=V[G_{\gamma}][K]
.
∎

To see
(2)
, let
q\in\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
and

T:=T^{\,\tau,\,q}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}
.
Since
\tau\in V[G_{\gamma}]
, we have

T\in\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}

by Lemma
2.4.15

(4)
.

Claim
.

T
is not
H
-independent.
90
90

90

I.e.,
T\notin\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ind}}
.

Proof.

Choose some
q^{*}\in\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1)
with
q^{*}\leq q
,
and let

T^{*}=T^{\,\sigma,\iota(q^{*})}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}
.
By Lemma
4.1.10
,

T^{*}\in V[G_{\nu}]\subseteq V[G_{\gamma}]
and

T^{*}
is not
H
-independent.
It thus suffices to show that
T^{*}\subseteq T
.
To prove this, work in
V[G_{\gamma}]
and
let
v\in T^{*}
.
Choose
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
with
p\leq\iota(q^{*})
and

p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}v\subseteq\sigma
.

Let
p{\upharpoonright}\gamma:=p{\upharpoonright}((\gamma\times\kappa)\cap\operatorname{dom}(p))
.

Then
p{\upharpoonright}\gamma\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
and

p{\upharpoonright}\gamma\leq\iota(q^{*})

since
\iota(q^{*})\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
.
As the formula “
x\subseteq y
” is absolute,

p{\upharpoonright}\gamma\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}}v\subseteq\sigma
.
By the density of
\operatorname{ran}(\iota)
in
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
,
there exists
r\in\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1)

such that

\iota(r)\leq p{\upharpoonright}\gamma
. Then

(i)

\iota(r)\leq\iota(q^{*})
and

(ii)

\iota(r)\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}}v\subseteq\sigma
.

Since
\iota
is a dense embedding,

(i)
implies
r\leq q^{*}
as
\mathrm{Add^{*}}(\kappa,1)
is separative.
Because
\tau=\sigma^{\iota}
,

(ii)
implies
r\Vdash v\subseteq\tau
.
Thus,
r
witnesses that

v\in T

as required.
∎

This completes the proof of Lemma
4.1.11
.
∎

We now define the notion of an
H
-witness function for
\tau
and
\mathbb{Q}
.
The existence of such a function
is an explicit
and equivalent way of stating that
the trees
T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,q}
are not
H
-independent for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 4.1.12
.

Suppose
\mathbb{Q}
is a forcing and
\tau
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for an
element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
An
H
-witness function
for
\tau
and
\mathbb{Q}

is a function

w:\mathbb{Q}\times d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

such that for all
p\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\alpha<d

(1)

w(p,\alpha)\in T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,p}
and

(2)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\beta<d}N_{w(p,\beta)}\subseteq H
.

Such functions exist for the names

\tau
from
Lemma
4.1.11
and
\mathbb{Q}=\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
91
91

91

Note that
H
-witness functions need not exist.
For instance, if
\tau=\check{x}
for some
x
in the ground model, then

T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,q}=T(\{x\})

for any
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Therefore
T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,q}
is

H
-independent and
there is no
H
-witness function for
\check{x}
.

Definition 4.1.13
.

A
step function
for a given forcing
\mathbb{Q}
is a function
s:\mathbb{Q}\times d\to\mathbb{Q}

such that

s(q,\alpha)\leq q

for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\alpha<d
.

We now show that if there is an
H
-witness function, then there is an
H
-witness function with some nice properties and a corresponding step function.

Lemma 4.1.14
.

Suppose
\mathbb{Q}
is a forcing and
\tau
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for an element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(1)

If there exists an
H
-witness function for
\tau
and
\mathbb{Q}
, then there exists an
H
-witness
function
w
such that

\tau_{[p]}\subsetneq w(p,\alpha)\,

for all
p\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\alpha<d
.

(2)

If
w
is an
H
-witness function for
\tau
and
\mathbb{Q}
, then
there exists a step function
s
for
\mathbb{Q}
such that

s(p,\alpha)\Vdash w(p,\alpha)\subseteq\tau.

Proof.

To see
(1)
, suppose
u:\mathbb{Q}\times d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

is an
H
-witness function for
\tau
and
\mathbb{Q}
.
For any
p\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have
\tau_{[p]}\cup u(p,\alpha)\in T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,p}
for all
\alpha<d
, since
\tau_{[p]}
equals the stem of
T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,p}

by Lemma
2.4.15

(1)
.
It is easy to see that
T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,p}
has no terminal nodes.
Hence there is a sequence

\langle w_{p}^{\alpha}:\alpha<d\rangle
in
T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,p}

such that
\tau_{[p]}\cup u(p,\alpha)\subsetneq w_{p}^{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<d
.
Define

w:\mathbb{Q}\times d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

by letting

w(p,\alpha):=w_{p}^{\alpha}
for all
p\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\alpha<d
. It is clear that
w
is as required.

To see
(2)
, let
w:\mathbb{Q}\times d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
be an
H
-witness function for
\tau
and
\mathbb{Q}
.
By the definition of
T_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\tau,q}
,
there exists
s_{q}^{\alpha}

such that
s_{q}^{\alpha}\leq p
and

s_{q}^{\alpha}\Vdash w(p,\alpha)\subseteq\tau

for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\alpha<d
.
Define
s:\mathbb{Q}\times d\to\mathbb{Q}
by letting
s(q,\alpha):=s^{\alpha}_{q}

for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\alpha<d
.
Then
s
is as required.
∎

4.1.c.
Construction of continuous homomorphisms

In our proof of Theorem
1.4
,
we will define a continuous homomorphism
f
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.
The values of the homomorphism come from the evaluations of the
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name
\tau
from the previous subsection
by a
\kappa
-perfect set of generic filters.
The difficulty is to ensure that
\operatorname{ran}(f)\subseteq X
.
The next lemma, the
Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
, does this by controlling quotient forcings:
It will guarantee the existence
in
V[G]

of a continuous function
whose range consists of elements with well-behaved quotient forcings.
This function will induce the required homomorphism
f
via
\tau
.

We will construct
this function
along an appropriately chosen step function.
We will need the next definition.

Definition 4.1.15
.

Suppose
s
is a step function for a given forcing
\mathbb{Q}
.
A strict order reversing map

h:{}^{<\kappa}d\to\mathbb{Q}
is called an
s
-function

if for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
and all
\alpha<d
:

(4.2)

h\big(t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle\big)<s\big(h(t),\alpha\big).

Note that it is necessary to require that
h
is strict order reversing in the previous definition
since this does not follow from (
4.2
).

However, (
4.2
) does imply that

h(u)<h(t)
when
u
is a direct successor of
t
.
Moreover,
if
\mathbb{Q}=\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)

and
h
is an
s
-function, then

[h]:{}^{\kappa}d\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is continuous and

[h](x)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}s\big(h(x{\upharpoonright}\alpha),x(\alpha)\big)

holds for all
x\in{}^{\kappa}d
.

Lemma 4.1.16
.

(Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
)

The following holds in
V[G]
.
Suppose that
\gamma<\lambda
and

s
is a step-function for
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
Then there exists an
s
-function
h

such that for all
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h])
:

(1)

x
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
.
93
93

93

Here, we identify
x
with

{{x}{\downarrow}}=\{t\in\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1):t\subsetneq x\}
.

(2)

V[G]
is a

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic
extension of
V[G_{\gamma}][x]
.

We shall prove two cases of the Nice Quotient Lemma separately: the countable case in
Subsection
4.2
and the uncountable case in
Subsections
4.3.b
and
4.3.c
.

In the rest of this subsection, we aim to construct a continuous homomorphism

f:{}^{\kappa}d\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa

from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.
To this end,
fix an ordinal
\gamma
and an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name
\tau
in
V[G_{\gamma}]

as in Lemma
4.1.11
.
Then there exists an
H
-witness function for
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
and
\tau
.
Thus, we may also fix
an
H
-witness function
w
for
\tau
and step function
s
for
\tau
and
w
as in Lemma
4.1.14
.
Lastly, let

h
be an
s
-function as in the previous lemma.

We argue in
V[G]
.
We define a function
f:{}^{\kappa}d\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
by letting

f(z):=\tau^{[h](z)}

for all
z\in{}^{\kappa}d
.

Lemma 4.1.17
.

\operatorname{ran}(f)\subseteq X
.

Proof.

Let
z\in({}^{\kappa}d)^{V[G]}
, and let
x:=[h](z)
.
Then
f(z)=\tau^{x}
.
Since
\tau
was chosen so that
Lemma
4.1.11

(1)

holds in
V[G_{\gamma}]

and
x
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]

by Lemma
4.1.16

(1)
,

\qquad\qquad\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash f(z)\in X_{\varphi,a}.

holds in
V[G_{\gamma}][x]
.
Therefore

f(z)\in{(X_{\varphi,a})}^{V[G]}=X
,
by Lemma
4.1.16

(2)
.
∎

It now suffices to prove the following.

Lemma 4.1.18
.

f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.

Proof.

We will define
a map

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

and show that it is an order homomorphism for

({}^{\kappa}\kappa,H)

with
f=[\iota]
.
This is sufficient by Lemma
3.3.3

(1)
.

Let
\iota(\emptyset):=\emptyset.

If
\operatorname{lh}(v)\in\mathrm{Succ}
and
v=u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle
, then let

\iota(v):=w\big(h(u),\alpha\big)
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(v)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
let
\iota(v):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq v}\iota(u)
.

Claim
.

For all
v\in{}^{<\kappa}d
,
h(v)\Vdash_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}^{V[G_{\gamma}]}\iota(v)\subseteq\tau
.

Proof.

This is proved by induction on
\operatorname{lh}(v)
.
It is clear for
v=\emptyset
.
For the successor case, note that

s(h(u),\alpha)\Vdash_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}w(h(u),\alpha)\subseteq\tau

holds in
V[G]

by Lemma
4.1.14

(2)
.
Therefore this also holds in
V[G_{\gamma}]
since the formula
“
p\Vdash\theta\subseteq\eta
” is absolute between transitive models of
{\sf ZFC}
.
94
94

94

It
can be defined by a recursion which
uses only absolute concepts; see
[
KunenBook2013
, Theorem II.4.15]
.

Since
h
is an
s
-function, we have
s(h(u),\alpha)\subseteq h(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)
.
Moreover,
\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)=w(h(u),\alpha)
by the definition of
\iota
.
Hence

h(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)\Vdash_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}^{V[G_{\gamma}]}\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)\subseteq\tau.

If
\operatorname{lh}(v)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
, then
h(v)\supseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq v}h(u)
and
\iota(v)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq v}\iota(u)
.
Thus
h(v)\Vdash_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}^{V[G_{\gamma}]}\iota(v)\subseteq\tau
by the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Now
\iota
is strict order preserving, since

\iota(u)\subseteq\tau_{[h(u)]}\subsetneq w(h(u),\alpha)=\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)

by the previous claim, Lemma
4.1.14

(1)
and the definition of
\iota
.
To see that
\iota
is an order homomorphism for
({}^{\kappa}\kappa,H)
, note that

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}N_{\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}N_{w(h(u),\alpha)}\subseteq H

by the definition of
\iota
and
since
w
is an
H
-witness function.
To see that

f=[\iota]
, recall that
f(z)=\tau^{[h](z)}
by the definition of
f
.
Since
h(z{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\Vdash_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}^{V[G_{\gamma}]}\iota(z{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\subseteq\tau
, we have
\iota(z{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\subseteq f(z)
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.4
except for the proof of the
Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
.

4.2.
The countable case

In this section, we complete the proof of the open dihypergraph dichotomy for definable sets in the countable case (Theorem
1.4
for
\kappa=\omega
).
The missing step is the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,{<}\lambda)
.
We make use of the next lemma:

Lemma 4.2.1
.

(Solovay
[
Solovay1970
]
, see
[
MR1940513
, Corollary 26.11]
)

For any
x\in{({}^{\omega}\mathrm{Ord})}^{V[G]}
, there exists a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic
filter
95
95

95

For
\kappa=\omega
,
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
equals
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,{<}\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is inaccessible.

K
over
V[x]
such that
V[G]=V[x][K]
.

The proof idea for Solovay’s lemma is that for any regular uncountable cardinal
\mu
,
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,\mu)
is the unique forcing of size
\mu
that makes
\mu
countable
[
MR1940513
, Lemma 26.7]
.

Using this, one shows that the
quotient forcing of
V[x]
in
V[G]
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,{<}\lambda)
.

We use the notation from the previous section for
\kappa=\omega
.
It remains to prove the following.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

(Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,{<}\lambda)
)

The following holds in
V[G]
.
Suppose that
\gamma<\lambda
and

s
is a step-function for
\operatorname{Add}(\omega,1)
.
Then there exists an
s
-function
h

such that for all
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h])
:

(1)

x
is
\operatorname{Add}(\omega,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
.

(2)

V[G]
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic extension of
V[G_{\gamma}][x]
.

Proof.

By Solovay’s Lemma
4.2.1
for
V[G_{\gamma}]
, it suffices to construct an
s
-function
h
such that
(1)
holds.
Let
\langle D_{n}:n<\omega\rangle\in V[G]
enumerate all dense subsets of
\operatorname{Add}(\omega,1)
that are elements of
V[G_{\gamma}]
.
This is possible since
({2^{\omega}})^{V[G_{\gamma}]}
is countable in
V[G]
.
We construct
h:{}^{<\omega}d\to\operatorname{Add}(\omega,1)
with the following properties for all
u,v\in{}^{<\omega}d
and
\alpha<\kappa
:

(i)

If
u\subsetneq v
, then
h(v)<h(u)
.

(ii)

h(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)<s(h(u),\alpha)
.

(iii)

h(u)\in D_{\operatorname{lh}(u)}
.

We construct
h(u)
by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(u)
.
Let
h(\emptyset)\in D_{0}
.
Now fix
u\in{}^{<\omega}d
, and suppose that
h(u)
has been constructed.
For each
\alpha<d
,
choose
h(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)<s(h(u),\alpha)
in
D_{\operatorname{lh}(u)+1}
.
Then
h
satisfies

(i)
-
(iii)
.
By
(i)
,
h
is order preserving, and so by
(ii)
, it is an
s
-function.
By
(iii)
, each
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h])
is
\operatorname{Add}(\omega,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
.
∎

Remark 4.2.3
.

While
\mathsf{OGD}^{\omega}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{1})
is provable in
{\sf ZFC}

[
CarroyMillerSoukup
, Theorem 1.1]
,
\mathsf{OGD}^{\omega}_{\omega}(\mathsf{D}_{\omega})
has the consistency strength of an inaccessible cardinal, since
\mathsf{OGD}^{\omega}_{\omega}(\bf{\Pi}^{1}_{1})
implies that
\mathsf{PSP}_{\omega}(\bf{\Pi}^{1}_{1})
holds and thus there is an inaccessible cardinal in
L

(see
[
MR1940513
, Theorem 25.38]
.)

4.3.
The uncountable case

In this section, we complete the proof of the open dihypergraph dichotomy for definable sets in the uncountable case (Theorem
1.4
for
\kappa>\omega
).
We that assume
\kappa
is uncountable
throughout the section.

4.3.a.
\kappa
-analytic sets

We begin with the special case of Theorem
1.4
for

\kappa
-analytic
sets.
The proof is much more direct than in the general case,
since it does not need the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
.
It adapts an argument from
[
SzThesis
]
*Section 3.1
for the open graph dichotomy
\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1}_{1}(\kappa))
.
We first give a proof
for closed sets.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

In
V[G]
, suppose
X
is a closed subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
. Then
\mathsf{ODD}^{H{\upharpoonright}X}_{\kappa}
holds.

Proof.

Recall our Assumptions
4.1.5
.
In particular, we assume that
H{\upharpoonright}X
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring. It suffices to show that
there is a continuous homomorphism
f:{}^{\kappa}d\to X
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
.

In
V[G]
, let
S
be a subtree of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
such that
[S]=X=X_{\varphi,a}
.
There is some
\alpha<\lambda
such that
S\in V[G_{\alpha}]
, since
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
has the
\lambda
-c.c.
Now let
\gamma>\alpha
and let
\tau
be an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-name as in Lemma
4.1.11
.

In
V[G_{\gamma}]
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}
forces that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}}\Vdash\check{\tau}\in[S]
by
Lemma
4.1.11

(1)
.
Since the formula “
x\in[S]
” is absolute between transitive models of
{\sf ZFC}
, we have

(4.3)

\mathbbm{1}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}\Vdash^{V[G_{\gamma}]}\tau\in[S].

In
V[G]
,
we construct functions

h:{}^{<\kappa}d\to\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)

and

t:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

with the following properties
for all
u,v\in{}^{<\kappa}d
:

(i)

If
u\subsetneq v
, then
t(u)\subsetneq t(v)
and
h(v)<h(u)
.

(ii)

h(u)\Vdash^{V[G_{\gamma}]}t(u)\subseteq\tau
.

(iii)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}N_{t({u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle})}\subseteq H.

Since the tree
T^{\tau,q}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}
is not
H
-independent for all
q\in\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)

by Lemma
4.1.11

(2)
,
there exists an
H
-witness function for
\tau
.
Thus, we may fix an
H
-witness function
w
and a step function
s
for
\tau
as in Lemma
4.1.14
.
Construct
h(v)
and
t(v)
by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(v)
.
Let
h(\emptyset):=\mathbbm{1}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}

and
t(\emptyset):=\emptyset
.
Now suppose that
v\in{}^{<\kappa}d
and

h(u),\,t(u)
have been constructed for all
u\subsetneq v
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(v)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
let
t(v):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq v}t(u)
.
Since

\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
is
{<}\kappa
-closed, there is some
h(v)<h(u)
for all
u\subsetneq v
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(v)\in\mathrm{Succ}
and
v=u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle
,
let
t(v):=w(h(u),\alpha)

and
h(v)<s(h(u),\alpha)
.

Now suppose that

h:{}^{<\kappa}d\to\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)

and

t:{}^{<\kappa}d\to S

have been constructed.
Note that
t
is strict order preserving by
(i)
.
Hence
f=[t]:{}^{\kappa}d\to X
is defined.
By
(iii)
,
t
is an order homomorphism for
({}^{\kappa}\kappa,H)
, so

f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
It thus suffices that

\operatorname{ran}(f)\subseteq[S]=X
.
This holds since
(
4.3
) and
(ii)
imply that

\operatorname{ran}(t)\subseteq S
.
∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

The following alternative version of the previous proof can be understood as a special case of our proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Let
S
,
\gamma
and
\tau
be as in the previous proof. Then

\mathbbm{1}_{\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)}\Vdash^{V[G_{\gamma}]}\tau\in[S]
.
Choose an
H
-witness function
w
and a step function
s
as in Subsection
4.1.c
.
A similar construction as in
the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\omega,{<}\lambda)
96
96

96

See the proof of Lemma
4.2.2
.

shows that there exists an
s
-function

h:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

such that
[h](x)
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
for all
x\in{}^{\kappa}d
.
97
97

97

This is a weaker version of the Nice Quotient Lemma
4.1.16
obtained by omitting
(2)
.

For
f:{}^{\kappa}d\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
with
f(x)=\tau^{[h](x)}
,
it follows that
\operatorname{ran}(f)\subseteq[S]
by the absoluteness of “
x\in[S]
”.
One can show that
f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
as in
Lemma
4.1.18
.
Thus,
f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}[S]=H{\upharpoonright}X
.

By the previous lemma, Theorem
1.4
holds for all closed subsets of the
\kappa
-Baire space.
Since every

\kappa
-analytic
subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is the continuous image of a closed subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,
we can extend this
to

\kappa
-analytic
sets by Lemma
3.1.5
.

Corollary 4.3.3
.

Theorem
1.4
holds for all

\kappa
-analytic
subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

4.3.b.
Definable sets

We prove the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
and thus Theorem
1.4
for uncountable
\kappa
.
The main difficulty is that quotient forcings are not well behaved in the uncountable setting:

Remark 4.3.4
.

Solovay’s Lemma
4.2.1
does not extend to uncountable regular cardinals
\kappa
by the following well-known counterexample.
Let
V[J*K]
be an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)*\dot{\mathbb{Q}}
-generic extension of
V
, where
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
adds a stationary set
S
and
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}
shoots a club through its complement.
Thus
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}^{J}
is not stationary set preserving in
V[J]
.
Hence
V[J*K]
is not a generic extension of
V[J]
by a
{<}\kappa
-closed forcing.
This is discussed in more detail in
[
SchlichtPSPgames
, Subsection 1.3]
.

Instead of Solovay’s lemma, we will use the following Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
Its proof is postponed to
the next subsection.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

(Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
)

Let
M
be a transitive class model of
{\sf ZFC}
.
98
98

98

I.e.,
M
can be a proper class or a set.

In
M
, suppose that
\mu
is an uncountable cardinal with
\mu^{<\mu}=\mu
,

2\leq\delta\leq\mu
and

s:\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)\times\delta\to\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
is a step function for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
For any
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic filter
K
over
M
,
there exists in
M[K]
an
s
-function

h:{}^{<\mu}\delta\to\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)

such that for all

x\in\operatorname{ran}([h])
:

(1)

x
is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic over
M
.

(2)

M[K]
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic extension of
M[x]
.

In fact, in
(1)
,
for all distinct
y,z\in{}^{\mu}\delta
,

[h](y)
and
[h](z)
are mutually
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic over
M
.

Lemma 4.3.6
.

(Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
)

The following holds in
V[G]
.
Suppose that
\gamma<\lambda
and

s
is a step-function for
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
Then there exists an
s
-function

h:{}^{<\kappa}d\to\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)

such that for all
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h])
:

(1)

x
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
.

(2)

V[G]
is a

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic
extension of
V[G_{\gamma}][x]
.

In fact in
(1)
, for all distinct
y,z\in{}^{\kappa}d
,

[h](y)
and
[h](z)
are mutually
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
.

Proof.

We rearrange
V[G]
as an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic extension of a model
M
.
Fix some
\alpha\in[\gamma,\lambda)
with
s\in V[G_{\alpha}]
by the
\lambda
-c.c.
Since
\mathbb{P}^{\alpha}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)\times\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
,
99
99

99

See Corollary
2.4.6
.

we write

V[G]=V[G_{\alpha}\times K\times J],

where is
K\times J
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)\times\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic filter over
V[G_{\alpha}]
.
Then
V[G]
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic extension of
M:=V[G_{\alpha}\times J]
.

Note that
s
is a step-function in
M
,
since this concept is absolute between transitive
{\sf ZFC}
-models with the same
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
Fix an
s
-function
h
in
V[G]

as in the previous lemma.
Then each
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h])
is
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
, since
V[G_{\gamma}]\subseteq M
.
It remains to see that
V[G]=M[K]
is a

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic extension of
V[G_{\gamma}][x]
.
Recall that
M[K]
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic extension of
M[x]
.
Moreover,

M[x]=V[G_{\alpha}\times J][x]=V[G_{\gamma}][x][G_{[\gamma,\alpha)}\times J]

is a
\mathbb{P}_{[\alpha,\gamma)}\times\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic extension of
V[G_{\gamma}][x]
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{[\alpha,\gamma)}\times\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
is equivalent to
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
,
100
100

100

See Corollary
2.4.6
.

this suffices.
∎

Remark 4.3.7
.

In the uncountable case only, the proof yields the following stronger version of Theorem
1.4
.
For a given
d
-dihypergraph
I
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,
let

\mathsf{ODDI}^{I}_{\kappa}
denote the statement:

\mathsf{ODDI}^{I}_{\kappa}
: Either
I
has a
\kappa
-coloring, or
there exists
an
injective
continuous homomorphism
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
.
101
101

101

See Definition
5.5.1
below.

We claim that
if
\kappa<\lambda
are regular uncountable cardinals and
2\leq d\leq\kappa
,
then the following hold in all
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-generic extensions
V[G]
:

(1)

\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
if
\lambda
is inacessible.
102
102

102

The definitions of

\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
and

\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

are analogous to the ones in Definition
1.3
.
Note that
(1)

\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
for all

by Lemma

.

(2)

\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
if
\lambda
is Mahlo.

To prove this, recall that after the Nice Quotient Lemma
4.1.16
,
we defined a continuous homomorphism
f
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
by letting
f(x):=\tau^{[h](x)}
, where
\gamma
and
\tau
are as in Lemma
4.1.11
.
In particular, note that
\tau
is a name in
V[G_{\gamma}]
for a new element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
It remains to show that
f
is injective. Let
x,y
be distinct elements of
{}^{\kappa}d
,
let
x^{\prime}:=[h](x)
and
y^{\prime}:=[h](y)
.
Then
f(x)=\tau^{x^{\prime}}\in V[G_{\gamma}][x^{\prime}]-V[G_{\gamma}]

and

f(y)=\tau^{y^{\prime}}\in V[G_{\gamma}][y^{\prime}]-V[G_{\gamma}]
.
Since

x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are mutually generic over
V[G_{\gamma}]
,
we have

V[G_{\gamma}][x^{\prime}]\cap V[G_{\gamma}][y^{\prime}]=V[G_{\gamma}]
.
Therefore

f(x)\neq f(y)
.

Theorem

and Corollary

below provide an alternative proof using a weak variant of the
\Diamond
principle.

Remark 4.3.8
.

Note that the Nice Quotient Lemmas
4.3.5
and
4.3.6
both fail in the countable case,

While Lemma

does hold without the last condition on mutual genericity by Lemma

, we do not know if this is the case for Lemma
4.3.5
.

4.3.c.
The Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)

This subsection is dedicated to the proof of Lemma
4.3.5
.
We make the same assumptions as in this lemma
throughout the subsection.
In particular,

M
is a transitive class model of
{\sf ZFC}
.
In
M
,
\mu
is an uncountable cardinal with
\mu^{<\mu}=\mu
,

2\leq\delta\leq\mu
and

s:\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)\times\delta\to\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)

is a step function for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
103
103

103

See Definition
4.1.13

Our proof uses ideas from
[
SchlichtPSPgames
]
.
We first give a short description of the proof.
Working in
M
, we define a forcing
\mathbb{Q}
whose domain contains

partial functions of size
{<}\mu
approximating
an
s
-function.
104
104

104

See Definition
4.1.15
.

We first prove that

\mathbb{Q}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
We then show that
if
h
is the
s
-function added in the natural way by a given
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter,
then
h
satisfies the requirements in Lemma
4.3.5
.
A significant portion of the arguments in this subsection are aimed at proving the second requirement that elements of
\operatorname{ran}([h])
have well-behaved quotients.

We now present the detailed proof.
We argue either in
M
or in a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic extension of
M
throughout this subsection.

Recall that an
s
-function for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
is a strict order preserving
map
h:{}^{<\mu}\delta\to{}^{<\mu}\mu
105
105

105

Note that
{}^{<\mu}\mu
is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
with the ordering reversed.

which is “built along”
s
in
the sense of Definition
4.1.15
.
In
(a)
of the next definition, we consider partial functions
with the same property.
Our forcing
\mathbb{Q}
will consist of
such partial
s
-functions of size
{<}\mu

with the additional technical requirement of strictness defined in
(b)

that is used to ensure
\mathbb{Q}
is separative.

Definition 4.3.9
.

(a)

A
partial
s
-function
is a
strict order preserving
partial function

p:{}^{<\mu}\delta\rightharpoonup{}^{<\mu}\mu

such that

\operatorname{dom}(p)
is a subtree of
{}^{<\mu}\delta
, and
if
u,u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
, then

p\big(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle\big)\supsetneq s\big(p(u),\alpha\big).

(b)

A partial
s
-function
p
is
strict

if for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
with
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
we have

p(t)\supsetneq\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{u\subsetneq t}p(u).

We now define the forcing
\mathbb{Q}
that will be used in our proof of the Nice Quotient
Lemma
4.3.5
for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
It is embedded into a poset
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
that will be useful as well.

Definition 4.3.10
.

(a)

\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
consists of all strict partial
s
-functions.

(b)

\mathbb{Q}
consists of all
q\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
with
\left|\operatorname{dom}(q)\right|<\mu
.

(c)

We equip both
\mathbb{Q}
and
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
with the partial order
\leq
defined by letting

p\leq q

if the following conditions hold:

(i)

\operatorname{dom}(q)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(p)
.

(ii)

q(t)=p(t)
for every non-terminal node
t
of
\operatorname{dom}(q)
.

(iii)

q(t)\subseteq p(t)
for every terminal node
t
of
\operatorname{dom}(q)
.

Thus, a condition
q
is extended by moving its topmost values upwards and end-extending the tree
\operatorname{dom}(q)
.
Note that the definition of
\mathbb{Q}
is absolute to transitive models

N\supseteq M
of
{\sf ZFC}
with
({}^{<\mu}\mu)^{M}=({}^{<\mu}\mu)^{N}
.
Specifically, since
\mathbb{Q}
is
{<}\mu
-closed by Lemma
4.3.16
below, the definition of
\mathbb{Q}
is absolute between
M
and
\mathbb{Q}
-generic extensions of
M
.
However,

\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{N}
may contain some
total
s
-functions

h:{}^{<\mu}\delta\to{}^{<\mu}\mu

that are not in
M
.

We now discuss some properties of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
which will be useful in this subsection.
The next lemma
gives an equivalent reformulation
of the definition of strict partial
s
-functions.

Lemma 4.3.11
.

Let
q:{}^{<\mu}\delta\rightharpoonup{}^{<\mu}\mu
be a

partial function whose domain is a subtree of
{}^{<\mu}\delta
.
Then

q
is a strict partial
s
-function (i.e.,
q\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
)
if and only if
for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(q)
,

(4.4)

q(t)\supsetneq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big).

Proof.

First suppose that

q
is a strict partial
s
-function
and
t\in\operatorname{dom}(q)
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
, then

q(t)\supsetneq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}q(t{\upharpoonright}(\alpha+1))\supseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big).

The inclusion on the left
holds since
q
is strict, while the
inclusion on the right holds
since
q
is a partial
s
-function.
Now assume

\operatorname{lh}(t)\in\mathrm{Succ}
and
t=u{}^{\frown}\langle\beta\rangle
.
Since
s
is a step function
and

q
is a partial
s
-function,
we have

\begin{gathered}q(u)\subseteq s(q(u),\beta)\subsetneq q(t),\\[4.0pt]
s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big)\subsetneq q(t{\upharpoonright}(\alpha+1))\subseteq q(u)\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\end{gathered}

for all
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(u)
.
Therefore

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big)=s(q(u),\beta)\subsetneq q(t)
.

Conversely, (
4.4
) implies immediately that
q
is a strict partial
s
-function.
In particular,
q
is strict order preserving since
s
is a step-function.
∎

Recall that
K\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
is a
downward directed
subset of the poset
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}

if for every
p,q\in K
, there exists
r\in K
with
r\leq p,q
.
In particular, any
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter
K
over
M
is a downward directed subset
of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{M[K]}
.
We next define
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
for any downward directed
subset
K
of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. This will turn out to equal the infimum in relevant cases.

Definition 4.3.12
.

Suppose
K
is a downward directed subset of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
denote the partial function from
{}^{<\mu}\delta
to
{}^{\leq\mu}\mu
defined as follows:

\begin{gathered}\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\big):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\operatorname{dom}(q):q\in K\},\\[4.0pt]
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{q(t):{q\in K,\,t\in\operatorname{dom}(q)}\}.\end{gathered}

Note that the definition of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K

is absolute between transitive models
N\subseteq N^{\prime}
of
{\sf ZFC}
with
K\in N
.
The next lemma shows that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
is indeed the infimum of
K
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
in all relevant cases.

Lemma 4.3.13
.

Let
K
be a downward directed subset of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(1)

If
q\in K
and
t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(q)
, then

(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)=q(t)
.

(2)

Suppose that for any
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)
, there is some
p\in K
such that
t
is a non-terminal node in
\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
Then
\operatorname{ran}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)\subseteq{}^{<\mu}\mu
.

(3)

If
\operatorname{ran}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)\subseteq{}^{<\mu}\mu
, then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
, and

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K

is the infimum of
K
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(4)

If
K
is a downward directed subset of
\mathbb{Q}
of size
{<}\mu
, then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
is the infimum of
K
in
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
,
note that
q(t)\subseteq(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)
.
Conversely, if
p\in K
and
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
, then there exists
r\in K
such that
r\leq p,q
and therefore

p(t)\subseteq r(t)=q(t)
.
Hence
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)\subseteq q(t).

For
(2)
, let
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)
and
take
p\in K
such that
t
is a non-terminal node in
\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
Then
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)=p(t)\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
by
(1)
.

For
(3)
,
we first claim that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Since
\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)
is a subtree of
{}^{<\mu}\delta
,
it suffices to show (
4.4
) for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K

by the previous lemma.
Let
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)
and take
p\in K
with
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
Then
t{\upharpoonright}\alpha
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(p)
for all
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)
,
so
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha)=p(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha)
by
(1)
.
Since (
4.4
) holds for
p
,

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big((\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(p(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big)\subsetneq p(t)\subseteq(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t).

We now claim that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
is the infimum of
K
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
We first show that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\leq p
for any
p\in K
.
By definition,
\operatorname{dom}(p)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)

and
p(t)\subseteq(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)
for any
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
Moreover, if
t
is a non-terminal in
\operatorname{dom}(p)
, then
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)=p(t)
by
(1)
.
Hence
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K\leq p
.
Now, suppose
h\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and
h\leq p
for all
p\in K
. It suffices to show

h\leq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
.
By definition,
\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(h)
and
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)\subseteq h(t)
for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)
.
Moreover, if
t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)
, then there exists
p\in K
such that
t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
Hence

(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)(t)=p(t)=h(t)
by

(1)
and
h\leq p
.
Therefore
h\leq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
.

For
(4)
, note that
|\operatorname{dom}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)|<\mu
and
\operatorname{ran}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K)\subseteq{}^{<\mu}\mu
.
Thus the claim follows from
(3)
since
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
∎

Remark 4.3.14
.

There is no general relationship between
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
and the infimum of
K
in the Boolean completion
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
of
\mathbb{Q}
.
However, they are equal if
K
is a downward directed subset of
\mathbb{Q}
of size
{<}\mu

using Lemma
4.3.13

(4)

and the fact that
\mathbb{Q}
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
.
106
106

106

\mathbb{Q}
can be assumed to be a dense subset of
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
,
since we will prove in Lemma
4.3.28
below that
\mathbb{Q}
is separative.

Our first goal is to show that the forcing
\mathbb{Q}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
.
This is done in Lemma
4.3.16
.
Before proving this,
we characterize
when two given elements of
\mathbb{Q}
are compatible, in the next lemma.
This will be used to prove that
\mathbb{Q}
is
non-atomic
107
107

107

See the proof Lemma
4.3.16
.

and separative.
108
108

108

See Lemma
4.3.28
.

Lemma 4.3.15
.

Two elements
p
and
q
of
\mathbb{Q}
are compatible if and only if
the following hold for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)
:

(1)

p(t)\parallel q(t)
.
109
109

109

I.e.,
p(t)\subseteq q(t)
or
q(t)\subseteq p(t)
.

(2)

If
t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(p)
, then

q(t)\subseteq p(t)
.

(3)

If
t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(q)
, then

p(t)\subseteq q(t)
.

In particular, if
p
and
q
are compatible, then

p(t)=q(t)

whenever
t
is non-terminal in both
\operatorname{dom}(p)
and
\operatorname{dom}(q)
.

Proof.

First, assume
p
and
q
are compatible
and
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)
.
Let
r\in\mathbb{Q}
be a common extension of
p
and
q
.
Then

r(t)\supseteq p(t)
and
r(t)\supseteq q(t)
, and hence
p(t)\parallel q(t)
.
If

t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(p)
,
then
q(t)\subseteq r(t)=p(t)
.
Similarly,
if
t
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(q)
,
then
p(t)\subseteq r(t)=q(t)
.

Now assume
(1)
–
(3)
hold for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)
.
Let
r
be the unique function with
\operatorname{dom}(r)=\operatorname{dom}(p)\cup\operatorname{dom}(q)
and
r(t)=p(t)\cup q(t)
for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
.
Then
r(t)=\max\{p(t),q(t)\}

for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)

by
(1)
.
Moreover,

r(t)=p(t)
for all
non-terminal nodes
t
of
\operatorname{dom}(p)

by
(2)
,
and

r(t)=q(t)
for all
non-terminal nodes
t
of
\operatorname{dom}(q)

by
(3)
.
Using these observations, it is easy to check that
r\in\mathbb{Q}
and
that
r\leq p,q
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.16
.

\mathbb{Q}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\mathbb{Q}
is a non-atomic
{<}\mu
-closed forcing of size
\mu

by Lemma
2.4.4
.
The assumption
\mu^{<\mu}=\mu
implies that
|\mathbb{Q}|=\mu
.
To show that
\mathbb{Q}
is
{<}\mu
-closed, suppose that
\langle p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\xi\rangle
is a descending sequence of elements of
\mathbb{Q}
of length
\xi<\mu
. Then

p=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\{p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\xi\}
is an element of
\mathbb{Q}
such that
p\leq p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\xi
by Lemma
4.3.13

(4)
.
To show that
\mathbb{Q}
is non-atomic, let
p\in\mathbb{Q}
. Take a minimal element
t
of
{{}^{<\mu}\delta}-\operatorname{dom}(p)
, let

u=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big),

and choose
u_{0},u_{1}\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
with
u_{0}\perp u_{1}
and
u_{0},u_{1}\supsetneq u
.
Finally, let
q_{i}:=p\cup\{(t,u_{i})\}
for
i=0,1
.
Then
q_{0},q_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}
by Lemma
4.3.11
.
It is clear that

q_{0},q_{1}\leq p
, and

q_{0}\perp q_{1}
holds by
the previous lemma.
∎

Throughout the rest of this subsection, we use the following notation.

•

Suppose
K
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter over
M
.
In
M[K]
, let

h_{K}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K.

•

In
M
, let
\dot{K}
denote the canonical name for a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter,
and let
\dot{h}
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-name for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\dot{K}

in the sense that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\>\dot{h}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\dot{K}.

h_{K}
is well defined
since
K
is a filter on
\mathbb{Q}
and hence an upward directed subset of

\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{M[K]}
.
In the rest of this subsection, we will show that

h_{K}
is an
s
-function which
satisfies the conclusion of the Nice Quotient Lemma
4.3.5
for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
We first prove
that
h_{K}
is indeed an
s
-function.
We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3.17
.

If
t\in{}^{<\mu}\delta
, then

D_{t}:=\{r\in\mathbb{Q}:t\in\operatorname{dom}(r)\}

is a dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
\operatorname{lh}(t)
.
Assume

D_{t{\upharpoonright}\alpha}
is dense in
\mathbb{Q}

for all
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)
.
To show that
D_{t}
is dense in
\mathbb{Q}
, let
p\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Using the fact that
\mathbb{Q}
is
{<}\mu
-closed, construct a decreasing sequence

\langle q_{\alpha}:\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)\rangle
of conditions below
p

such that

q_{\alpha}\in D_{t{\upharpoonright}\alpha}
, and let

q:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\{q_{\alpha}:\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)\}.

Then
q\in\mathbb{Q}

and
q\leq p

by Lemma
4.3.13

(4)
, and

t{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in\operatorname{dom}(q)
for all
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)
.
We define a condition

r\in D_{t}
below
q

as follows.
If
t\in\operatorname{dom}(q)
, then let
r:=q
.
If
t\notin\operatorname{dom}(q)
, fix
u\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
with

u\supsetneq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big)

and let
r:=q\cup\{(t,u)\}
.
Then
r\in\mathbb{Q}
by Lemma
4.3.11
,
and
it is clear that
t\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
and
r\leq q
.
Thus,

r
is an element of
D_{t}
below
p
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.18
.

Suppose that
K
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter over
M
. Then in
M[K]
:

(1)

\operatorname{dom}(h_{K})={}^{<\mu}\delta
.

(2)

h_{K}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and
h_{K}
is the infimum of
K
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(3)

h_{K}
is an
s
-function.

Proof.

(1)
follows from the previous lemma.
For
(2)
, it suffices to show
\operatorname{ran}(h_{K})\subseteq{}^{<\mu}\mu
by Lemma
4.3.13

(3)
.
To see this, note that
\operatorname{dom}(h_{K})={}^{<\mu}\delta=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{p\in K}\operatorname{dom}(p)
by
(1)
and the definition of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}
.
Thus,
h_{K}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
by Lemma
4.3.13

(2)
, as required.

(3)
follows immediately from
(1)
and
(2)
.
∎

The following lemma shows that any
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter

K
is definable from
h_{K}
.
Hence

M[K]=M[h_{K}]
.

Lemma 4.3.19
.

For all
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filters
K
over
M

and all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,

q\in K\ \Longleftrightarrow\ h_{K}\leq q.

Proof.

If
q\in K
, then
h_{K}\leq q

by the previous corollary.
Conversely, suppose
h_{K}\leq q
.
Since
K
is
\mathbb{Q}
-generic, it suffices to show that all elements
p
of
K
are compatible with
q
in
\mathbb{Q}
.
Given
p\in K
,
let
r:=h_{K}{\upharpoonright}(\operatorname{dom}(p)\cup\operatorname{dom}(q))
.
Then
r\in\mathbb{Q}
since

h_{K}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{M[K]}
and
|\operatorname{dom}(p)\cup\operatorname{dom}(q)|<\mu
.
Moreover,
r\leq p,\,q

since
h_{K}\leq p,\,q
.
∎

In the remainder of the subsection, we will prove that
h_{K}
satisfies the conclusion of the Nice Quotient Lemma
4.3.5
for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
,
i.e., that
for all
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h_{K}])
,

(1)

x
is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic over
M
,
and

(2)

M[K]
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic extension of
M[x]
.

In order to show
(2)
