, we will later calculate the quotient forcing for a given element of
\operatorname{ran}([h_{K}])
.
When doing so, it will be more convenient to work with certain dense subforcings
\mathbb{Q}^{*}
of
\mathbb{Q}
.
These will be
the intersections of
{<}\mu
many subforcings
of the form
\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)
given in the next definition.
Regarding notation,

0pt(A)
,
\operatorname{lh}(A)
were defined in Subsection
2.3
,
and
\vartheta_{(p)},\,\vartheta_{[p]}
in Definition
2.4.13
.

Definition 4.3.20
.

Let
\vartheta
and
\sigma
be
\mathbb{Q}
-names such that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\vartheta\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge[\dot{h}](\vartheta)=\sigma
.
Then
\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)
denotes the subforcing of
\mathbb{Q}
which consists of all conditions
p\in\mathbb{Q}
such that the following hold:
110
110

110

Note that
we can equivalently
replace
\vartheta_{(p)}
by
\vartheta_{[p]}

and
\sigma_{(p)}
by
\sigma_{[p]}

everywhere in this definition,
by Lemma
2.4.14
.

(a)

0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
and

0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\vartheta_{(p)})
.

(b)

\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
and
\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})
.

(c)

\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\gamma\in\operatorname{dom}(p)

for all
\gamma<0pt(\operatorname{dom}(p))
.

Note that if
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)
, then
we get the equality
\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)=\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})

in
(b)
,
by Lemma
4.3.24
below.

We next show that
\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)
is a dense subforcing of
\mathbb{Q}
and
more generally, that
the intersection
\mathbb{Q}^{*}
of
{<}\mu
many subforcings of this form is also dense in
\mathbb{Q}
.
We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3.21
.

Suppose
\langle p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\xi\rangle
is a strictly decreasing sequence of elements of
\mathbb{Q}
of length
\xi<\mu
.
Let

p:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\{p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\xi\}.

Then
0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{\alpha})

and

\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

The first part of the conclusion holds since

\operatorname{dom}(p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{dom}(p_{\alpha})
by definition.
Moreover,

\operatorname{lh}(p(t))=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\operatorname{lh}(p_{\alpha}(t)):\>\alpha<\xi,\,t\in\operatorname{dom}(p_{\alpha})\}
for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
by definition.
Therefore

\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\operatorname{lh}(p(t)):t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\operatorname{lh}(p_{\alpha}(t)):\>\alpha<\xi,\,t\in\operatorname{dom}(p_{\alpha})\}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{\alpha})
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.22
.

Suppose that
\xi<\mu
.
For all
i<\xi
,
let
\vartheta_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}

denote
\mathbb{Q}
-names such that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\vartheta_{i}\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge\sigma_{i}=[\dot{h}](\vartheta_{i})
,
and let

\mathbb{Q}^{*}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{i<\xi}\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta_{i},\sigma_{i}).

Then

\mathbb{Q}^{*}

is a dense subforcing of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. Construct by recursion a strictly decreasing sequence

\langle p_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
of conditions in
\mathbb{Q}
as follows.
Let
p_{0}:=q
.
If
n<\omega
and
p_{n}
has been defined, use Lemma
4.3.17

and the fact that

\mathbb{Q}
is
{<}\mu
-closed to choose
p_{n+1}
such that

(i)

p_{n+1}
decides

\vartheta_{i}{\upharpoonright}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{n})
and
\sigma_{i}{\upharpoonright}\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{n})

for all
i<\xi
,

(ii)

(\vartheta_{i})_{[p_{n}]}\in\operatorname{dom}(p_{n+1})
for all
i<\xi
,

(iii)

0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{n+1})>0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{n})
, and

(iv)

\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{n+1})>\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{n})
.

Let
p:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\{p_{n}:n<\omega\}
.
Then
p\in\mathbb{Q}
and
p\leq q
by Lemma
4.3.13

(4)
.
To prove that
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
,
let
i<\xi
.
We show that
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta_{i},\sigma_{i})
.
The fact that

0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
and
\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}

follows from
(iii)
,
(iv)
and Lemma
4.3.21
.
By
(i)
,

0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{n})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}{(\vartheta_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}}

and

\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{n})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}{(\sigma_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}}

for all
n<\omega
.
From this and Lemma
4.3.21
, we obtain:

\begin{gathered}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{n<\omega}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{n})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{n<\omega}\operatorname{dom}{(\vartheta_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}{(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}},\\
\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{n<\omega}\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{n})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{n<\omega}\operatorname{dom}{(\sigma_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}}\subseteq\operatorname{dom}{(\sigma_{i})_{(p)}}.\end{gathered}

Thus,
Definition
4.3.20

(a)
and
(b)

hold for

\vartheta_{i},\,\sigma_{i}
and
p
.
To show that

(c)
also holds, let

\gamma<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
and take
n<\omega
with

\gamma\leq 0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{n})
.
Then by
(i)
,

\gamma\subseteq\operatorname{dom}{(\vartheta_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}}

or equivalently,
\gamma\leq\operatorname{lh}{\left((\vartheta_{i})_{[p_{n+1}]}\right)}
.
Let

t=(\vartheta_{i})_{[p_{n+1}]}{\upharpoonright}\gamma=(\vartheta_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}{\upharpoonright}\gamma.

Then

t\in\operatorname{dom}(p_{n+2})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(p)

by
(ii)
.
Moreover,

t\subseteq(\vartheta_{i})_{(p_{n+1})}\subseteq(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}.

Therefore
(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\gamma=t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
, as required.
∎

The next lemma
is a stronger form of the following observation:
Let
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)
, where
\vartheta
and
\sigma
are
\mathbb{Q}
-names
such that
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\vartheta\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge[\dot{h}](\vartheta)=\sigma
.
Let
t
be the least initial segment of
\vartheta_{[p]}
with
t\notin\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
111
111

111

t
exists since

\vartheta_{[p]}\notin\operatorname{dom}(p)

if
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)

by Definition
4.3.20

(a)

and Lemma
2.4.14
.
For instance, if
\vartheta=\check{x}
for some
x
in
({}^{\mu}\mu)^{M}
, then
\vartheta_{[p]}=x
.

Then

t=\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
.
Furthermore, let

v:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{p(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha):{\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)}\big\}.

For any choice of
u
with
v\subsetneq u
,

r:=p\cup\{(t,u)\}

remains a strict partial
s
-function (i.e.,
r\in\mathbb{Q}
)
and moreover,

\sigma_{(r)}\supseteq u
.
Note that

\sigma_{(p)}=v
by Lemma
4.3.24
below.
We will need the next lemma
for
{<}\mu
many pairs of names
(\vartheta_{i},\sigma_{i})
.

Lemma 4.3.23
.

Using the same assumptions as in Lemma
4.3.22
,
suppose that
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.
Let
t_{i}:=(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
for all
i<\xi
.

(1)

t_{i}
is the least initial segment of
(\vartheta_{i})_{[p]}
with
t_{i}\notin\operatorname{dom}(p)
for all
i<\xi
.

(2)

If
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
,
u_{i}\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
and

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{p(t_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha):{\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)}\big\}\subsetneq u_{i}

for all
i<j<\xi

and

r:=p\cup\{(t_{i},u_{i}):i<\xi\},

then
r\in\mathbb{Q}
,

r\leq p
and

r\Vdash(t_{i}\subseteq\vartheta_{i}\wedge u_{i}\subseteq\sigma_{i})

for all
i<\xi
.
112
112

112

In fact,
p\Vdash t_{i}\subseteq\vartheta_{i}
since
t_{i}\subseteq(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
,
note that

t_{i}\in{}^{<\mu}\delta

since

p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta_{i},\sigma_{i})
,
and in particular, since

0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}\big({(\vartheta_{i})}_{(p)}\big)

by definition.
The latter also implies
t_{i}\subseteq{(\vartheta_{i})}_{[p]}
by Lemma
2.4.14
.
It thus suffices that
t_{i}{\upharpoonright}\gamma\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
for all
\gamma<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
, but this follows from
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta_{i},\sigma_{i})
by definition.

For
(2)
, we first show
r\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Since
\xi<\mu
and

t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for all
i<j<\xi
,

r
is a partial function from
{}^{<\mu}\delta
to
{}^{<\mu}\mu

with
|\operatorname{dom}(r)|<\mu
.

\operatorname{dom}(r)
is a subtree of
{}^{<\mu}\delta

by
(1)
.
Moreover,
r
is a strict partial
s
-function by Lemma
4.3.11
and
our assumption about the values
u_{i}=r(t_{i})
.
Therefore

r\in\mathbb{Q}
, as required.

It is clear that
r\leq p
.
If
i<\xi
, then

r
forces
t_{i}\subseteq\vartheta_{i}

since
t_{i}\subseteq(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}\subseteq(\vartheta_{i})_{(r)}
.
Therefore

r\Vdash\dot{h}(t_{i})\subseteq[\dot{h}](\vartheta_{i})=\sigma_{i}
.
Since
r\Vdash\dot{h}\leq r
by Corollary
4.3.18

(2)
,

r
also forces that
u_{i}\subseteq\dot{h}(t_{i})
. Therefore

r\Vdash u_{i}\subseteq\sigma_{i}
.
∎

The next lemma states that for
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
,
\sigma_{(p)}
can be calculated from
p
and
\vartheta
.

Lemma 4.3.24
.

If
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)

where

\vartheta
and
\sigma
are
\mathbb{Q}
-names such that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\vartheta\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge\sigma=[\dot{h}](\vartheta)
,
then

\sigma_{(p)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{p(\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha):\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\big\}.

In particular,
\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})=\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)
.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
,
and let

v:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{p(\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha):\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\big\}.

We first show
v=\sigma_{(p)}
.
Since

p\Vdash\dot{h}\leq p

by Lemma
4.3.19
and

p\Vdash\vartheta_{(p)}\subseteq\vartheta
,

p\Vdash p(\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\subseteq\dot{h}(\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\subseteq[\dot{h}](\vartheta)=\sigma.

for all
\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
.
Thus

p\Vdash v\subseteq\sigma
, so
v\subseteq\sigma_{(p)}
.
Suppose
v\subsetneq\sigma_{(p)}
.
Take
u\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
with
v\subsetneq u

and
u\perp\sigma_{(p)}
.
Let

t:=(\vartheta_{(p)}){\upharpoonright}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)

and
r:=p\cup\{(t,u)\}.

Then
r\in\mathbb{Q}
and

r\Vdash u\subseteq\sigma

by Lemma
4.3.23
.
However,
r\leq p
and therefore
r\Vdash\sigma_{(p)}\subseteq\sigma
, a contradiction.

To show the second part of the conclusion,
observe that

\sigma_{(p)}=v
implies

\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})=\operatorname{lh}(v)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\operatorname{lh}(p(\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha):\>\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\}\subseteq\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p).

Conversely,

\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})

follows from our assumption that
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.
∎

The next lemma will be used to prove the
{<}\mu
-closure of the quotient forcings for elements of
\operatorname{ran}([h_{K}])
.

Lemma 4.3.25
.

Suppose that

\mathbb{Q}^{*}=\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma),

where

\vartheta
and
\sigma
are
\mathbb{Q}
-names such that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\vartheta\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge\sigma=[\dot{h}](\vartheta)
.
If

\langle p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\xi\rangle
is a strictly decreasing chain of elements of
\mathbb{Q}^{*}
of length
\xi<\mu
and

p:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\{p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\xi\},

then
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
and
\sigma_{(p)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\sigma_{(p_{\alpha})}
.

Proof.

We first show that
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.
Since
|\operatorname{dom}(p)|<\mu
,
we have
p\in\mathbb{Q}
by Lemma
4.3.13
.
The fact that
0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
and
\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)
are limit ordinals follows
from Lemma
4.3.21
in the case that
\xi\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
and
from the fact that
p_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
in the case that
\xi=\alpha+1
.

Moreover,

\begin{gathered}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{\alpha})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{dom}(\vartheta_{(p_{\alpha})})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\vartheta_{(p)}),\\
\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{\alpha})\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p_{\alpha})})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})\end{gathered}

by
Lemma
4.3.21

and since

p_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
and
p\leq p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<\xi
.
Thus, Definition
4.3.20

(a)
and
(b)
hold.
To show

(c)
, let
\gamma<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
.
Then
\gamma<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p_{\alpha})
for some
\alpha<\xi
.
Since

\vartheta_{(p_{\alpha})}\subseteq\vartheta_{(p)}
,
we have

\vartheta_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}\gamma=\vartheta_{(p_{\alpha})}{\upharpoonright}\gamma\in\operatorname{dom}(p_{\alpha})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(p).

To show the second part of the conclusion, let
v:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\sigma_{(p_{\alpha})}
.
Then
v\subseteq\sigma_{(p)}
.
Moreover,

\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p)})=\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{lh}(\operatorname{ran}p_{\alpha})=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\xi}\operatorname{dom}(\sigma_{(p_{\alpha})})=\operatorname{dom}v

by the previous lemma
and Lemma
4.3.21
.
Hence
\sigma_{(p)}=v
.
∎

The next lemma proves the first part of the Nice Quotient Lemma
4.3.5
for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.

Lemma 4.3.26
.

Suppose
K
is
\mathbb{Q}
-generic over
M
and

\langle x_{i}:i<\xi\rangle
is a sequence of distinct
elements of
({}^{\mu}\delta)^{M[K]}
of length
\xi<\mu
.
Then

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<\xi}[h_{K}](x_{i})

is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,\xi)
-generic over
M
.
113
113

113

[h_{K}](x_{i})
is identified with the filter of its initial segments in
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.

Proof.

Let
i<\xi
.
By basic facts about forcing,
there exist
\mathbb{Q}
-names
\vartheta_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}
such that

(\vartheta_{i})^{K}=x_{i}

and

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Vdash\vartheta_{i}\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge[\dot{h}](\vartheta_{i})=\sigma_{i}
.
In more detail, let
\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-name such that
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{i})^{K}=x_{i}
and let

\displaystyle\vartheta_{i}

\displaystyle:=\big\{\big((\alpha,\beta),q\big):\>(\alpha,\beta)\in\mu\times\delta,\ \,q\Vdash\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge(\alpha,\beta)\in\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}\big\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}

\displaystyle:=\big\{\big((\alpha,\beta),q\big):\>(\alpha,\beta)\in\mu\times\mu,\ \,q\Vdash(\alpha,\beta)\in[\dot{h}](\vartheta_{i})\big\}.

Since
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
for all
i<j<\xi
, there exists an ordinal
\alpha<\mu
and a condition
q\in K
such that

q\Vdash\vartheta_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha\neq\vartheta_{j}{\upharpoonright}\alpha

for all
i<j<\xi
.
We may also assume
0pt(\operatorname{dom}q)\geq\alpha
, since

\{p\in\mathbb{Q}:0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)\geq\alpha\}
is dense in
\mathbb{Q}
by Lemma
4.3.17
.

Claim
.

If
D
is a dense subset of
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,\xi)
, then

D^{\prime}:=\big\{r\in\mathbb{Q}\>:\>{\exists u\in D}\ \ {\forall i<\xi}\ \ {r\Vdash u(i)\subseteq\sigma_{i}}\big\}

is dense below
q
.

Proof.

Suppose
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
q^{\prime}\leq q
.
Take
p\leq q^{\prime}
with
p\in\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}{\bigcap}}_{i<\xi}\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta_{i},\sigma_{i})
by Lemma
4.3.22
.
For all
i<\xi
, choose
t_{i}\in{}^{<\mu}\delta
and
u_{i}\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
as in Lemma
4.3.23
. That is, let

t_{i}:=(\vartheta_{i})_{(p)}{\upharpoonright}0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)
and
choose
u_{i}
so that

u_{i}\supsetneq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{p(t_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\>:\>{\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}p)}\big\}.

In addition, we may assume
\langle u_{i}:i<\xi\rangle\in D
.
Since
p\leq q
, we have
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for all
i<j<\xi
.
Thus
r:=p\cup\{(t_{i},u_{i}):i<\xi\}
is a condition in
\mathbb{Q}
below
p
which forces
u_{i}\subseteq\sigma_{i}
for all
i<\xi
by Lemma
4.3.23
.
Hence
r\leq q^{\prime}
and
r\in D^{\prime}
as required.
∎

To see that
G^{\prime}:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<\xi}[h_{K}](x_{i})
is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,\xi)
-generic,
let
D
be a dense subset of
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,\xi)
.
Then
D^{\prime}\cap K\neq\emptyset

by the previous claim.
Hence there exists
u\in D

with
u(i)\subseteq(\sigma_{i})^{K}=[h_{K}](x_{i})

for all
i<\xi
, i.e., with

u\in G^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
4.3.26
.
∎

Remark 4.3.27
.

The previous lemma
shows that the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
holds with the following stronger
\xi
-dimensional variant
of
(1)
:

If
\langle x_{i}:i<\xi\rangle
is a sequence of distinct
elements of
({}^{\mu}\delta)^{M[K]}
of length
\xi<\mu
,
then

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<\xi}[h_{K}](x_{i})

is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,\xi)
-generic over
M
.

In the rest of this subsection, we will prove the second part of the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
We need to show that
the quotient forcing
in a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic extension
M[K]
of
M

for an element
\sigma^{K}

of
\operatorname{ran}([h_{K}])

is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.
The precise statement is given in Lemma
4.3.34
below.

For this purpose, it will be more convenient to work
with the Boolean completion of
\mathbb{Q}
.
We therefore use the next lemma.

Lemma 4.3.28
.

\mathbb{Q}
is separative.

Proof.

Suppose
p,q\in\mathbb{Q}
and
p\not\leq q
.
We aim to find
r\in\mathbb{Q}
with
r\leq p
and
r\perp q
.
We may assume
p\parallel q
.
First, suppose
q(t)\not\subseteq p(t)

for some
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)
.
Then
p(t)\subsetneq q(t)
and
t
is a terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(p)
by Lemma
4.3.15

(1)
and
(2)
.
Take
v\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
with
p(t)\subsetneq v
and
v\perp q(t)
.
Let
r
be the element of
\mathbb{Q}

obtained from
p
by extending the value of
t
to
v
.
That is,
let
\operatorname{dom}(r):=\operatorname{dom}(p)
, and for all
u\in\operatorname{dom}(r)
, let

r(u):=\begin{cases}v&\text{ if $u=t$,}\\
p(u)&\text{ if $u\neq t$}.\end{cases}

Then

r<p
, and
r\perp q
by Lemma
4.3.15

(1)
.

Now, suppose
q(t)\subseteq p(t)
for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)
.
Then
q(t)=p(t)

for all
t\in\operatorname{dom}(p)\cap\operatorname{dom}(q)
such that
t
is non-terminal in
\operatorname{dom}(q)

by Lemma
4.3.15

(3)
.
Thus, since
p\not\leq q
, we must have
\operatorname{dom}(q)\not\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(p)
.
Take
t\in\operatorname{dom}(q)
of minimal length with
t\notin\operatorname{dom}(p)
, and let

u:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(p(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)}s\big(q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha),t(\alpha)\big).

The second equality holds since
for all
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)
,

t{\upharpoonright}\alpha
is a non-terminal node of
\operatorname{dom}(q)
with
t{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in\operatorname{dom}(p)
,
and hence
p(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha)=q(t{\upharpoonright}\alpha)
.
Then
u\subsetneq q(t)
holds by Lemma
4.3.11
since
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Take
v\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
with
u\subsetneq v
and
v\perp q(t)
,
and let
r:=p\cup\{(t,v)\}
.
Then
r\in\mathbb{Q}
by Lemma
4.3.11
, and clearly
r<p
.
We further have
r\perp q
by Lemma
4.3.15

(1)
.
∎

Recall
that
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
denotes the Boolean completion of
\mathbb{Q}
. We may assume that

\mathbb{Q}
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
, since
\mathbb{Q}
is separative.
Write
\mathbb{B}:=\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{Q})
.
Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}
denote the infimum and supremum in
\mathbb{B}
.
114
114

114

Recall that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}K
agrees with
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}K
for downward directed subsets
K
of
\mathbb{Q}
of size
{<}\mu
by Remark
4.3.14
.

Write

\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket:=\llbracket{\varphi}\rrbracket_{\mathbb{B}}
for all formulas
\varphi
in the forcing language.
Assume
\vartheta
and
\sigma
denote
\mathbb{B}
-names such that

\llbracket{\vartheta\in{}^{\mu}\delta\wedge[\dot{h}](\vartheta)=\sigma}\rrbracket=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{B}}
, and let
\mathbb{Q}^{*}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}(\vartheta,\sigma)
.
Note that
\mathbb{Q}^{*}
is a dense subforcing of
\mathbb{B}
by Lemma
4.3.22
.

Lemma 4.3.29
.

For all
u,v\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
:

(1)

If
u\subseteq v
, then
\llbracket{u\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket\geq\llbracket{v\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket
.

(2)

If
\llbracket{u\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket>\llbracket{v\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket
, then
u\subsetneq v
.

Proof.

(1)
is immediate.
For

(2)
, suppose
\llbracket{u\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket>\llbracket{v\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket
.
Then
u\parallel v
since otherwise
\llbracket{u\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket\perp\llbracket{v\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket
.
If
v\subseteq u
, then
\llbracket{v\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket\leq\llbracket{u\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket
by
(1)
.
Therefore
u\subsetneq v
.
∎

Recall from Definition
2.4.11

that

\mathbb{B}(\sigma)=\mathbb{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma)
denotes the complete Boolean subalgebra of
\mathbb{B}
that is completely generated by
\{\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket:\>\alpha,\beta<\mu\}
.

Note that
\sigma_{(p)}\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
for all
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}

by Lemma
4.3.24
.
Define the map
\pi^{\ast}:\mathbb{Q}^{*}\to\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
by letting

\pi^{\ast}(p):=\llbracket{\sigma_{(p)}\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket

for all
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.
115
115

115

\pi^{\ast}(p)\in\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
since
\pi^{\ast}(p)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}\big\{\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket:(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma_{(p)}\big\}

and
\sigma_{(p)}\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
.

Let
\mathbb{R}^{*}:=\operatorname{ran}(\pi^{\ast})=\{\pi^{\ast}(q):q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}\}
.

Lemma 4.3.30
.

For all
p,q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
:

(1)

q\leq\pi^{\ast}(q)
.

(2)

If
p\leq q
, then
\pi^{\ast}(p)\leq\pi^{\ast}(q)
.

Proof.

(1)
holds
since
q\Vdash\sigma_{(q)}\subseteq\sigma
.
For
(2)
, suppose
p\leq q
. Then
\sigma_{(p)}\supseteq\sigma_{(q)}
and hence
\pi^{\ast}(p)\leq\pi^{\ast}(q)
by
the previous lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.3.31
.

(1)

If
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
, then
\pi^{\ast}(q)
is a reduction
116
116

116

See Definition
2.4.7
.
of
q
to
\mathbb{R}^{*}
with respect to the inclusion
\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{*}}:\mathbb{R}^{*}\to\mathbb{B}
.

(2)

\mathbb{R}^{*}
is a complete subforcing of
\mathbb{B}
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, suppose
r\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
and
r\leq\pi^{\ast}(q)
.
We aim to show
r\parallel q
.
We may assume
r<\pi^{\ast}(q)
by the previous lemma.
Take
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
with
r=\pi^{\ast}(p)
.
Then

\llbracket{\sigma_{(p)}\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket=\pi^{\ast}(p)<\pi^{\ast}(q)=\llbracket{\sigma_{(q)}\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket.

Therefore
\sigma_{(p)}\supsetneq\sigma_{(q)}
by Lemma
4.3.29
.
Let
t:=\vartheta_{(q)}{\upharpoonright}0pt(\operatorname{dom}q)
and take
u\in{}^{<\mu}\mu
with
u\supseteq\sigma_{(p)}
.
Then

u\supsetneq\sigma_{(q)}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\big\{q(\vartheta_{(q)}{\upharpoonright}\alpha):\alpha<0pt(\operatorname{dom}q)\big\}

by Lemma
4.3.24
.
Thus
q
,
t
and
u
satisfy the requirements of Lemma
4.3.23
, and hence

\overline{q}:=q\cup\{(t,u)\}\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\overline{q}\leq q
and

\overline{q}\Vdash u\subseteq\sigma
.
Since
u\supseteq\sigma_{(p)}
, we have

\overline{q}\leq\llbracket{u\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket\leq\llbracket{\sigma_{(p)}\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket=\pi^{\ast}(p)=r.

This shows that
q
and
r
are compatible in
\mathbb{Q}
and hence in
\mathbb{B}
.

For
(2)
, it suffices to show that every
b\in\mathbb{B}
has a reduction to
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.
Given
b\in\mathbb{B}
, take
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
with
q\leq b
.
Since
\pi^{\ast}(q)
is a reduction of
q
to
\mathbb{R}^{*}
,

\pi^{\ast}(q)
is also a reduction of
b
to
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.
∎

Let
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
be the Boolean completion of
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.
Since
\mathbb{R}^{*}
is a complete subforcing of
\mathbb{B}

and therefore separative,
we may assume
that
\mathbb{R}^{*}
is dense in
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})

and that
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
is a complete Boolean subalgebra of
\mathbb{B}
.
117
117

117

See Fact
2.4.8

(2)
.

It is easy to see
that
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
is then equal to the complete Boolean subalgebra of
\mathbb{B}
that is completely generated by
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.

Lemma 4.3.32
.

\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})=\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})\subseteq\mathbb{B}(\sigma)

since
\mathbb{R}^{*}\subseteq\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
by definition.
For
\mathbb{B}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
,
it suffices to show

\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mu
.
Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in\mu
and let

a:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}\{\pi^{\ast}(q):q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*},\,(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma_{(q)}\}.

Then
a\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})
.
We claim that
\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket=a
.
Since
\mathbb{Q}^{*}
is dense in
\mathbb{B}
and
q\leq\pi^{*}(q)
for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}\{q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}:\>q\leq\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket\}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}\{q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}:\>(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma_{(q)}\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}{\bigvee}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}\{\pi^{\ast}(q):\>q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*},\,(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma_{(q)}\}=a.

On the other hand, if
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
and
(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma_{(q)}
, then

\pi^{\ast}(q)=\llbracket{\sigma_{(q)}\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket\leq\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket.

Hence

a\leq\llbracket{(\alpha,\beta)\in\sigma}\rrbracket
.
∎

Let
\pi:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
be the retraction associated to the inclusion map
\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{B}(\sigma)}:\mathbb{B}(\sigma)\to\mathbb{B}
. That is, let

\pi(b):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}{\bigwedge}}\nolimits^{\mathbb{B}}\{a\in\mathbb{B}(\sigma):a\geq b\}

for all
b\in\mathbb{B}
.
118
118

118

See Definition
2.4.9
.

Lemma 4.3.33
.

\pi(p)=\pi^{\ast}(p)

for all
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.
Then
\pi(p)\leq\pi^{\ast}(p)

since
\pi^{\ast}(p)\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})=\mathbb{B}(\sigma)

and
p\leq\pi^{\ast}(p)
.
Moreover,
\pi^{\ast}(p)\leq\pi(p)
, since

\pi^{\ast}(p)
is a reduction of
p
to
\mathbb{R}^{*}

and hence also to
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{R}^{*})=\mathbb{B}(\sigma)

and

\pi(p)
is the largest reduction of
p
to
\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
by Lemma
2.4.10
.
∎

The next lemma proves the second part of the Nice Quotient Lemma for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.

Lemma 4.3.34
.

{\mathbb{B}(\sigma)}
forces that the quotient forcing

\mathbb{B}/\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.

Proof.

Suppose
J
is a
\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
-generic filter over
M
.
It suffices to show that in
M[J]
,
the forcing
\mathbb{B}/J
is non-atomic and
has a dense subforcing of size
{\leq}\mu
that is
{<}\mu
-closed,
by Lemma
2.4.4
.
We use the standard facts about quotient forcings in Fact
2.4.8

(3)
.

To see that
\mathbb{B}/J
is non-atomic in
M[J]
, it suffices to show that
M[J][K]\neq M[J]
for all
\mathbb{B}/J
-generic filters
K
over
M[J]
.
Let
K
be
\mathbb{B}/J
-generic over
M[J]
.
Then
K
is also
\mathbb{B}
-generic over
M
,
M[J][K]=M[K]
and
J=K\cap\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
.
Then
M\big[\sigma^{K}\big]=M[J]
by
Lemma
2.4.12
.
Take
y,z\in({}^{\mu}\delta)^{M[K]}
with

\sigma^{K}=[h_{K\cap\mathbb{Q}}](y)
and
y\neq z
.
119
119

119

Recall the notation
h_{K\cap\mathbb{Q}}
from page
•
‣
4.3.c
after Lemma
4.3.16
.

Then
[h_{K\cap\mathbb{Q}}](z)

is
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
-generic over
M\big[\sigma^{K}\big]

by Lemma
4.3.26
.
Since
[h_{K\cap\mathbb{Q}}](z)\in M[K]
,
we have
M[K]\neq M\big[\sigma^{K}\big]
as required.

In
M[J]
,
let

\mathbb{P}^{*}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}\cap(\mathbb{B}/J).

Note that
\mathbb{P}^{*}
is a dense subforcing of
\mathbb{B}/J

since
\mathbb{Q}^{*}
is a dense subforcing of
\mathbb{B}
,
and
|\mathbb{P}^{*}|\leq|\mathbb{Q}^{*}|\leq|\mathbb{Q}|\leq\mu
.
To prove that

\mathbb{P}^{*}
is
{<}\mu
-closed,
let
K
be
\mathbb{B}/J
-generic over
M[J]
.
Then
K
is
\mathbb{B}
-generic over
M

and
J=K\cap\mathbb{B}(\sigma)
.
It suffices
to show that

\mathbb{P}^{*}=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}:\sigma_{(q)}\subseteq\sigma^{K}\},

since the right hand side is
{<}\mu
-closed
by Lemma
4.3.25
.
Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}
.
Then

q\in\mathbb{P}^{*}\Longleftrightarrow\pi(q)\in J\Longleftrightarrow\pi(q)\in K

by Lemma
2.4.10

(2)
.
Since
\pi(q)=\pi^{\ast}(q)=\llbracket{\sigma_{(q)}\subseteq\sigma}\rrbracket

by Lemma
4.3.33
,

\pi(q)\in K\Longleftrightarrow\sigma_{(q)}\subseteq\sigma^{K}
, as required.
∎

If
K
is a
\mathbb{B}
-generic filter over
M
,

x\in\operatorname{ran}([h_{K\cap\mathbb{Q}}])
,
and
\sigma
is a
\mathbb{B}
-name for
x
with
\llbracket{\sigma\in\operatorname{ran}([\dot{h}])}\rrbracket=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{B}}
120
120

120

Recall the notation
\dot{h}
from page
•
‣
4.3.c
.
Note that all
x\in\operatorname{ran}([h_{K\cap\mathbb{Q}}])
are of this form, as in the proof of Lemma
4.3.26
.

then
M[K]
is an
\operatorname{Add}(\kappa,1)
-generic extension of

M[K\cap\mathbb{B}(\sigma)]=M[x]

by the previous lemma.
This completes the proof of the Nice Quotient Lemma
4.3.5
for
\operatorname{Add}(\mu,1)
.

4.4.
A global version

Recall that a class forcing extension is called
tame
if it is a model of
{\sf ZFC}
.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

Assume there exists a proper class of inaccessible cardinals.
Then there is a tame class forcing extension of
V

in which

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

holds for all regular infinite cardinals
\kappa
and all
d
with
2\leq d\leq\kappa
.
121
121

121

Note that this implies
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
for all
d
with
2\leq d<\kappa
by Lemma
3.1.2
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{\kappa}:=\langle\kappa_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}\rangle
denote the order preserving enumeration of the closure
C
of the class of inaccessible cardinals and
\omega
.
We define the following Easton support iteration
\overline{\mathbb{P}}=\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},\dot{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}\rangle
with bounded support at regular limits and full support at singular limits.
Let
\dot{\nu}_{\alpha}
be a
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
-name for the least regular cardinal
\nu\geq\kappa_{\alpha}

that is not collapsed by
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
, and let
\dot{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}
be a
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
-name for
\operatorname{Col}(\dot{\nu}_{\alpha},{{<}\kappa_{\alpha+1}})
.
(We may assume that the names
\dot{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}
are chosen in a canonical way so that the iteration is definable, for instance by working with nice names for elements of the forcing.)
Let
\mathbb{P}
denote the class forcing defined by
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
.
Moreover, let
\dot{\mathbb{P}}^{(\alpha)}
denote a
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
-name for the tail forcing of the iteration at stage
\alpha
.
It follows from the definition of the iteration that
|\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}|<\kappa_{\alpha+1}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}}\Vdash\dot{\mathbb{P}}^{(\alpha)}
is
{<}\kappa_{\alpha}
-closed, for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}
.

Now suppose that
G
is
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
V
.
We write
G_{\alpha}:=G\cap\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}:=(\dot{\mathbb{P}}^{(\alpha)})^{G_{\alpha}}
for
\alpha\in\mathrm{Ord}
. Moreover, let
\nu_{\alpha}:=\dot{\nu}^{G_{\alpha}}
for
\alpha\geq 1
and write
\overline{\nu}:=\langle\nu_{\alpha}:\alpha\geq 1\rangle
.

Claim
.

(a)

If
\kappa_{\alpha}
is inaccessible in
V
, then
\kappa_{\alpha}
remains a regular cardinal in
V[G]
,
\nu_{\alpha}=\kappa_{\alpha}
and
{(\kappa_{\alpha}^{+})}^{V[G]}=\kappa_{\alpha+1}
.

(b)

If
\kappa_{\alpha}
is a singular limit in
V
, then

{(\kappa_{\alpha}^{+})}^{V[G]}=\nu_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

If
\kappa_{\alpha}
is inaccessible in
V
, it follows from the
\Delta
-system lemma that
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
has the
\kappa_{\alpha}
-c.c.

The claims in
(a)
easily follow from this and the fact that
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}
is
{<}\kappa_{\alpha+1}
-closed.
If
\kappa_{\alpha}
is a singular limit in
V
, then

\nu_{\alpha}
is the least regular cardinal strictly above
\kappa_{\alpha}
in
V[G_{\alpha}]
by the definition of
\dot{\nu}_{\alpha}
.
Moreover,
\nu_{\alpha}
is not collapsed in
V[G]
, since
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}
is
{<}\kappa_{\alpha+1}
-closed.
∎

By the previous claim,
\overline{\nu}
enumerates the class of regular infinite cardinals in
V[G]
.
Thus, suppose that
\alpha\geq 1
,
\kappa=\nu_{\alpha}
and
2\leq d\leq\kappa
.
In
V[G]
,
let
X\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
be a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, and
let
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
be a box-open
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Claim
.

\mathsf{ODD}^{H{\upharpoonright}X}_{\kappa}
holds in
V[G]
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\beta}}

forces that

\dot{\mathbb{P}}_{\beta}
is homogeneous for all
\beta\in\mathrm{Ord}
, the tail forcing
\mathbb{P}^{(\beta)}
is homogeneous for all
\beta\in\mathrm{Ord}
.

We first claim that
X,H\in V[G_{\alpha+1}]
.
Note that
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}
does not add new elements of
{}^{\kappa}\mathrm{Ord}
since
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}
is
{<}\kappa_{\alpha+1}
-closed.
Suppose that
X=X_{\varphi,a}^{V[G]}
where
\varphi
is a formula and
a\in{}^{\kappa}\mathrm{Ord}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}
is homogeneous,
X=X_{\psi,a}^{V[G_{\alpha+1}]}
where
\psi(x,a)
denotes the formula
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}}\Vdash^{V[G_{\alpha+1}]}\varphi(x,a)
.
Hence
X\in V[G_{\alpha+1}]
, and similarly
H\in V[G_{\alpha+1}]
.

Since
\kappa=\nu_{\alpha}
,
\dot{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}
is a name for
\operatorname{Col}(\kappa,{<}\kappa_{\alpha+1})
and hence
\mathsf{ODD}^{H{\upharpoonright}X}_{\kappa}
holds in
V[G_{\alpha+1}]

by Theorem
1.4

(1)
.
Since
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha+1)}
is
{<}\kappa_{\alpha+1}
-closed, a
\kappa
-coloring of
H{\upharpoonright}X
in
V[G_{\alpha+1}]
remains a
\kappa
-coloring in
V[G]
, and a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H{\upharpoonright}X
in
V[G_{\alpha+1}]
remains one in
V[G]
.
Hence
\mathsf{ODD}^{H{\upharpoonright}X}_{\kappa}
holds in
V[G]
.
∎

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

holds in
V[G]
by the previous claim.
∎

It is open whether a global version of Theorem
1.4

(2)
holds in analogy with the previous theorem.
The problem is that in an iterated forcing extension as above, new box-open dihypergraphs are added by the tail forcings.

Remark 4.4.2
.

In the model of the previous theorem, we actually have
\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa},\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

for all regular uncountable
\kappa
and
d\leq\kappa

by Remark
4.3.7
.
122
122

122

For
d<\kappa
, we have
\mathsf{ODDI}^{d}_{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})
.
In fact, the continuous homomorphism can even be chosen to be a homeomorphism onto a closed set in this case by
Definition
5.5.1
and Corollary
below.

5.
Variants

We begin with some preliminary results
regarding conditions under which
the map
[\iota]
is closed and the tree
T(\iota)
is sufficiently closed for a given strict order preserving map
\iota
in Subsection
5.1
.

We show in Subsection
5.2

that the open graph dichotomy

\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)

is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{2}_{\kappa}(X)
.
In fact, we prove a more general version of this equivalence for an extension of
\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
to
d
-hypergraphs of higher dimensions
2\leq d\leq\kappa
.

In the remainder of the section,
we show that the definition of
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
is optimal in several ways.

\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
witnesses that for any
d\geq 3
, the existence of a continuous homomorphism cannot be replaced by the existence of a large complete subhypergraph.
Stronger counterexamples are discussed
in Subsection
5.3
.
One may further wonder whether
the continuous homomorphism provided by
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
can be chosen
to have additional properties such as injectivity
for relatively box-open
123
123

123

I.e.,
H
is box-open on its domain
\operatorname{dom}_{H}
.

d
-dihypergraphs
H
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
We show that this cannot be done in general for
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\omega}
.
However, in the uncountable case
a weak version
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
of
\Diamond_{\kappa}
allows us to choose injective continuous homomorphisms.
For
d<\kappa
,
the continuous homomorphism in
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
can even
be chosen to be a homeomorphism onto a closed subset
without assuming
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
.
For
d=\kappa
one cannot in general find a homomorphism that is
a homeomorphism onto its image.
These results are proved in Subsection
5.5
.
Subsection
5.4
studies the principle
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
in preparation for
Subsection
5.5
.

5.1.
Closure properties of maps

Let
Y
and
Z
be topological spaces.
Recall that a map
f:Y\to Z
is called
closed
if
f(X)
is a closed subset of
Z
for any closed subset
X
of
Y
.
It is easy to see that
f
is closed if and only if
\overline{f(X)}=f(\overline{X})
for all subsets
X
of
Y
.

For any map

f:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
,

X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
,
let
{\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{f}(X)
denote the union of all
limit points of sets of the form

\{f(x_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\}

where

x_{\alpha}\in N_{t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle}\cap X

for all
\alpha<\kappa
,
and let

{\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{f}={\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{f}({}^{\kappa}\kappa)
.
For strict order preserving maps
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
,
we also write

{\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{\iota}(X):={\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{[\iota]}(X)
and

{\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{\iota}:={\mathrm{Lim}}_{t}^{[\iota]}
.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Suppose that
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
is
\perp
- and strict order preserving.

(1)

If
2\leq d<\kappa
, then

[\iota]
is a closed map.

(2)

If
d=\kappa
,
then

\overline{[\iota](X)}=[\iota](\overline{X})\cup\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{t\in T(X)}{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}(X).

for all subsets
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
In particular,

[\iota]
is a closed map if

{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Proof.

We prove
(1)
and
(2)
simultaneously.
Fix
2\leq d\leq\kappa
and a subset
X
of
{}^{\kappa}d
.
Note that it suffices to show
[\iota](\overline{X})=\overline{[\iota](X)}
for
(1)
.
It is clear that
[\iota](\overline{X})\subseteq\overline{[\iota](X)}
and that

{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}(X)\subseteq\overline{[\iota](X)}
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
in the case
d=\kappa
.
Now suppose that
y\in\overline{[\iota](X)}
.
It suffices to show
y\in[\iota](\overline{X})\cup\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{t\in T(X)}{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}(X)
.

y
is the limit of some convergent sequence

\langle[\iota](x_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\rangle
with
x_{\alpha}\in X
for each
\alpha<\kappa
.
If
\overline{x}:=\langle x_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
converges to some
x
, then
x\in\overline{X}
and
y\in[\iota](\overline{X})
.
Therefore we now assume that
\overline{x}
does not converge.
We will see that
d=\kappa
and
y\in{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}(X)
for some
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Let
\gamma\leq\kappa
be least such that

\langle x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\gamma:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is not eventually constant.
Then clearly
\gamma>0
.
We first claim that
\gamma
is a successor ordinal.
Fix some
\delta_{\beta}<\kappa
such that
\langle x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\beta:\delta_{\beta}\leq\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is constant for each
\beta<\gamma
and let
\delta:=\sup_{\beta<\gamma}\delta_{\beta}<\kappa
.
If
\gamma
were a limit ordinal, then
\langle x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\gamma:\delta\leq\alpha<\kappa\rangle
would be constant.

Let
\gamma=\beta+1
.
We may assume that

\langle x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\beta:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
has a constant value
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
by replacing
\overline{x}
by a tail.
Then
x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\gamma=t{}^{\frown}\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta)\rangle
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
Next, we claim that

\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta):\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is unbounded in
\kappa
; in particular
d=\kappa
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose that there is a strict upper bound
\delta<\kappa
with

x_{\alpha}(\beta)<\delta
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
Since

\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta):\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is not eventually constant,
there exist
i<j<\kappa
and disjoint unbounded subsets
I,\,J
of
\kappa
with

x_{\alpha}(\beta)=i
for all
\alpha\in I

and

x_{\alpha}(\beta)=j
for all
\alpha\in J
.
Then

[\iota](x_{\alpha})\supseteq\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)

for all
\alpha\in I
and

[\iota](x_{\alpha})\supseteq\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)

for all
\alpha\in J
.
Since
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving,

\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\perp\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)
.
But then
\langle[\iota](x_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\rangle
cannot be convergent.

To show that
y\in{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}(X)
,
we may assume that
\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta):\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is injective by replacing
\overline{x}
with a subsequence.
Let
\overline{z}=\langle z_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
be a sequence with
z_{\alpha}\in N_{t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle}\cap X
and

z_{\alpha}=x_{\delta}
if
\alpha=x_{\delta}(\beta)
.
Since
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving,
[\iota]
is injective.
Thus
y
is a limit point of the set
\{[\iota](z_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\}
and hence
y\in{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}(X)
.
∎

The next corollary follows from the previous lemma and Lemma
2.3.4
.

Corollary 5.1.2
.

Suppose

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
is
\perp
- and strict order preserving.
If
d<\kappa
or if
d=\kappa
and
{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
, then

[\iota]
is a homeomorphism from
{}^{\kappa}d
onto a closed subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

The next lemma shows that the assumption that
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving can be omitted from the previous lemma if
\kappa
is weakly compact.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Suppose that
\kappa=\omega
or
\kappa
is weakly compact.
Let
f:{}^{\kappa}d\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be a continuous map.

(1)

If
2\leq d<\kappa
, then

f
is a closed map.

(2)

If
d=\kappa
,
then

\overline{f(X)}=f(\overline{X})\cup\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{t\in T(X)}{\mathrm{Lim}}^{f}_{t}(X).

for all subsets
X
of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
In particular,

f
is a closed map if

{\mathrm{Lim}}^{f}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Proof.

(1)
holds since closed subsets of
{}^{\kappa}d
are
\kappa
-compact,

\kappa
-compactness is preserved under continuous images and

\kappa
-compact subsets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
are closed.

For
(2)
,
note that clearly
f(\overline{X})\subseteq\overline{f(X)}
and that

{\mathrm{Lim}}^{f}_{t}(X)\subseteq\overline{f(X)}
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
It suffices to show
y\in f(\overline{X})\cup\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{t\in T(X)}{\mathrm{Lim}}^{f}_{t}(X)
for any
y\in\overline{f(X)}
.
Fix a sequence

\langle f(x_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\rangle
converging to
y
with
x_{\alpha}\in X
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
We may assume
f(x_{\alpha})\neq y
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
If
\overline{x}:=\langle x_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is bounded, then it is contained in a
\kappa
-compact subset
C
of
\overline{X}
. Then
f(C)
is also
\kappa
-compact and thus closed, so
y\in f(C)\subseteq f(\overline{X})
.
Now suppose that
\overline{x}
is unbounded.
Let
\beta<\kappa
be least such that

\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta):\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is unbounded in
\kappa
.
We may assume that
\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta):\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is injective by replacing
\overline{x}
with a subsequence.
There are only
|\kappa{}^{\beta}|
many possibilities for
x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\beta
, and
|\kappa{}^{\beta}|<\kappa
since
\kappa
is inaccessible.
We may thus assume that
\langle x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}\beta:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

has a constant value
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
by replacing
\overline{x}

with a further subsequence.
Then
x_{\alpha}{\upharpoonright}{(\beta+1)}=t{}^{\frown}\langle x_{\alpha}(\beta)\rangle
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
Let
\overline{z}=\langle z_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
be a sequence with
z_{\alpha}\in N_{t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle}\cap X
and

z_{\alpha}=x_{\gamma}
if
\alpha=x_{\gamma}(\beta)
.
Since
y\neq f(x_{\gamma})
for all
\gamma<\kappa
,

y
is a limit point of the set
\{f(z_{\alpha}):\alpha<\kappa\}
, and thus
y\in{\mathrm{Lim}}^{f}_{t}(X)
.
∎

The next remark shows that the following assumptions cannot be omitted:
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving in Lemma
5.1.1
and
\kappa
is weakly compact in Lemma
5.1.3
.

Remark 5.1.4
.

Suppose that
\kappa
is not weakly compact.

(1)

There exists a strict order preserving map
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}2\to{}^{<\kappa}2
such that
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
is not closed, and thus

[\iota]
is not a closed map.
To see this, we first claim that there exists a
barrier

A
in
{}^{<\kappa}2
of size
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
,
124
124

124

A similar argument also works without the assumption that
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
.

i.e. a maximal antichain in
{}^{<\kappa}2
of size
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
with the following equivalent properties: (a) every
x\in{}^{\kappa}2

has an initial segment in
A
and (b)
T(A)
has no
\kappa
-branches.
If
\kappa
is inaccessible, let
A
be the boundary of a
\kappa
-Aronszajn subtree of
{}^{<\kappa}2
.
If
\kappa=\mu^{+}
, let
A:={}^{\mu}2
.

Fix an injective enumeration
\langle t_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
of
A
.
Further fix a strictly increasing sequence
\langle\gamma_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
with
\gamma_{\alpha}\geq\operatorname{lh}(t_{\alpha})
and let
\iota(t_{\alpha}):=\langle 0\rangle^{\gamma_{\alpha}}{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
We now extend
\iota

to a strict order preserving map
from
{}^{<\kappa}2
to
{}^{<\kappa}2
.
If
s\subsetneq t
for some
t\in A
, let
\iota(s):=\langle 0\rangle^{\operatorname{lh}(s)}
.
We further
extend
\iota
above
A
arbitrarily
to a strict order preserving map.
Then
\langle 0\rangle^{\kappa}
witnesses that
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
is not closed.
Note that we can also guarantee that
[\iota]
is injective
by extending
\iota
in a
\perp
-preserving way above
A
.
125
125

125

I.e., so that for all
u\in A
and all
incompatible
v,w\supsetneq u
, we have
\iota(v)\perp\iota(w)
).
E.g. let
\iota(u{}^{\frown}v):=\iota(u){}^{\frown}v
for all

u\in A
and
v\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

(2)

There exists a strict order preserving map
\theta:{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
such that
{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\theta}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
and
\operatorname{ran}([\theta])
is not closed.
To see this, define
\theta(s{}^{\frown}t):=\iota(s){}^{\frown}t
for all
s\in{}^{<\kappa}2
and
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
with
t(0)\geq 2
,
where
\iota
is as in
(1)
.
Note that if
[\iota]
is injective, then so is
[\theta]
.

If
\mu
is a regular cardinal, let
\mathrm{cof}_{\mu}
denote the class of limit ordinals

\alpha

with
\mathrm{cof}(\alpha)=\mu
,
and
for any ordinal
\gamma
,
let
\mathrm{cof}^{\gamma}_{\mu}:=\mathrm{cof}_{\mu}\cap\gamma
.
We define
\mathrm{cof}_{{\leq}\mu}
,
\mathrm{cof}_{<\mu}
,

\mathrm{cof}^{\gamma}_{{>}\mu}
,
\mathrm{cof}^{\gamma}_{>\mu}
etc. similarly
for arbitrary ordinals
\mu
.
Given a class
C
of limit ordinals and a subtree
T
of
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
, we say that

T
is
C
-closed
if every strictly increasing sequence in
T
with length in
A
has an upper bound in
T
.

The next lemma shows that the relevant closure properties of
T(\iota)
are determined by the following sets.
For
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
and
u\in{}^{<\kappa}d
, we write
T^{\iota,u}
for the downwards closure of

\{\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle):\alpha<\kappa\}
in
{}^{<\kappa}d
.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

If
2\leq d\leq\kappa
,
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
is
\perp
- and strict order preserving and
\mu<\kappa
is a regular infinite cardinal, then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

T(\iota)
is
\mathrm{cof}^{\kappa}_{\mu}
-closed.

(2)

For all
u\in{}^{<\kappa}d
,
T^{\iota,u}
is
\mathrm{cof}^{\kappa}_{\mu}
-closed.

Proof.

(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
:
Suppose that
w
is a limit of nodes in
T^{\iota,u}
with
w\in T(\iota)\,{\setminus}\,T^{\iota,u}
.
There exists some
\eta<\kappa
with
\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\eta\rangle)\subsetneq w
, since otherwise
w
properly extends
\iota(u)
and is incompatible with
\iota(t)
for all
t\supsetneq u
, but this would entail
w\notin T(\iota)
.
Then
\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\xi\rangle)\perp w
for all
\xi\neq\eta
, so
w
cannot be a limit of nodes in
T^{\iota,u}
.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(1)
:
Suppose that
w
is a limit of nodes in
T(\iota)
with
\operatorname{lh}(w)\in\mathrm{cof}^{\kappa}_{\mu}
.
We shall show that
w\in T(\iota)
.
For all
\gamma<\operatorname{lh}(w)
, pick some
s_{\gamma}
such that
w{{\upharpoonright}}\gamma\subseteq\iota(s_{\gamma})
and
\operatorname{lh}(s_{\gamma})>\operatorname{lh}(u)
.
Let

A:=\{t\in{}^{<\kappa}d:\iota(t)\subseteq w\}
. Note that the elements of
A
are pairwise comparable, since
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving.
Hence
u:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}A
and
v:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\iota(t):t\in A\}
are elements of
{}^{<\kappa}d
.
We may assume that
v\subsetneq w
, since otherwise
w=v\subseteq\iota(u)
.

We claim that
u\subsetneq s_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma
with
\operatorname{lh}(v)<\gamma<\operatorname{lh}(w)
.
To see this, note that for any
t\in A
, we have
\iota(t)\subseteq v\subseteq w{{\upharpoonright}}\gamma\subseteq\iota(s_{\gamma})
, and hence
t\subseteq s_{\gamma}
since
\iota
is
\perp
-preserving.
As this holds for all
t\in A
, we have
u\subseteq s_{\gamma}
.
As
\operatorname{lh}(s_{\gamma})>\operatorname{lh}(u)
, we have
u\subsetneq s_{\gamma}
.

For each
\gamma
as above, we can thus pick some
\eta_{\gamma}
with
u{}^{\frown}\langle\eta_{\gamma}\rangle\subseteq s_{\gamma}
.
It suffices to show
w{{\upharpoonright}}\gamma\subseteq\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\eta_{\gamma}\rangle)
, since this implies
w\in T^{\iota,u}\subseteq T(\iota)
.
To see this, note that
w{{\upharpoonright}}\gamma\parallel\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\eta_{\gamma}\rangle)
, since both are initial segments of
\iota(s_{\gamma})
.
But
\iota(u{}^{\frown}\langle\eta_{\gamma}\rangle)\subseteq w{{\upharpoonright}}\gamma\subseteq w
would entail
u{}^{\frown}\langle\eta_{\gamma}\rangle\subseteq u
by the definition of
u
.
∎

The next corollary is immediate:

Corollary 5.1.6
.

Suppose that
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
is
\perp
- and strict order preserving.

(1)

If
2\leq d<\kappa
, then
T(\iota)
is
\mathrm{cof}^{\kappa}_{>d}
-closed.

(2)

If the nodes
\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)

split at the same node
for each
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
,
126
126

126

I.e., the
\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)
’s extend pairwise different immediate successors of the some
s_{t}\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
, for each
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
.
Equivalently,
\iota
is an order homomorphism for the dihypergraph
\mathbb{H}_{\mathsf{super}}
defined right after Definition
.

then
T(\iota)
is
{<}\kappa
-closed.

The next remark shows
that
the restriction to
\mathrm{cof}^{\kappa}_{>d}

is necessary in the previous corollary.

Remark 5.1.7
.

Suppose
d\leq\kappa
and

\eta\in\mathrm{cof}^{\kappa}_{\leq d}
.
Then there exists a
\perp
- and strict order preserving function
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
such that
T(\iota)
is not
\{\eta\}
-closed.
To see this, choose a strictly increasing sequence
\langle\gamma_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mathrm{cof}(\eta)\rangle
cofinal in
\eta
,
and let

\iota=\iota_{d,\eta}
be any
\perp
- and strict order preserving map
from
{}^{<\kappa}d
to
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

such that

\iota\left(\langle\alpha\rangle\right)=\begin{cases}\langle 0\rangle^{\gamma_{\alpha}}{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle&\text{if $\alpha<\mathrm{cof}(\eta)$,}\\
\langle\alpha\rangle&\text{if $\alpha\in[\mathrm{cof}(\eta),d)$.}\end{cases}

Then the node

{\langle 0\rangle}^{\eta}

witnesses that

T(\iota)
is not
\{\eta\}
-closed.
For
d=\kappa
, it is also easy to make sure that

{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
127
127

127

Let, for example,
\iota(\langle\alpha\rangle{}^{\frown}t)=\iota(\langle\alpha\rangle){}^{\frown}t
for all
\alpha<\kappa
and
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
Note that
{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\iota}_{\emptyset}=\emptyset
is guaranteed by the choice of the
\iota(\langle\alpha\rangle)
’s.

Suppose that
d=\kappa
.
Then there exists a
\perp
- and strict order preserving function
\psi:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
such that
T(\psi)
is not
\{\eta\}
-closed for all limit ordinals
\eta<\kappa
, and at the same time

{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\psi}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
To see this, let
\langle\eta_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
be an enumeration of
all the limit ordinals below
\kappa
.
For all
\alpha<\kappa
, let
\iota_{\alpha}
be the map

\iota_{\kappa,\eta_{\alpha}}

defined in the previous paragraph.
Define

\psi:{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

where

\psi(\emptyset):=\emptyset

and

\psi(\langle\alpha\rangle{}^{\frown}t):=\langle\alpha\rangle{}^{\frown}\iota_{\alpha}(t)

for all
\alpha<\kappa
and

t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
Then
T(\psi)
is not
\{\eta\}
-closed for any limit ordinal
\eta<\kappa
,
and we can also ensure that
{\mathrm{Lim}}^{\psi}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Lemma 5.1.8
.

Suppose
2\leq d<\omega
.

(1)

If
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

is
\perp
- and strict order preserving, then

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
is a
\kappa
-perfect set.

(2)

If
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
has a
\kappa
-perfect subset, then
there exists a
\perp
- and strict order preserving map

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
with
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq X
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, let
\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
be

\perp
- and strict order preserving.
By Lemma
5.1.1
,

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])
is a closed set, so

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])=[T(\iota)]
.
It thus suffices to show that
T(\iota)
is a
\kappa
-perfect tree.

T(\iota)
is
{<}\kappa
-closed
by Corollary
5.1.6
.

T(\iota)
is also cofinally splitting since
\iota
preserves
\perp
.
For
(2)
, let

T
be a
\kappa
-perfect tree
T
with
[T]\subseteq X
.
It is easy to construct a
\perp
- and strict order preserving map

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to T
by recursion.
Then
\operatorname{ran}([\iota])\subseteq[T]\subseteq X
.
∎

5.2.
The open graph dichotomy

Throughout this subsection, we assume that
X
is a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
We say that a graph
G
on
X
has a
\kappa
-perfect complete subgraph
if there exists a
\kappa
-perfect subset
Y\subseteq X
such that the complete graph
\mathbb{K}_{Y}
is a subset of
G
.
Recall that the open graph dichotomy
\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
is the following statement:

\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
:
If
G
is an open graph on
X
, then
either
G
admits a
\kappa
-coloring or

G
has a
\kappa
-perfect
complete subgraph.

In this subsection, we show that
the open graph dichotomy
is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{2}_{\kappa}(X)
.
128
128

128

Thus, it also follows from

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,\mathsf{D}_{\kappa})

for any
d>2

by Lemmas
3.1.2
and
3.1.7
.

In fact, we prove a more general version of
this equivalence for an extension of
\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
to
d
-hypergraphs for higher dimensions
2\leq d\leq\kappa
.

Recall that a
d
-hypergraph
is a
d
-dihypergraph that is closed under permutation of hyperedges.
For any
d
-dihypergraph
I
,
129
129

129

In this subsection only,
H
always denotes a hypergraph and
I
denotes a dihypergraph.

let
{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}
denote the smallest hypergraph containing
I
, i.e.,

{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\{\overline{x}^{\pi}:\>{\overline{x}\in I,\ \pi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(d)}\}.

Then

{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}\left\{\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha\in d}N_{t{}^{\frown}\langle\pi(\alpha)\rangle}:\>{t\in{}^{<\kappa}d,\,\pi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(d)}\right\}
,
and
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
equals the complete graph
\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}2}
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

\mathsf{OHD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
states that the following holds for all box-open
d
-hypergraphs

H
on
X
:

\mathsf{OHD}^{H}_{\kappa}
:
Either
H
admits a
\kappa
-coloring, or
there exists a continuous homomorphism
from
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.

We first show that
\mathsf{OHD}^{2}_{\kappa}(X)
is equivalent to the open graph dichotomy
\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
.
Since
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}=\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}2}
,
it suffices to show that
a graph
G
on
X
has a
\kappa
-perfect complete subgraph
if and only if there is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}2}
to
G
.
The next lemma is
a more general version of this for dihypergraphs.
We say a
d
-dihypergraph

I

has a
\kappa
-perfect complete subhypergraph
if there exists a
\kappa
-perfect subset
Y\subseteq X
such that
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{Y}\subseteq I
.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Suppose that
2\leq d\leq\kappa
and

I
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
X
.

(1)

If
f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
,
then
\operatorname{ran}(f)
has a
\kappa
-perfect subset.

(2)

I
has a
\kappa
-perfect complete subhypergraph if and only if
there exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}\!d}
to
I
.

Proof.

For
(1)
, note first that

f
is also a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{\operatorname{ran}(f)}
.
Since

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{\operatorname{ran}(f)}

is relatively box-open,
there exists
a continuous order homomorphism

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}d\to{}^{<\kappa}\kappa

for

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{\operatorname{ran}(f)}

by Lemma
3.3.3
.
For all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
,
there exists
u,v\supsetneq t
with
\iota(u)\perp\iota(v)

since otherwise,
every sequence in
\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{\alpha<d}(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle\alpha\rangle)}\cap\operatorname{ran}([\iota]))

would be constant,
so
\iota
would not be an order homomorphism
for
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{\operatorname{ran}(f)}
.
Using this observation and the continuity of
\iota
,
it is easy to construct a continuous
\perp
- and strict order preserving map

\theta:{}^{<\kappa}2\to\operatorname{ran}(\iota)
by recursion.
Then
\operatorname{ran}([\theta])
is a
\kappa
-perfect subset of

\operatorname{ran}([\iota])

by Lemma
5.1.8
.

For
(2)
,
suppose first that
f
is a homomorphism from

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}d}

to
I
.
Then

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{\operatorname{ran}(f)}\subseteq I
.
Since
\operatorname{ran}(f)
has a
\kappa
-perfect subset
by
(1)
,

I
has a
\kappa
-perfect complete subhypergraph.
Conversely,
let
Y
be a
\kappa
-perfect subset of
X
such that
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{Y}\subseteq I
.
Take any map
f:{}^{\kappa}d\to Y
that is a homeomorphism onto its image.
Since
f
is injective, it is a homomorphism from

\mathbb{K}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}\!d}
to
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{Y}\subseteq I
.
∎

The special case of
(2)
for the complete dihypergraph
I:=\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}
shows that
X
has a
\kappa
-perfect subset if and only if there exists a continuous injection from
{}^{\kappa}d
onto a closed subset of
X
.
130
130

130

This can also be proved directly as in
[
LuckeMottoRosSchlichtHurewicz
]
*Lemma 2.9 using Lemma
5.1.1
.

It also implies the next corollary since
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}
has a
\kappa
-coloring if and only if
|X|\leq\kappa
.

Corollary 5.2.3
.

\mathsf{PSP}_{\kappa}(X)\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{ODD}^{\mathbb{K}^{d}_{X}}_{\kappa}
for all
d
with
2\leq d\leq\kappa
.

Let
H
be a
d
-hypergraph on
X
.
Then
any homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
is also a homomorphism from
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
,
and
hence
\mathsf{OHD}^{H}_{\kappa}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
.
Thus

\mathsf{OHD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X,\mathcal{H}_{d})
,
where
\mathcal{H}_{d}
denotes the class of all
d
-hypergraphs.
The next theorem
shows that
\mathsf{OHD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
is in fact equivalent to

\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
.

Theorem 5.2.4
.

Suppose
2\leq d\leq\kappa
and
I
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
X
.

(1)

If
I
is box-open on
X
, then

{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}
is box-open on
X
.

(2)

I\equiv_{\mathsf{f}}{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}
.
131
131

131

See Definition
3.4.1
.

(3)

If
I
is box-open on
X
, then

\mathsf{ODD}^{I}_{\kappa}\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{OHD}^{{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}}_{\kappa}
.
132
132

132

Equivalently, there exists a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I
if and only if there exists a continuous homomorphism from
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}
.

In particular,
\mathsf{OHD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
.

Proof.

(1)
is clear.

(2)
holds since a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is
{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}
-independent if and only if it is
I
-independent.
For
(3)
,
note that

\mathsf{ODD}^{I}_{\kappa}\Longleftrightarrow\mathsf{ODD}^{{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}}_{\kappa}
by

(1)
,
(2)
and Corollary
3.4.3
.
The latter is equivalent to

\mathsf{OHD}^{{I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}}_{\kappa}
by the paragraph preceding the theorem.
The last claim follows from
(1)
and
(3)
.
∎

Corollary 5.2.5
.

Suppose
I
is an open digraph
on
X
,

and let
G:={I}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}
be the smallest graph containing
I
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

There is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
to
I
.

(2)

There is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}\!2}
to
G
.

(3)

G
has a
\kappa
-perfect complete subgraph.

In particular,
the open graph dichotomy
\mathsf{OGD}_{\kappa}(X)
is equivalent to
\mathsf{ODD}^{2}_{\kappa}(X)
.

Proof.

(1)

\Leftrightarrow

(2)
follows from Theorem
5.2.4

(3)

since
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}=\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}2}
.

(2)

\Leftrightarrow

(3)
follows from Lemma
5.2.2

(2)

since
\mathbb{K}^{2}_{{}^{\kappa}2}=\mathbb{K}_{{}^{\kappa}2}
.
∎

5.3.
Counterexamples

A
complete subhypergraph
of a
d
-dihypergraph
H
on
X
is a subhypergraph of the form
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{Y}\subseteq H
for some subset
Y\subseteq X
.
In the definition of
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
, one cannot replace
the existence of a continuous homomorphism with the existence of a large complete subhypergraph if
|d|\geq 3
,
since
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring by Lemma
3.1.1

and it does not have a complete subhypergraph of size
2
.
Instead of complete subhypergraphs,
it is thus more interesting to consider subhypergraphs of the form

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}

consisting of all
injective

d
-sequences in some subset
Y
of
X
.
However,
\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\not\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
for any
Y
of size
|d|
.
Similarly, the hypergraph
{\mathbb{H}}^{{\mathsf{sym}}}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring
and if
d
is infinite, then

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\not\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
for any
Y
of size
|d|
.
If
d
is finite, then

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\not\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
for any
Y
of size
|d|+1
.

We now describe some more interesting counterexamples with stronger properties.
Moreover, we will see
that one cannot replace open by closed dihypergraphs in
\mathsf{ODD}^{d}_{\kappa}(X)
.
The claims are shown by generalizing known examples to uncountable cardinals.

Example 5.3.1
.

For any ordinal
d\geq 3
, there exists
a product-open
133
133

133

I.e.,
H
is an open subset of
{}^{d}X
in the product topology.

d
-dihypergraph

H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
on
{}^{\kappa}2

with the properties:

(a)

H
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring.

(b)

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\not\subseteq H
for any
Y
of size at least
\max\{5,|d|\}
.

For
d=3
,
H
can be chosen to be a hypergraph.

It suffices to provide an example for
d=3
, since one can then apply a projection
(see Lemma
3.1.6
).
We follow
[
TodorcevicFarah
]
*Section 10 and
[
SzThesis
]
*Example 3.4.
Let
X
denote the set of those
x\in{}^{\kappa}2
with
x(\alpha)=0
for
\alpha=0
and all limits
\alpha<\kappa
.
Since
X
is homeomorphic to
{}^{\kappa}2
,
it suffices to define an open dihypergraph on
X
with the required properties.
Recall that for
x\neq y
in
{}^{\kappa}2
,
\Delta(x,y)
denotes the least
\alpha<\kappa
with
x(\alpha)\neq y(\alpha)
.
134
134

134

Note that
\Delta(x,y)
equals the length of the node
x\wedge y
where
x
and
y
split.

For any

\overline{x}\in\mathbb{I}^{3}_{X}
,
let

\overline{x}_{0},\,\overline{x}_{1},\,\overline{x}_{2}

list
\operatorname{ran}(\overline{x})
such that

\Delta(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{1})<\Delta(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2})
.
As can be seen in Figure
2
,

\overline{x}_{0}
is unique while the role of
\overline{x}_{1}
and
\overline{x}_{2}
can be switched.
Let

\Delta_{0}^{\overline{x}}:=\Delta(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{1})=\Delta(\overline{x}_{0},\overline{x}_{2})

and

\Delta_{1}^{\overline{x}}:=\Delta(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2})
.
Then
\Delta_{0}^{\overline{x}},\,\Delta_{1}^{\overline{x}}
are successor ordinals with
\Delta_{0}^{\overline{x}}<\Delta_{1}^{\overline{x}}
.
For any

\overline{x}\in\mathbb{I}^{3}_{X}
, let

\overline{x}\in H\ :\Longleftrightarrow\ \overline{x}_{2}{\left(\Delta_{0}^{\overline{x}}-1\right)}\neq\overline{x}_{2}{\left(\Delta_{1}^{\overline{x}}-1\right)}.

It is easy to see that
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
is an open
3
-hypergraph.

\overline{x}_{0}
\overline{x}_{1}
\overline{x}_{2}
\Delta_{0}^{\overline{x}}
\Delta_{1}^{\overline{x}}

Figure 2.
A triple in
H

For
(a)
, it suffices to show that any
H
-independent subset
Y
of
X
is nowhere dense in
X
, since
X
is homeomorphic to
{}^{\kappa}2
and thus not the union of
\kappa
many nowhere dense subsets.
To this end, take any
s\in T(X)
.
There exist
s_{0},s_{1},s_{2}\in T(X)

with
s\subseteq s_{i}
for all
i\leq 2
and

N_{s_{0}}\times N_{s_{1}}\times N_{s_{2}}\subseteq H

by the definition of
H
.
Since
Y
is
H
-independent, there is some
i\leq 2
with
N_{s_{i}}\cap Y=\emptyset
.

To show
(b)
,
Suppose that
Y\subseteq X
and
|Y|=5
.
By considering the splitting nodes of pairs in
Y
from bottom to top, it is easy to check that there exist
x_{0},\,x_{1},\,x_{2},\,x_{3}\in Y
with

\Delta_{0}<\Delta_{1}<\Delta_{2}
, where

\Delta_{i}:=\Delta(x_{i},x_{i+1})

for
i\leq 2
.

x_{0}
