\mathbb{I}^{3}_{Y}\not\subseteq H

Let
i,j
be such that
i<j\leq 2
and

x_{3}{\left(\Delta_{i}-1\right)}=x_{3}{\left(\Delta_{j}-1\right)}.

Since
(x_{i},x_{j},x_{3})\not\in H
, we have
\mathbb{I}^{3}_{Y}\not\subseteq H
.

We have not studied whether there exists a
d
-hypergraph with the previous properties for any
d\geq 4
.
In the previous example,
the set of all
x\in{}^{\kappa}2
with
x(\alpha)=0
for all even
\alpha<\kappa
is a
\kappa
-perfect
H
-independent set for all
d\geq 3
.
The next example shows that
one can strengthen the non-existence of a
\kappa
-coloring to the non-existence of a large independent set.

Example 5.3.2
.

For any ordinal
d\geq 3
, there exists
a product-open

d
-dihypergraph
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa

with the properties:

(a)

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\cap H\neq\emptyset
for any
Y
of size
\kappa^{+}
.

(b)

\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\not\subseteq H
for any
Y
of size
\kappa^{+}
.

For
d=3
,
H
can be chosen to be a hypergraph.
Note that
(a)
implies that
there is no
H
-independent set of size
\kappa^{+}
and hence

H
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring.

It suffices to provide an example for
d=3
, since one can then apply a projection
(see Lemma
3.1.6
).
We follow
[
HeHigherDimOCA
]
*Example 1.3.
135
135

135

This is also a special case of an argument of Blass
[
blass1981partition
]
*p. 273 with a stronger conclusion.

For any

\overline{x}\in\mathbb{I}^{3}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}
,
let
\overline{x}^{0}\leq_{\mathsf{lex}}\overline{x}^{1}\leq_{\mathsf{lex}}\overline{x}^{2}
list
\operatorname{ran}(\overline{x})
in lexicographic order
and define

\overline{x}\in H\ :\Longleftrightarrow\ \Delta{\left(\overline{x}^{0},\overline{x}^{1}\right)}\leq\Delta{\left(\overline{x}^{1},\overline{x}^{2}\right)}.

Note that
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
is an open hypergraph.

We first show
(b)
.
Suppose that
\mathbb{I}^{3}_{Y}\subseteq H
.
We claim that the lexicographic order on
Y
is a well-order with order type at most
\kappa+1
.
All splitting nodes of
T(Y)
are compatible and thus lie on a branch
z
.
Since
\mathbb{I}^{3}_{Y}\subseteq H
, we have
y\leq_{\mathsf{lex}}z
for all
y\in Y
.
Therefore for any splitting node
s\subsetneq z
of
T(Y)
,
the order type of the set of all
y\in Y{\setminus}\{z\}
with
y\wedge z=s

is an ordinal
{<}\kappa
.
Since
z
may be in
Y
, the order type of
Y
is at most
\kappa+1
.
Hence
|Y|\leq\kappa
.

For
(a)
,
suppose that
\mathbb{I}^{d}_{Y}\cap H=\emptyset
.
Then
\Delta{\left(\overline{x}^{0},\overline{x}^{1}\right)}>\Delta{\left(\overline{x}^{1},\overline{x}^{2}\right)}

for all

\overline{x}\in\mathbb{I}^{3}_{Y}
.
We claim that the reverse lexicographic order on
Y
is a well-order with order type at most
\kappa+1
.
As before, all splitting nodes of
T(Y)
lie on a branch
z
.
If
s\subsetneq z
is a splitting node, then exactly one element of
Y
splits off at
s
.
Therefore the order type is at most
\kappa+1
, and hence
|Y|\leq\kappa
.

We have not studied whether there exists a
d
-hypergraph with the properties of the previous example for any
d\geq 4
.
He
[
HeHigherDimOCA
, Theorem 4.3]
, Di Prisco and Todorčević
[
di1998perfect
, Sections 3 & 4]

showed for
dimensions
3\leq n<\omega

that open
n
-hypergraphs with additional structural properties
either admit a countable coloring or contain a perfect complete subhypergraph.

The next example shows that

\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
may fail for some closed hypergraph
H

in any dimension
d\geq 2
.

Example 5.3.3
.

For any ordinal
d\geq 2
,
there exists a product-closed
136
136

136

I.e.,
H
is a relatively closed subset
of
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}
, where
{}^{d}{({}^{\kappa}\kappa)}
has the product topology.
Equivalently,
H\cup{\Delta}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}
is a closed subset of
{}^{d}{({}^{\kappa}\kappa)}
with the product topology.

d
-hypergraph
H\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}

on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
with the properties:

(a)

H
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring.

(b)

(i)

H
does not have a complete subhypergraph of size
\kappa^{+}
.

(ii)

There is no continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.

We first provide an example for
d=2
.
An example for
\kappa=\omega
can be found in
[
TodorcevicFarah
, Proposition 10.1]
, so suppose
\kappa
is uncountable.
We follow
[
MR1940513
]
*Exercise 29.9 and
[
SzThesis
]
*Example 3.3.
The example is based on the notion of a
strongly
\kappa
-dense linear order
.
This is a linear order with no
({<}\kappa,{<}\kappa)
-gaps.
A
({<}\kappa,{<}\kappa)
-gap in a linear order
(\mathbb{L},\leq)
is a pair
(A,B)
of subsets of
\mathbb{L}
with the properties:

(1)

a<b
for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
.

(2)

There is no
x\in\mathbb{L}
with
a<x<b
for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
.

(3)

|A|,|B|<\kappa
.

It is easy to see that a strongly
\kappa
-dense linear order
\mathbb{L}
of size
\kappa
exists for any
\kappa
with
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
.
137
137

137

It is also easy to show using a back-and-forth argument that any two strongly
\kappa
-dense linear orders of size
\kappa
are isomorphic.

For instance, let
\mathbb{Q}_{\kappa}
denote the set of all
x\in{}^{\kappa}\{-1,0,1\}
that take values in
\{-1,1\}
up to some
\alpha<\kappa
and are constant with value
0
from
\alpha
.

\mathbb{Q}_{\kappa}
with the lexicographical order is strongly
\kappa
-dense.

Let
(\mathbb{L},<)
be a strongly dense linear order of size
\kappa
, and let

X:=\{y\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{L})\,:\,{\leq}{\restriction}y\text{ is a well-order}\}.

Since
|\mathbb{L}|=\kappa
,
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{L})
can be identified with
{}^{\kappa}2
.
Since
\kappa
is uncountable,
X
is a closed subset.
138
138

138

For
\kappa=\omega
,
X
would be a
\mathbf{\Pi}^{1}_{1}
subset.

Therefore it suffices to define a closed graph
H
on
X
with the required properties.
For
x,y\in X
, let

(x,y)\in H\ :\Longleftrightarrow\ x\subsetneq y\text{ or }y\subsetneq x.

It is clear that
H
is a closed graph on
X
which has no complete subgraph of size
\kappa^{+}
.
Thus there is no continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
to
H
by Corollary
5.2.5
.

We now show that
H
does not admit a
\kappa
-coloring.
Towards a contradiction, suppose that
X=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}X_{\alpha}
where
X_{\alpha}
is
H
-independent for each
\alpha<\kappa
.
Construct a strictly
\subseteq
-increasing chain

\langle x_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
in
X
,
a strictly

\leq
-increasing chain

\langle q_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
in
\mathbb{L}

and a strictly

\leq
-decreasing chain

\langle r_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
in
\mathbb{L}

such that for all
\alpha<\kappa
, we have

q_{\alpha}<r_{\alpha}
and
p<q_{\alpha}
for all
p\in x_{\alpha}
.
Whenever possible,
choose
x_{\alpha}
,
q_{\alpha}
,
r_{\alpha}
such that

x_{\alpha}\in X_{\alpha}
.
Finally, let
x:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}x_{\alpha}
and suppose that
x\in X_{\beta}
.
Then
x_{\beta}
must also be in
X_{\beta}
by the construction.
Since
(x_{\beta},x)\in H
,
X_{\beta}
is not
H
-independent.

Given the example for
d=2
,
the
d>2
case follows from the next claim.

Claim
.

Suppose
d>2
and

G
is a graph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
Let
H_{G}
be the
d
-hypergraph consisting of those sequences
\overline{x}
such that
\operatorname{ran}(\overline{x})=\{x,y\}
for some
(x,y)\in G
.

(1)

(i)

If
G
is closed on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, then
H_{G}
is product-closed on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

(ii)

If
G\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
, then
H_{G}\in\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
.

(2)

A function
c:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to\kappa
is a
\kappa
-coloring of
H_{G}

if and only if
it is a
\kappa
-coloring of
G
.

(3)

(i)

H_{G}
does not have a complete subhypergraph of size
3
.

(ii)

If there is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H_{G}
,
then there is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
to
G
.

Proof.

(1)

is clear.

(2)
holds since a subset of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is
H_{G}
-independent if and only if it is
G
-independent.

In
(3)
,

(3)(i)
is immediate.
For

(3)(ii)
, suppose that
f
is a continuous homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H_{G}
.
We construct a continuous
\perp
- and strict order preserving map

\iota:{}^{<\kappa}2\to{}^{<\kappa}d
such that

f(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)})\times f(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)})\subseteq G

holds for all
t\in{}^{<\kappa}d
.
We construct
\iota(t)
by recursion on
\operatorname{lh}(t)
.
Let
\iota(\emptyset):=\emptyset
.
If
\operatorname{lh}(t)\in{\mathrm{Lim}}
,
and
\iota(s)
has been defined for all
s\subsetneq t
, then
let
\iota(t):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{s\subsetneq t}\iota(s)
.
We now assume
\iota(t)
has been defined
and construct

\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)

and
\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)
.
For each
i<d
, take
x_{i}
extending
\iota(t){}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
.
Since

\overline{x}:=\langle x_{i}:i<d\rangle
is a hyperedge of
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}

and

f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
\mathbb{K}^{d}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}
,
there exist
j<k<d

with
f(x_{j})\neq f(x_{k})
.
Using the continuity of
f
,
take
\alpha<\kappa

such that

\alpha>\operatorname{lh}(\iota(t))
and

f(N_{x_{j}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})\cap f(N_{x_{k}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})=\emptyset
.
Since
f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H_{G}

and
x_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha
extends
\iota(t){}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
for each
i<d
,
we have

\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\prod}{\prod}{\prod}}_{i<d}f(N_{x_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})\subseteq H_{G}
.
Since

f(N_{x_{j}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})
and
f(N_{x_{k}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})
are disjoint,
we must have

f(N_{x_{j}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})\times f(N_{x_{k}{\upharpoonright}\alpha})\subseteq G.

Let

\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)=x_{j}{\upharpoonright}\alpha

and

\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)=x_{k}{\upharpoonright}\alpha
.

Now, suppose that
\iota
has been constructed and let

g:=f\circ[\iota]
.
Then
g
is continuous.
To see that
g
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
to
G
,
suppose
(x,y)\in\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
and let
t\in{}^{\kappa}2

be the node where
x
and
y
split.
Then

(g(x),g(y))\in g(N_{t{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle})\times g(N_{t{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle})=f(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)})\times f(N_{\iota(t{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)})\subseteq G
,
as required.
∎

Remark 5.3.4
.

There is a simpler way to get a dihypergraph with the same properties as in the previous claim instead of a hypergraph.
Given a graph
G
on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
d>2
, let

I_{G}
denote the
d
-dihypergraph that consists of all
\langle x\rangle{}^{\frown}\langle y:1\leq i<d\rangle
with
(x,y)\in G
.
It is again clear that

(1)
,

(2)

and
(3)
(3)(i)

hold for
I_{G}
.

(3)
(3)(ii)

holds as well, since
for any homomorphism
f
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
I_{G}
,
f{\upharpoonright}{}^{\kappa}2
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{2}}
to
G
.

Remark 5.3.5
.

Note that the closed set
X
in the above example is not homeomorphic to
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
In fact, one can show
as in
[
MR3430247
, Section 2]

that
X
is not a continuous image of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.
To see this, one only has to replace the relation
R_{x}
coded by a subset
x
of
\kappa
by the relation
{<_{\mathbb{L}}}{\upharpoonright}x
throughout the proof of
[
MR3430247
, Lemma 2.4]
.

5.4.
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}

In this subsection, we define the combinatorial principle
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}

that is used for some of our results, for instance Theorem
,
and discuss its relationship with some other versions of
\Diamond_{\kappa}
.
It is a weak version of
\Diamond_{\kappa}
that holds at all inaccessible cardinals.

Definition 5.4.1
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal and let
\gamma\geq 2
.
A
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{cof})
-sequence
(respectively
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence
) is a sequence

\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

of sets
A_{\alpha}\subseteq{}^{\alpha}\gamma
such that

(a)

|A_{\alpha}|<\kappa
for all
\alpha<\kappa
, and

(b)

for all
y\in{}^{\kappa}\gamma
, the set

\{\alpha<\kappa:\>y{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in A_{\alpha}\}

is cofinal (stationary) in
\kappa
.

Let

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{cof})
(respectively,
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{stat})
)
denote the statement that there exists a

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{cof})
-sequence (respectively,
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{cof})
-sequence).

Let
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
denote the statement
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\kappa}(\mathrm{cof})
.
Similarly,
a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
-sequence is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\kappa}(\mathrm{cof})
-sequence.

We shall see in the next lemma
that all these principles are equivalent as long as

\kappa
is an uncountable regular cardinal
and

2\leq\gamma\leq\kappa
.
Note that
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
is exactly the
principle
(\mathrm{D}\ell)_{\kappa}
introduced in
[
ShelahModelsIV
, Definition 2.10]
.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

Suppose that
\kappa
is an uncountable regular cardinal.
Then
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\beta}(\varphi)
and
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\psi)
are equivalent
for all
\beta,\gamma
with

2\leq\beta,\gamma\leq\kappa

and all
\varphi,\psi\in\{\mathrm{cof},\mathrm{stat}\}
.

Proof.

It is clear that

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{stat})\Longrightarrow\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{cof})

holds for all ordinals
\gamma\geq 2
.

Claim
.

If
2\leq\beta<\gamma\leq\kappa
, then

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{stat})\Longrightarrow\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\beta}(\mathrm{stat})
.

Proof.

Suppose that

\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\gamma}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
Then
\langle A_{\alpha}\cap{}^{\alpha}\beta:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\beta}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
∎

Claim
.

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})\Longrightarrow\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\kappa}(\mathrm{stat})
.

Proof.

This is similar to
the argument
sketched in

[
KunenBook2013
, Exercise III.7.9]
.
Suppose
\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
Let
f
be any bijection between
\kappa\times\kappa
and
\kappa
.
For all

\alpha<\kappa
, let

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}:=\big\{s^{-1}(\{1\}):s\in A_{\alpha}\big\}
,
139
139

139

Thus,
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
is a subset of
\mathcal{P}(\alpha)
, and

A_{\alpha}
consists of the characteristic functions of elements of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
.

and let

\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}=\left\{f^{-1}{\left(S\right)}:S\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\right\}.

Using the fact that
C=\{\alpha<\kappa:f(\alpha\times\alpha)=\alpha\}
is a club subset of
\kappa
,
it is easy to see that
for all
Y\subseteq\kappa\times\kappa
,
the set of ordinals
\alpha<\kappa
with

Y\cap(\alpha\times\alpha)\in\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}
is stationary in
\kappa
.
For all
\alpha<\kappa
,
let
B_{\alpha}:=\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}\cap{}^{\alpha}\alpha
.
Then

\langle B_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
Indeed, if
x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, then
for a club set of ordinals
\alpha<\kappa

we have

\operatorname{ran}(x{\upharpoonright}\alpha)\subseteq\alpha
,
or equivalently,

x{\upharpoonright}\alpha=x\cap(\alpha\times\alpha)\in{}^{\alpha}\alpha
.
140
140

140

This step uses that
\kappa
is regular.

Thus, the set

\{\alpha<\kappa:x{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in B_{\alpha}\}
is a stationary subset of
\kappa
.
∎

It now suffices show that

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{cof})\Longrightarrow\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
.
First, note that

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})

always holds
if
\kappa
is inaccessible, since

\langle{}^{\alpha}2:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
In the case that
\kappa
is not inaccessible, the implication

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{cof})\Longrightarrow\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})

follows from the next claim.
It is a
variant
of the Proposition in
[
MatetCorrigendumDiamond
]
whose proof works verbatim in this setting.

Claim
.

Suppose that
\lambda<\kappa
is an infinite cardinal such that
2^{\lambda}\geq\kappa
.
Suppose that there exists a sequence

\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

of sets
A_{\alpha}\subseteq{}^{\alpha}2
such that

(a)

|A_{\alpha}|<\kappa
for all
\alpha<\kappa
, and

(b)

for all
y\in{}^{\kappa}2
, there exists
\alpha\geq\lambda

such that
y{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in A_{\alpha}
.

Then
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
holds.

Lemma
5.4.2
follows immediately from the previous claims.
∎

Lemma 5.4.3
.

(1)

If
\Diamond_{\kappa}
holds or
\kappa
is inaccessible, then
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
holds.

(2)

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
implies that
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
.

Proof.

If

\langle a_{\alpha}\in{}^{\alpha}2:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is a
\Diamond_{\kappa}
-sequence, then

\big\langle\{a_{\alpha}\}:\alpha<\kappa\big\rangle

is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
If
\kappa
is inaccessible, then

\langle{}^{\alpha}2:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence.
Under both of these assumptions,
\kappa
is an uncountable regular cardinal
and therefore

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
implies

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}=\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\kappa}(\mathrm{cof})

by Lemma
5.4.2
.

To prove
(2)
, suppose that

\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
-sequence.
It is sufficient to show that

{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\subseteq\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\alpha<\kappa}T(A_{\alpha})

since the latter set
has size
\kappa
.
Suppose
s\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.
Let
x
be an arbitrary element of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
extending
s
,
and let

\alpha\geq\operatorname{lh}(s)
be such that
x{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in A_{\alpha}
.
Then
s\subseteq x{\upharpoonright}\alpha
and hence

s\in{}T(A_{\alpha})
.
∎

Remark 5.4.4
.

Suppose that either
\kappa=\omega
or that
\kappa
is a singular strong limit cardinal.
Then

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
holds
since
\langle{}^{\alpha}2:\alpha<\kappa\rangle

is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,2}(\mathrm{stat})
-sequence,
but
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}=\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa,\kappa}(\mathrm{cof})
is false.
To show the latter statement, first note that
that
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
implies
\kappa
is regular by
Claim
5.4.3

(2)
.
Seeking a contradiction, suppose that
\Diamond^{i}_{\omega}
holds and let

\langle A_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
be a
\Diamond^{i}_{\omega}
-sequence.
Define
x\in{}^{\omega}\omega

by letting

x(n):=\sup\{\operatorname{ran}(a):a\in A_{n+1}\}+1

for each
n<\omega
.
Take
m<\omega
with

x{\upharpoonright}m\in A_{m}
.
Then

x(m-1)>\sup\{\operatorname{ran}(a):a\in A_{m}\}\geq\sup\left(\operatorname{ran}(x{\upharpoonright}m)\right)\geq x(m-1),

a contradiction.

Remark 5.4.5
.

(1)

For
\kappa=\lambda^{+}\geq\aleph_{1}
,

Definition
5.4.1

(a)

is equivalent to

|A_{\alpha}|\leq\lambda
for all
\alpha<\kappa
.
Therefore
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
is equivalent to
\Diamond_{\kappa}

by a result of Kunen’s

[
JensenKunenDiamond
]
.
141
141

141

See also
[
KunenBook2013
]
*Theorem III.7.8 and Exercise III.7.16.

(2)

For
\kappa=\lambda^{+}\geq\aleph_{2}
,

\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
is equivalent to
\Diamond_{\kappa}

[
ShelahDiamonds
]

and therefore also to
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
.

(3)

For inaccessible cardinals
\kappa
,

\Diamond_{\kappa}
and
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
are not in general equivalent,
since the failure of
\Diamond_{\kappa}
at the least inaccessible cardinal
\kappa
is consistent
[
GolshaniDiamondInaccessible
]
.

To the best knowledge of the authors, it is not known whether
any of the assumptions

\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
,

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}

and
\Diamond_{\kappa}
are equivalent
when
\kappa
is weakly inaccessible but not inaccessible.

The next lemma describes a

\delta
-dimensional equivalent version
of
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
for ordinals
2\leq\delta<\kappa
.
Recall that

{\Delta}^{\delta}_{X}
denotes the set of constant sequences
\langle c\rangle^{d}\in{}^{d}X

and

\mathbb{K}^{\delta}_{X}={}^{\delta}X-{\Delta}^{\delta}_{X}
.

Lemma 5.4.6
.

Suppose
\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal and
2\leq\delta<\kappa
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
.

(2)

There exists a sequence

\langle B_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
of sets

B_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{K}^{\delta}_{{}^{\alpha}\kappa}
such that

(a)

|B_{\alpha}|<\kappa
for all
\alpha<\kappa
, and

(b)

for all
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{K}^{\delta}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}
,
there exists
\alpha<\kappa

with

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\alpha}\in B_{\alpha}
.
142
142

142

Recall that

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\alpha}:=\langle x_{i}{\upharpoonright}\alpha:i<\delta\rangle

for any sequence

\overline{x}=\langle x_{i}:i\in\delta\rangle\in{}^{\delta}{({}^{\kappa}\kappa)}
and
\alpha<\kappa
.

Proof.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(1)
:
Suppose that
\langle B_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a sequence as in
(2)
.
For all
\alpha<\kappa
, let

A_{\alpha}:=\{s_{0}:\langle s_{\beta}:\beta<\delta\rangle\in B_{\alpha}\}.

We claim that
\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
-sequence.
To see this, suppose
x\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
\gamma<\kappa
.
It suffices to find
\alpha<\kappa
such that
\alpha>\gamma

and
x{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in A_{\alpha}
.
Take
\overline{x}=\langle x_{\beta}:\beta<\delta\rangle
in

\mathbb{K}^{\delta}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}

with
x_{0}=x
and

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\gamma}\in{\Delta}^{\delta}_{{}^{\gamma}\kappa}
.
By
(2)
(2)(b)
, there exists
\alpha<\kappa
such that

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\alpha}\in B_{\alpha}
.
Then
x{\upharpoonright}\alpha=x_{0}{\upharpoonright}\alpha\in A_{\alpha}
,
and
\alpha>\gamma
since

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\gamma}\in{\Delta}^{\delta}_{{}^{\gamma}\kappa}

but

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\alpha}\in B_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{K}^{\delta}_{{}^{\alpha}\kappa}
.

(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
:
Let
\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
be a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
-sequence.
For every

\gamma\leq\kappa
, let

g_{\gamma}:{}^{\delta\cdot\gamma}\kappa\to{}^{\delta}({}^{\gamma}\kappa)

be the bijection
which maps each

s\in{}^{\delta\cdot\gamma}\kappa

to the unique element

g_{\gamma}(s)=\langle t_{\beta}:\beta<\delta\rangle
of

{}^{\delta}({}^{\gamma}\kappa)

with

t_{\beta}(\alpha)=s(\delta\cdot\alpha+\beta)

for all
\alpha<\gamma
and
\beta<\delta
.
For each
\gamma<\kappa
, let

B_{\gamma}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\textstyle\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}{\bigcup}}_{\beta<\delta}\left\{g_{\gamma}\big(s{\upharpoonright}(\delta\cdot\gamma)\big):s\in A_{\delta\cdot\gamma+\beta}\right\}.

We claim that
\langle B_{\gamma}:\gamma<\kappa\rangle
satisfies
(2)
.
Since
\kappa
is regular,

|B_{\gamma}|<\kappa
for all
\gamma<\kappa
.
To see
(2)
(2)(b)
,
take
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{K}^{\delta}_{{}^{\kappa}\kappa}
and let
z:=g_{\kappa}^{-1}(\overline{x})
.
Since
z\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and
\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
is a
\Diamond^{i}_{\kappa}
-sequence,
there exist ordinals

\gamma<\kappa

and
\beta<\delta
such that

z{\upharpoonright}(\delta\cdot\gamma+\beta)\in A_{\delta\cdot\gamma+\beta}
.
Then

\overline{x}_{\upharpoonright\gamma}=g_{\kappa}(z)_{\upharpoonright\gamma}=g_{\gamma}\big(z{\upharpoonright}(\delta\cdot\gamma)\big)\in B_{\gamma}.

∎

5.5.
Strong variants

We consider the following extensions of
\mathsf{ODD}^{H}_{\kappa}
. These are ordered by increasing strength.

Definition 5.5.1
.

Suppose that
2\leq d\leq\kappa
and

H
is a
d
-dihypergraph on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

\mathsf{ODDI}^{H}_{\kappa}
:
Either
H
admits a
\kappa
-coloring, or
there exists
an
continuous homomorphism
from
\mathbb{H}_{{}^{\kappa}\!{d}}
to
H
.
